#### 2.7.2.1 Securing AI

The rapid expansion of AI into new industries with new stakeholders, coupled with an evolving threat landscape, presents a tough challenge for security. AI impacts our lives every day, from local AI systems on our mobile phones suggesting the next word in our sentences to large manufacturers using AI to improve industrial processes. AI has the potential to revolutionize our interactions with technologies, improve our quality of life, and enrich security – but without high‐quality technical standards, AI has the potential to create new attacks and worsen security.

The ETSI Industry Specification Group on Securing Artificial Intelligence (ISG SAI) focuses on three key areas: using AI to enhance security, mitigating against attacks that leverage AI, and securing AI itself from attack. The ETSI ISG SAI works alongside a landscape of huge growth in AI, creating standards to preserve and improve the security of AI.

ETSI GR SAI 004 v1.1.1 was published in December 2020. It describes the problem of securing AI‐based systems and solutions, with a focus on ML, and the challenges relating to confidentiality, integrity and availability at each stage of the ML lifecycle. It also describes some of the broader challenges of AI systems including bias, ethics, and explainability. A number of different attack vectors are described, as well as several real‐world use cases and attacks.

ETSI GR SAI 005 v1.1.1 was published in March 2021. It summarizes and analyzes existing and potential mitigation against threats for AI‐based systems as discussed in ETSI GR SAI 004 v1.1.1. The goal was to have a technical survey for mitigating against threats introduced by adopting AI into systems. The technical survey shed light on available methods of securing AI‐based systems by mitigating against known or potential security threats. It also addresses security capabilities, challenges, and limitations when adopting mitigation for AI‐based systems in certain potential use cases.

### 2.8.1 Poseidon

In order to improve the accuracy of identifying what a node is (based on captured IP header data) and housing ML techniques for additional use cases in SDN environment, Poseidon

Poseidon is a platform that can be easily expanded based on python implementation. It can provide context‐aware capabilities for items and traffic added or deleted from the network. It uses ML technologies to check interactive activities on the network, and through learning to track and block various malicious activities‐related behaviors. In the preliminary test, Poseidon obtained a malicious activity detection rate of 84%, while the false positive rate was only 2.2%.

### 2.8.2 NetworkML

In order to classify each device into a functional role, IQTLabs implements NetworkML.

As shown in [Figure 2.6](#c02.xhtml#c02-fig-0006), taking the network traffic for a single device as input, NetworkML uses a feedforward neural network from the scikit‐learn‐package to predict the functional role of network‐connected devices. By using network traffic of a single device, it means all packets sent and received by that device over a given time period. In addition, in its default configuration, NetworkML has 11 roles: active directory controller, administrator server, administrator workstation, confluence server, developer workstation, distributed file share, exchange server, graphics processing unit (GPU) laptop, github server, public key infrastructure (PKI) server, and printer.

The NetworkML in this project can be used via Docker and in a standalone manner on a Linux host. In addition, in order to avoid problems related to data privacy, NetworkNL only uses the packet header to predict the functional role.

 

### 2.8.3 Credential‐Digger

In the field of data protection, one of the most critical threats is represented by hardcoded (or plaintext) credentials in open‐source projects. Although several tools are already available to detect leaks in open‐source platforms, but the diversity of credentials (depending on multiple factors such as the programming language, code development conventions, or developers' personal habits) is a bottleneck for the effectiveness of these tools. Their lack of precision leads to a very high number of pieces of code incorrectly detected as leaked secrets. Data wrongly detected as a leak is called false positive data and composes the huge majority of the data detected by currently available tools. As a result, SAP implements Credential‐Digger

As shown in [Figure 2.7](#c02.xhtml#c02-fig-0007), Credential‐Digger takes the path of code as input and determine whether there is a certificate or password leak in the code. It first uses rules, which comprises a regex, to find all possible leaks, then ML models will be used for false positive detection. It has three types of models, i.e. path model, snippet model, and similarity model.

*   Path models. The path model classifies a file as false positive according to its file path. The path models first process the path of file in order to extract all the worlds contained in the path. Then, each word is converted into its root. Finally, the roots of the words are used for the ML classification.
*   Snippet model. The snippet model classifies a discovery as false positive according to its code snippet, i.e. the line of code detected as a possible leak in the commit. The goal of the snippet model is to classify false positive passwords (it can be used also with tokens, but its precision is lower).
*   Similarity model. No matter how robust a classifier is, instances of misclassified snippets will always remain a reality. As a result, after files are processed by snippet models, the reviewer will verify that the code snippets are correctly classified. Usually, those misclassified snippets share similarities between each other. Upon manually updating a snippet to a new state, e.g. from “leak” to “false\_positive,” Similarity models are able to automatically detect similar snippets in the repository and update their state accordingly, which can save the user a lot of time.

In addition to local files and directories, Credential‐Digger also supports the scanning of online and local repositories. In addition, this project is very easy to expand, where we can add rules and models according to our needs.

![Figure 2.7 The processing flow of Credential‐Digger.](images/c02f007.png)

[**Figure 2.7**](#c02.xhtml#R_c02-fig-0007) The processing flow of Credential‐Digger.

With the continuous development of ML technologies, more researchers have begun to use them to solve network security and network management problems. At the same time, they often open source their experimental code as Proof of Concepts.

#### 2.9.1.1 Phishing URL Classification

Malicious Web sites are a cornerstone of Internet criminal activities. These Web sites contain various unwanted content such as spam‐advertised products, phishing sites, dangerous “drive‐by” harness that infect a visitor's system with malware. The most influential approaches to the malicious URL problem are manually constructed lists in which all malicious web page's URLs are listed, as well as users systems that analyze the content or behavior of a Web site as it is visited. The disadvantage of Blacklisting approaches is that we have to do the tedious task of searching the list for presence of the entry. The list can be very large considering the amount of web sites on the Internet. Also the list cannot be kept up to date because of the ever‐growing web link over time.

In the given System,

With the continuous updating of network attack technologies, the number of rules in traditional rule‐based intrusion detection systems is becoming more judgmental. In addition, in terms of zero‐day attack detection, rule‐based intrusion detection systems have natural disadvantages. Therefore, in recent years, more researchers have tried to use ML‐based intrusion detection systems for security protection.

### 2.9.2 Active Learning

In the process of supervised and semisupervised learning, the training data needs to be labeled. However, in many practical fields, labeling all data will incur a great cost and cause a waste of manpower. For example, in the field of anomaly detection, there is often only a small part of the anomaly data that security experts are interested in. Anaël et al. proposed “ILAB: An Interactive Labelling Strategy for Intrusion Detection” in Beaugnon et al. ([2017](#c02.xhtml#c02-bib-0001)) and provided an open‐source implementation ![Figure 2.9 The processing flow of active learning.](images/c02f009.png)

[**Figure 2.9**](#c02.xhtml#R_c02-fig-0009) The processing flow of active learning.

The workflow of active learning is shown in [Figure 2.9](#c02.xhtml#c02-fig-0009). First, a detection model is trained by initial labeled data. After that, the system starts to work, and the unlabeled data will be placed in the unlabeled pool. Then, active learning is performed to select representative samples from the unlabeled pool and provides them to experts for labeling. Finally, after the expert finishes labeling the selected data, the marked data will be used for training the detection model.

In order to overcome the problem of sampling bias, ILAB considers both uncertainty and rarity when it selects data. Therefore, ILAB can find those rare malicious samples. As a result, when compared with other schemes, ILAB can select samples with more training value.

 

### 2.9.3 Concept Drift Detection

With the rapid development of network and information technology, traffic classification has received significant attention and plays an important role in intrusion detection, flow identification, etc. In the standard ML framework, data are usually processed in an offline way. Since network traffic, logs, and data packets are usually generated continuously, data in the network domain is usually regarded as a stream. Hence, the underlying data distribution may be changed and severely reduce the performance of ML models, which is called concept drift.

In order to overcome the occurrence of concept drift and maintain the performance of ML models, many conceptual drift detectors have been proposed. As shown in [Figure 2.10](#c02.xhtml#c02-fig-0010), whenever a concept drift is detected, the model update phase will be triggered. In the prototype system called “concept‐drift,” ![Figure 2.10 The processing flow to overcome concept drift.](images/c02f010.png)

[**Figure 2.10**](#c02.xhtml#R_c02-fig-0010) The processing flow to overcome concept drift.

 

## 2.10 Conclusion

This chapter introduced the current status and situation of network and security management in both academia and industry. We provided the basic classification of AI and ML, and briefly introduced how different types of ML techniques can empower network and security management. Finally, we summarized the related products, standards, and proof‐of‐concepts for AI and ML in network and security management.

# 3  
Learning Network Intents for Autonomous Network Management\*

 

## 3.1 Introduction

Nowadays, the network carries a large number of applications related to important fields, such as government services, online payment, live broadcast, virtual reality, augmented reality. Due to the extremely demanding performance, network and service providers propose intents to the network, hoping that the network can meet their requirements by deploying network policies (Ujcich et al., [2020](#c03.xhtml#c03-bib-0008), Szyrkowiec et al., [2018](#c03.xhtml#c03-bib-0007)). From the perspective of the network, it prioritizes the intent of applications according to different service levels and does its best to realize the intent of low‐service‐level applications. From the perspective of applications, it hopes that its network intent can be timely and accurately realized. In order to reconcile the contradiction between the network and the application, the Internet Service Provider and the Cloud Service Vendor raise equipment investment, hire more service maintenance operators, or design complex scheduling algorithms. However, this leads to more complex network architectures and unpredictable behaviors of network applications.

Network applications can be a simple path selection task (Hassani et al., [2020](#c03.xhtml#c03-bib-0002)) or a complex computational offloading task (Xia et al., [2014](#c03.xhtml#c03-bib-0009)). The network environment may be a static local area network or a dynamic edge computing network. When a simple network task runs on a static network environment, the behavior of tasks fits the intentions and can be predictable in general. When a complex network task is running in a dynamic and time‐varying network environment, the behavior of the network task is often unpredictable, so that we need to do extra work to determine whether the behavior of the network task is consistent with the operator's intention. For example, when a mobile device offloads tasks to the edge network, it is expected that these tasks can achieve the lowest latency and the highest completion rate. However, the network environment often changes due to the movement of the device so that the network cannot continue providing enough resources. Inevitably, it has to sacrifice the completion rate of the task to ensure the low latency of the task. This results in the status of the task being inconsistent with the intention of the operator. The contradiction becomes more prominent when the task is more complex and the network environment changes over time, e.g. in the 5G network (Saha et al., [2016](#c03.xhtml#c03-bib-0005)).

In order to deal with a series of new challenges, for example, the applications' intent may not be accurately realized and the behavior of the network task is unpredictable, and it is necessary to propose an automated solution that can automatically infer the intentions of network behavior. It is convenient for judging whether the intention is consistent with that of the operator's. The behavioral intents of network applications are abstract, which are independent of the specific network environment, such as finding a shortest path. It is transferable across different network environments.

The first step of the automated solution is to infer the intent of the network behavior from the network measurement data. For example, for the task offloading of the Internet of Vehicles (Sonmez et al., [2021](#c03.xhtml#c03-bib-0006)), there are multiple offloading strategies to achieve different optimization goals for different types of applications. It is not easy to infer its intentions. Even a simple network application, such as finding a connected and loop‐free path between two points, it implies two intentions. One is connectivity, and the other is acyclic. It is very common that a network behavior includes multiple intentions. In order to fully reveal the intentions of a network behavior, the representation method of the intentions should be a structured model composed of multiple atomic intentions.

The issue can be greatly inspired by symbolism. The meaningful symbol refers to an abstract concept with a certain practical significance. Some of the concepts can be regarded as intentions and are widely used in different tasks and environments to form new concepts. For example, for the concept of “connectivity” and “shortest,” they can be combined into the concept of “finding the shortest path.” If we can learn the basic symbols that express intents from measurement data, through the combination of these symbols, we can get a structured expression that represents the intentions of a meaningful network behavior.

It is important to figure out what a symbol is and how it can express a concept. British scholars Charles Kay Ogden and Ivor Armstrong Richards proposed the semantic triangle in the important semantics work “The Meaning of Meaning” published in 1923 (Odgen and Richards, [1923](#c03.xhtml#c03-bib-0004)). This theory refers to the mutual restriction and interaction between symbols, concepts, and objective things. As shown in [Figure 3.1](#c03.xhtml#c03-fig-0001), the semantic triangle contains the following meanings: (i) There is a solid line connection between concept/reference and thing/referent. Concepts are generalized on the basis of objective things, which mean a concept refers to a thing. (ii) There is also a solid line connection between concept/reference and symbols. Concepts are expressed through symbols, which mean a symbol symbolizes a concept. (iii) There is a dashed connection between a symbol and a thing/referent. A symbol stands for a thing/referent, and there is no inherent necessary connection between the two. For example, we can use the symbol ![upper L](images/c03-i0001.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>L</mi></mrow></math> or other symbols to represent the link. The concept of link and its role will emerge in the mind of the network and service operators, which is a concept/reference in the semantic triangle. The thing/referent in the semantic triangle is a link of the specific network in reality.

![Figure 3.1 The semantic triangle.](images/c03f001.png)

[**Figure 3.1**](#c03.xhtml#R_c03-fig-0001) The semantic triangle.

![Figure 3.2 The triangle theory of intention symbol semantics.](images/c03f002.png)

[**Figure 3.2**](#c03.xhtml#R_c03-fig-0002) The triangle theory of intention symbol semantics.

With the help of the semantic triangle theory, we propose a triangle theory of intention symbol semantics. As shown in [Figure 3.2](#c03.xhtml#c03-fig-0002), the intention symbol is to indicate a random vector. Every element of a random vector is a random variable, which represents a certain concept such as bandwidth. Sampling from the probability distribution of each random variable, we can get the concrete examples of abstract bandwidth concepts represented by random vectors, such as a bandwidth threshold.

As shown in the left part of [Figure 3.3](#c03.xhtml#c03-fig-0003), the symbol ![upper A](images/c03-i0002.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>A</mi></mrow></math> is used to indicate the concept of connectivity, which is described by a random vector containing three random variables: ![mu](images/c03-i0003.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>&#956;</mi></mrow></math>, ![upsilon](images/c03-i0004.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>&#965;</mi></mrow></math>, and ![eta](images/c03-i0005.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>&#951;</mi></mrow></math>. The first variable represents the last selected link, the second variable denotes the next link to be selected, and the third variable means the relationship between the two variables ![mu](images/c03-i0006.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>&#956;</mi></mrow></math> and ![upsilon](images/c03-i0007.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>&#965;</mi></mrow></math>. As shown in the right part of [Figure 3.3](#c03.xhtml#c03-fig-0003), ![normal upper Phi 0](images/c03-i0008.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">&#934;</mi><mn>0</mn></msub></math> and ![normal upper Phi 1](images/c03-i0009.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">&#934;</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></math> represent two nodes of a link. Intuitively, we know that in order to ensure that the next link to be selected is connected to the current path, ![normal upper Phi 1](images/c03-i0010.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">&#934;</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></math> of the last selected link and ![normal upper Phi 0](images/c03-i0011.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">&#934;</mi><mn>0</mn></msub></math> of the next link must be equal, which means that they are connected to the same switch. Therefore, on the left side of [Figure 3.3](#c03.xhtml#c03-fig-0003), the three random variables of the concept connectivity are most likely to be sampled to “![normal upper Phi 1](images/c03-i0012.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">&#934;</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></math>,” “![normal upper Phi 0](images/c03-i0013.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">&#934;</mi><mn>0</mn></msub></math>,” and “![equals](images/c03-i0014.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mo>=</mo></mrow></math>,” respectively, according to their probability distributions. The above is about using the triangle theory of intention symbol semantics to describe a concept of connectivity intention from the perspective of random vectors.

![Figure 3.3 Examples of intention symbol semantics.](images/c03f003.png)

[**Figure 3.3**](#c03.xhtml#R_c03-fig-0003) Examples of intention symbol semantics.

The intention symbol semantics theory defines the relationship between symbols, concepts, and instantiated things. In the rest of this chapter, we will focus on inferring intention symbols from the perspective of statistical learning, and what kind of structures these symbols form to completely describe the intent of a network behavior. Finally, we will introduce how the symbolic structure is transferable.

 

## 3.2 Motivation

On the way to realize automated network management, it is inefficient and infeasible to artificially equip a tool with various intentions for network management. Artificial intentions are either very abstract and do not have the ability to be directly executed, or require users to have comprehensive network knowledge to express a correct intention. This chapter uses the behavioral data of network applications to explore a way to mine intentions from data and uses symbols to express intentions. It is a direction worth exploring. These symbols can form a structure to express the complete intent. They can replace artificial management intentions. The intention symbol semantics theory guides the inference of network intentions. The inference of the network intention is transformed into the problem of fitting the probability distribution represented by the symbol. It is motivated to solve two important problems: one is the inference of network intent. It can not only allow network operators to judge whether the current network behavior meets the requirements but also learn the basic knowledge of the network, which provides a basis for automatically expressing network intents. The second is to solve the computational problem of using symbols to express intents. It expresses intents as probability distributions.

 

## 3.3 The Hierarchical Representation and Learning Framework for Intention Symbols Inference

This section will take the path selection task as an example to explain how to use the framework we designed to infer the intention of the network application through data and express it as a symbolic structure. The proposed framework is devised with two components: symbolic semantic learning (SSL) and symbolic structure inferring (SSI). The SSL is about learning symbols that represent intent. The SSI is about inferring the structural form of the intention symbol, so as to fully express the intention of a network behavior. The SSL and the SSI will be introduced in detail below.

 

### 3.3.1 Symbolic Semantic Learning (SSL)

The premise of inferring intention symbols is to judge the possibility of symbol existence according to prior knowledge, that is, the place where intention will appear. Taking path selection as an example, according to the prior knowledge of network management, we can intuitively judge that the task will have intention in three places. We use symbols ![upper A](images/c03-i0015.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>A</mi></mrow></math>, ![upper B](images/c03-i0016.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>B</mi></mrow></math>, and ![upper C](images/c03-i0017.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>C</mi></mrow></math> as marks, respectively. The path must satisfy the intention of connectivity, so as shown in [Figure 3.4](#c03.xhtml#c03-fig-0004), the connectivity intention represented by symbol ![upper A](images/c03-i0018.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>A</mi></mrow></math> will appear in the dark gray box. It means that only when two links are connected to the same switch can they satisfy the connectivity. In addition, the path also needs to satisfy the deadlock free intention, so as shown in [Figure 3.5](#c03.xhtml#c03-fig-0005), the symbol ![upper B](images/c03-i0019.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>B</mi></mrow></math> representing the deadlock free intention appears in the dark gray box. It means a path needs to avoid loops. Finally, there is an intention about the performance. For example, the path is required to be the shortest or guaranteed by deterministic delay. As shown in [Figure 3.6](#c03.xhtml#c03-fig-0006), the symbol ![upper C](images/c03-i0020.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>C</mi></mrow></math> representing the performance intention on the path appears in the gray part.

![Figure 3.4 Connectivity intention symbol.](images/c03f004.png)

[**Figure 3.4**](#c03.xhtml#R_c03-fig-0004) Connectivity intention symbol.

![Figure 3.5 Deadlock free intention symbol.](images/c03f005.png)

[**Figure 3.5**](#c03.xhtml#R_c03-fig-0005) Deadlock free intention symbol.

![Figure 3.6 Whole path intention symbol.](images/c03f006.png)

[**Figure 3.6**](#c03.xhtml#R_c03-fig-0006) Whole path intention symbol.

The above is the first step of symbols inference, which uses prior knowledge to judge the possible intention of a network task. This is a kind of preliminary judgment of possible intention symbols by means of operators' prior knowledge. It can also be determined by artificial intelligence (AI), which is an open question.

 

#### 3.3.1.1 Connectivity Intention

The triangle theory of intention symbol semantics mentioned in [Section 3.1](#c03.xhtml#c03-sec-0001) puts forward the view that each intention symbol needs to be given a random vector to express its meaning. Each element of a random vector is a random variable. The symbol ![upper A](images/c03-i0021.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>A</mi></mrow></math> expressing connectivity occurs between two links, so it was endowed with a random vector of length three. The first and the second random variables represent two links, respectively, and the third random variable expresses the relationship between the two links. We mark them as ![mu Subscript a](images/c03-i0022.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>&#956;</mi><mi>a</mi></msub></math>, ![upsilon Subscript a](images/c03-i0023.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>&#965;</mi><mi>a</mi></msub></math>, ![eta Subscript a](images/c03-i0024.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>&#951;</mi><mi>a</mi></msub></math>, respectively.

In most cases, intent is a description of the state of one or more attributes. For example, the shortest path is the description of the path length attribute; deterministic delay guarantee is the description of delay; deadlock free path is the description of the nodes that the path passes through. Therefore, it is a natural and sensible idea that the sampling range of random variables with intention symbols is the attribute. The network attributes involved in path selection include node index. A link is described by node indexes at both ends, marked as ![phi 0](images/c03-i0025.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>&#966;</mi><mn>0</mn></msub></math> and ![phi 1](images/c03-i0026.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>&#966;</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></math>, respectively. The power set of these two signs can be regarded as the sampling range of random variables ![mu Subscript a](images/c03-i0027.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>&#956;</mi><mi>a</mi></msub></math> and ![upsilon Subscript a](images/c03-i0028.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>&#965;</mi><mi>a</mi></msub></math>. In other words, the possible values of ![mu Subscript a](images/c03-i0029.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>&#956;</mi><mi>a</mi></msub></math> and ![upsilon Subscript a](images/c03-i0030.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>&#965;</mi><mi>a</mi></msub></math> are the set of signs as the set elements: ![left-parenthesis phi 0 right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0031.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mrow><msub><mi>&#966;</mi><mn>0</mn></msub></mrow></mfenced></math>, ![left-parenthesis phi 1 right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0032.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mrow><msub><mi>&#966;</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></mrow></mfenced></math>, ![left-parenthesis phi 0 comma phi 1 right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0033.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mrow><msub><mi>&#966;</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>&#966;</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></mrow></mfenced></math> and ![empty-set](images/c03-i0034.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>&#8709;</mi></mrow></math>. In addition, ![eta Subscript a](images/c03-i0035.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>&#951;</mi><mi>a</mi></msub></math> describes the relationship between ![mu Subscript a](images/c03-i0036.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>&#956;</mi><mi>a</mi></msub></math> and ![upsilon Subscript a](images/c03-i0037.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>&#965;</mi><mi>a</mi></msub></math>, that is, the relationship between two sets. In most instances, there are equality, inequality, true inclusion, inclusion, intersection, and nonintersection. These five relationships are used as the sampling range of random variables ![eta Subscript a](images/c03-i0038.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>&#951;</mi><mi>a</mi></msub></math>.

After completing the design of symbol ![upper A](images/c03-i0039.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>A</mi></mrow></math> in the three aspects of symbol, random vector, and network attribute as the sampling range, as discussed in the triangle theory of intention symbol semantics, the next step is to obtain the instantiation of intention by means of statistical learning. That is, the sampling of the intent random vector.

Assume that the random vector ![upper A equals left-parenthesis mu Subscript a Baseline comma upsilon Subscript a Baseline comma eta Subscript a Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0040.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>A</mi><mo>=</mo><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>&#956;</mi><mi>a</mi></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>&#965;</mi><mi>a</mi></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>&#951;</mi><mi>a</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math> follows a probability model as follows:

(3.1)![upper A tilde upper P left-parenthesis upper A right-parenthesis](images/c03-math-0001.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll"><mi>A</mi><mo>&#8764;</mo><mi>P</mi><mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mi>A</mi></mfenced></math> 

Its elements are not independent of each other. ![mu Subscript a](images/c03-i0041.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>&#956;</mi><mi>a</mi></msub></math> and ![upsilon Subscript a](images/c03-i0042.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>&#965;</mi><mi>a</mi></msub></math> depend on ![eta Subscript a](images/c03-i0043.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>&#951;</mi><mi>a</mi></msub></math>. By observing ![eta Subscript a](images/c03-i0044.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>&#951;</mi><mi>a</mi></msub></math> to take a certain relationship, we determine which combination modes of ![mu Subscript a](images/c03-i0045.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>&#956;</mi><mi>a</mi></msub></math> and ![upsilon Subscript a](images/c03-i0046.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>&#965;</mi><mi>a</mi></msub></math> have significant probabilities. The model can be further expressed as follows:

#### 3.3.1.2 Deadlock Free Intention

Similar to the previous connectivity intention, the design of deadlock free intention symbol ![upper B](images/c03-i0053.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>B</mi></mrow></math> also includes a random vector, the value range of the random vector, and the probability distribution of the random vector. As shown in [Figure 3.5](#c03.xhtml#c03-fig-0005), the deadlock free intent involves multiple nodes. When selecting a path, deadlock can be avoided as long as the current link does not intersect with the previously selected paths. Therefore, the length of random vector of symbol ![upper B](images/c03-i0054.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>B</mi></mrow></math> is four. The first and the second random variables describe the previously selected paths and the link to be selected, respectively. The third random variable denotes the relationship between the first and the second random variables. The fourth random variable means the number of occurrences of the relationship. These four random variables are recorded as ![mu Subscript b](images/c03-i0055.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>&#956;</mi><mi>b</mi></msub></math>, ![upsilon Subscript b](images/c03-i0056.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>&#965;</mi><mi>b</mi></msub></math>, ![eta Subscript b](images/c03-i0057.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>&#951;</mi><mi>b</mi></msub></math>, and ![gamma Subscript b](images/c03-i0058.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>&#947;</mi><mi>b</mi></msub></math>, respectively. Assume that the random vector ![upper B equals left-parenthesis mu Subscript b Baseline comma upsilon Subscript b Baseline comma eta Subscript b Baseline comma gamma Subscript b Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0059.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>B</mi><mo>=</mo><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>&#956;</mi><mi>b</mi></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>&#965;</mi><mi>b</mi></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>&#951;</mi><mi>b</mi></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>&#947;</mi><mi>b</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math> follows a probability model as follows:

(3.5)![upper B tilde upper P left-parenthesis upper B right-parenthesis](images/c03-math-0005.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll"><mi>B</mi><mo>&#8764;</mo><mi>P</mi><mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mi>B</mi></mfenced></math> 

Like the previous symbol ![upper A](images/c03-i0060.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>A</mi></mrow></math>, its elements are not independent of each other. ![mu Subscript b](images/c03-i0061.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>&#956;</mi><mi>b</mi></msub></math> and ![upsilon Subscript b](images/c03-i0062.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>&#965;</mi><mi>b</mi></msub></math> depend on ![eta Subscript b](images/c03-i0063.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>&#951;</mi><mi>b</mi></msub></math> and ![gamma Subscript b](images/c03-i0064.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>&#947;</mi><mi>b</mi></msub></math>. By observing ![eta Subscript b](images/c03-i0065.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>&#951;</mi><mi>b</mi></msub></math> to take a certain relationship and ![gamma Subscript b](images/c03-i0066.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>&#947;</mi><mi>b</mi></msub></math> to take the number of occurrences, we need to determine which combination modes of ![mu Subscript b](images/c03-i0067.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>&#956;</mi><mi>b</mi></msub></math> and ![upsilon Subscript b](images/c03-i0068.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>&#965;</mi><mi>b</mi></msub></math> have significant probabilities. The model can be further expressed as follows:

#### 3.3.1.3 Performance Intention

Performance intent is to describe the performance of a path in some aspect. The length of random vector of symbol ![upper C](images/c03-i0078.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>C</mi></mrow></math> is two. One describes the performance object, such as path length, path bandwidth, and the other shows the performance, such as the longest, shortest, maximum. The two random variables are recorded as ![mu Subscript c](images/c03-i0079.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>&#956;</mi><mi>c</mi></msub></math> and ![upsilon Subscript c](images/c03-i0080.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>&#965;</mi><mi>c</mi></msub></math>, respectively. #### 3.3.1.4 Discussion

In the previous part, we give three examples to discuss the design of intention symbols which express the requirements of network and service operators for some aspects of the network. In order to give semantics to symbols, it is necessary to endow random vectors for each symbol to represent some concepts and relationships. The specific semantic of intention is the sampling values or the expectation of random vectors, which are inferred from the data. These symbols obtained during the inference are to translate into the network policies and deployed to the network automatically.

 

### 3.3.2 Symbolic Structure Inferring (SSI)

Symbols ![upper A](images/c03-i0084.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>A</mi></mrow></math>, ![upper B](images/c03-i0085.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>B</mi></mrow></math>, and ![upper C](images/c03-i0086.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>C</mi></mrow></math> can be regarded as atomic intentions, expressing the ability of the network in a certain aspect. They need to be combined into a structure to express an accurate application intention. For example, the shortest path contains the intention of connectivity, the intention of shortest length, and the intention of deadlock free. Only the combination of these three intentions can express the intention that the application hopes the network to provide the shortest path. However, it is an important challenge to develop a mechanism to ensure that flow entries can satisfy all intents at the same time. As shown in [Figure 3.7](#c03.xhtml#c03-fig-0007), when looking for the path from node 1 to node 7, the “paths” with the length of two satisfy the intention of shortest, as shown in the bold links, but these “paths” do not meet the intention of connectivity. This means that the flow entry corresponding to the intention of shortest does not satisfy the intent of connectivity. The correct approach should be to find the flow table entries between node 1 and node 7 that meet the intent of connectivity, as shown in [Figure 3.8](#c03.xhtml#c03-fig-0008), and then find the flow table entries corresponding to the shortest path among them. As shown in the dark gray box in the figure. A reasonable shortest path that meets the user's intention is obtained.

![Figure 3.7 “Paths” satisfying the shortest intention.](images/c03f007.png)

[**Figure 3.7**](#c03.xhtml#R_c03-fig-0007) “Paths” satisfying the shortest intention.

These two cases show that there is a priority between atomic intentions, rather than a simple parallel relationship. The symbolic structure is based on priority between intents. Determining the priority between intention symbols is an important step. This step is called SSI.

The purpose of SSI is to determine the precedence between intention symbols. First, the path sets satisfying the high‐priority intention are obtained, and then the paths satisfying the lower‐priority intention are further filtered on the set. The precedence between intention symbols has three modes, as shown in [Figure 3.9](#c03.xhtml#c03-fig-0009). The ![upper X](images/c03-i0087.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>X</mi></mrow></math>, ![upper Y](images/c03-i0088.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>Y</mi></mrow></math> and ![upper Z](images/c03-i0089.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>Z</mi></mrow></math> represent three priorities from high to low, respectively. The same layer indicates that they have the same priority. Only the paths that meet the high‐priority intention can further judge whether these paths meet the next level intention.

![Figure 3.8 Paths satisfying the connectivity intention.](images/c03f008.png)

[**Figure 3.8**](#c03.xhtml#R_c03-fig-0008) Paths satisfying the connectivity intention.

![Figure 3.9 Three priority modes of intention symbols.](images/c03f009.png)

[**Figure 3.9**](#c03.xhtml#R_c03-fig-0009) Three priority modes of intention symbols.

 

## 3.4 Experiments

Two specific experiments are carried out to verify the feasibility of the framework discussed above. They are SSL and SSI. Then, we migrate the learned symbolic structure to four new networks with different network topologies, and let it find the shortest paths and the load‐balancing paths between all pairs of nodes. This is to verify the symbolic structure's ability of transferring knowledge across different networks.

 

### 3.4.1 Datasets

The experiment is divided into the training phase and the testing phase. In the training phase, we implement the classic shortest path routing algorithm in the network with a given topology to collect the target path between any pair of nodes

 

### 3.4.2 Experiments on Symbolic Semantic Learning

In order to evaluate the effectiveness of the proposed triangle theory of intention symbol semantics for autonomously achieving the intentions for path selection tasks, we conduct extensive experiments to verify the learning performance on intention symbols. The previous section mentioned three different intentions, namely the symbol ![upper A](images/c03-i0104.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>A</mi></mrow></math> for connectivity, the symbol ![upper B](images/c03-i0105.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>B</mi></mrow></math> for deadlock‐free, and the symbol ![upper C](images/c03-i0106.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>C</mi></mrow></math> for path performance.

Regarding the experiment of symbol ![upper A](images/c03-i0107.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>A</mi></mrow></math>, the ![upper A equals left-parenthesis mu Subscript a Baseline comma upsilon Subscript a Baseline comma eta Subscript a Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0108.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>A</mi><mo>=</mo><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>&#956;</mi><mi>a</mi></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>&#965;</mi><mi>a</mi></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>&#951;</mi><mi>a</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math> follows the probability distribution as follows:

As shown in [Figure 3.11](#c03.xhtml#c03-fig-0011), it is the probability distribution ![upper P left-parenthesis mu Subscript b Baseline comma upsilon Subscript b Baseline bar eta Subscript b Baseline comma gamma Subscript b Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0132.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>P</mi><mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mrow><msub><mi>&#956;</mi><mi>b</mi></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>&#965;</mi><mi>b</mi></msub><mo>&#8739;</mo><msub><mi>&#951;</mi><mi>b</mi></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>&#947;</mi><mi>b</mi></msub></mrow></mfenced></math> under the condition that ![eta Subscript b](images/c03-i0133.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>&#951;</mi><mi>b</mi></msub></math> takes the equal relationship, and ![gamma Subscript b](images/c03-i0134.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>&#947;</mi><mi>b</mi></msub></math> equals 1 after the training phase. It can be seen from the figure that when the value of ![mu Subscript b](images/c03-i0135.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>&#956;</mi><mi>b</mi></msub></math> is ![left-parenthesis phi 1 right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0136.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mrow><msub><mi>&#966;</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></mrow></mfenced></math> and the value of ![upsilon Subscript b](images/c03-i0137.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>&#965;</mi><mi>b</mi></msub></math> is ![left-parenthesis phi 0 right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0138.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mrow><msub><mi>&#966;</mi><mn>0</mn></msub></mrow></mfenced></math>, the probability is significantly greater than those in other cases. ![mu Subscript b](images/c03-i0139.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>&#956;</mi><mi>b</mi></msub></math>: ![left-parenthesis phi 1 right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0140.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mrow><msub><mi>&#966;</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></mrow></mfenced></math> = ![upsilon Subscript b](images/c03-i0141.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>&#965;</mi><mi>b</mi></msub></math>: ![left-parenthesis phi 0 right-parenthesis comma gamma Subscript b Baseline equals 1](images/c03-i0142.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mrow><msub><mi>&#966;</mi><mn>0</mn></msub></mrow></mfenced><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>&#947;</mi><mi>b</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></math> is a significant pattern in the path selection process. Its interpretation is that if the path is deadlock free, the current link to be selected will only be connected to one end of the path, and not to the other parts of the path. This mode is the semantic expression form of the intention of the symbol ![upper B](images/c03-i0143.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>B</mi></mrow></math>.

### 3.4.3 Experiments on Symbolic Structure Inferring

Although the intention semantics of the three symbols have been calculated through statistical learning, the precedence among them has not been calculated yet. This part of the experiment mainly calculates the priority relationship among the three intent symbols and constructs the symbol structure based on the priority relationship. As shown in [Figure 3.13](#c03.xhtml#c03-fig-0013), the linear structure mode has the highest probability. This means that the three intention symbols ![upper A comma upper B comma upper C](images/c03-i0150.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>A</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>B</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>C</mi></math> will follow a linear arrangement as shown on the leftmost side of [Figure 3.9](#c03.xhtml#c03-fig-0009). However, which of the three is the highest priority and which is the lowest priority have not been evaluated. The priority information is shown in [Figure 3.14](#c03.xhtml#c03-fig-0014). The structure of the form \[![1 comma 2 comma 3](images/c03-i0151.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>2</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>3</mn></math>\] has the highest probability. It means that the highest priority of the three intent symbols is ![upper A](images/c03-i0152.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>A</mi></mrow></math>, followed by ![upper B](images/c03-i0153.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>B</mi></mrow></math>, and the lowest priority is ![upper C](images/c03-i0154.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>C</mi></mrow></math>.

### 3.4.4 Experiments on Symbolic Structure Transferring

The previous experiments obtained the structure of the three intention symbols involved in the path selection task. In order to verify the accuracy of the symbols structure, they need to be transferred to different network topologies to test whether they can correctly complete the path selection task. The experimental results of this part are shown in [Figures 3.15](#c03.xhtml#c03-fig-0015)–[3.18](#c03.xhtml#c03-fig-0018). The abscissa of each graph is ![upper A](images/c03-i0155.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>A</mi></mrow></math>, ![upper A right-arrow upper B](images/c03-i0156.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>A</mi><mo>&#8594;</mo><mi>B</mi></math>, ![upper A right-arrow upper B right-arrow upper C](images/c03-i0157.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>A</mi><mo>&#8594;</mo><mi>B</mi><mo>&#8594;</mo><mi>C</mi></math>. They respectively represent the experimental results when the path selection task only satisfies the intention ![upper A](images/c03-i0158.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>A</mi></mrow></math>, the intentions ![upper A](images/c03-i0159.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>A</mi></mrow></math> and ![upper B](images/c03-i0160.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>B</mi></mrow></math>, and the intentions of ![upper A](images/c03-i0161.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>A</mi></mrow></math>, then ![upper B](images/c03-i0162.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>B</mi></mrow></math>, and finally ![upper C](images/c03-i0163.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>C</mi></mrow></math>. The ordinate of each graph is the value of ![upper P](images/c03-i0164.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>P</mi></mrow></math> and ![upper R](images/c03-i0165.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>R</mi></mrow></math>, which are light gray and dark gray columns, respectively. They are indicators to measure the correctness of the symbol structure. As mentioned earlier, the symbolic structure is the generation model that can generate ![upper D Subscript t a r g e t](images/c03-i0166.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>D</mi><mrow><mi>t</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>r</mi><mi>g</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>t</mi></mrow></msub></math> from ![upper D Subscript s p Baseline union upper D Subscript n o i s e](images/c03-i0167.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>D</mi><mrow><mi>s</mi><mi>p</mi></mrow></msub><mo>&#8746;</mo><msub><mi>D</mi><mrow><mi>n</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>i</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>e</mi></mrow></msub></math>. If according to the symbolic structure with the precedence order of the three intents inferred above, ![upper D Subscript t a r g e t](images/c03-i0168.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>D</mi><mrow><mi>t</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>r</mi><mi>g</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>t</mi></mrow></msub></math> can finally be obtained completely. It proves that this structure is the correct intent structure of the shortest path selection task. The ![upper P](images/c03-i0169.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>P</mi></mrow></math> value indicates what percentage of ![upper D Subscript t a r g e t](images/c03-i0170.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>D</mi><mrow><mi>t</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>r</mi><mi>g</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>t</mi></mrow></msub></math> is obtained when the intent corresponding to the abscissa is satisfied. The ![upper R](images/c03-i0171.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>R</mi></mrow></math> value indicates the proportion of the obtained path that also belongs to ![upper D Subscript t a r g e t](images/c03-i0172.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>D</mi><mrow><mi>t</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>r</mi><mi>g</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>t</mi></mrow></msub></math> when the intent of the corresponding abscissa is satisfied. Therefore, we can see that in the four experimental results graphs, the ![upper P](images/c03-i0173.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>P</mi></mrow></math> value is gradually equal to 1, and the ![upper R](images/c03-i0174.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>R</mi></mrow></math> value is always 1. ## 3.5 Conclusion

This chapter mainly discussed learning the intentions behind network tasks based on symbolism and statistical learning, and inferring the priority relationship between multiple intentions. We regarded intention as a meaningful symbol and proposed a triangle theory of intention symbol semantics. Under the guidance of the triangle theory, the algorithm learned that the semantics of an intention symbol is to sample a random vector. The result of sampling made the intention symbol have a clear meaning. When a network task involves multiple intents, it is also necessary to infer the priority relationship between the intents. The intents symbol structure is constructed based on this priority. Experiments showed that the framework of SSL and SSI is feasible.

# 4  
Virtual Network Embedding via Hierarchical Reinforcement Learning[¹](#c04.xhtml#c04-note-0001)

 

## 4.1 Introduction

As one of the most promising technologies for the future Internet, network virtualization decouples the virtual network services from the underlying substrate network, which offers a great solution to evaluate new protocols and services (Anderson et al., [2005](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0002), Cheng et al., [2018](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0008)). Besides, network virtualization techniques have been widely applied in many research testbeds, such as 4WARD (Feamster et al., [2007](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0018)) and CABO (Endo et al., [2011](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0016)). Today, as a key enabling technology for 5G network softwarization (Cheng et al., [2018](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0008), [2020](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0009)), network virtualization has been successfully used in the telecommunication market. It can be exemplified by OpenFlow (McKeown et al., [2008](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0024)), which is supported by the industry within the Open Networking Foundation (Open Networking Foundation, [2010](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0026)). In general, the process of embedding virtual networks into a substrate network is called virtual network embedding (VNE) (Cheng et al., [2021](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0010), Yan et al., [2020a](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0040)).

Nowadays, a business model called Infrastructure as a Service (IaaS) (Bhardwaj et al., [2010](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0005)) has been widely accepted by the market, where the role of Internet Service Providers (ISPs) is decoupled into two parts: the infrastructure provider (InP) and the service provider (SP). In this model, the InP is responsible for deploying and maintaining the network resources, and the SP takes charge of end‐to‐end services of users. As shown in [Figure 4.1](#c04.xhtml#c04-fig-0001), the function of SP can be divided into three lines: (1) Virtual Network (VN) provider that mounts the virtual resources into the InPs, (2) VN operator that manages the VNs according to the requests, (3) User service provider that focuses on business models by abstracting customized services. By embedding virtual resources into the physical network dynamically, the ISPs can reap large gains through effective VNE algorithms, which aim to optimize the dynamic resource allocation and offer various end‐to‐end customized services to users. In practice, the measurement criteria of an VNE algorithm cover many different objectives, including quality of service (QoS), economic profit, resource cost, embedding efficiency, and energy efficiency.

![Figure 4.1 IaaS business model.](images/c04f001.png)

[**Figure 4.1**](#c04.xhtml#R_c04-fig-0001) IaaS business model.

In VNE, the primary entity is a virtual network (VN) consisting of a set of network elements (network nodes and network links). Virtual nodes are joined by virtual links according to a virtual topology. By virtualizing both nodes and links, virtual topologies with varying features can be cohosted on the same substrate network hardware through network‐embedding algorithms. Besides, due to such an abstraction of virtual networks and substrate networks, network operators can manage networks in a centralized and dynamic way. Generally, the process of VNE consists of two steps, virtual node mapping and virtual link mapping (Chowdhury and Boutaba, [2010](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0011)). For virtual network mapping, a virtual node is allocated in a physical node, and with regard to virtual link mapping, a virtual network link between two virtual nodes has to be mapped into a sequence of physical network links which can connect the corresponding two physical nodes.

 

## 4.2 Motivation

Recall that the VNE problem is known to be NP‐hard (Yu et al., [2008](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0045)) due to the huge search space of the substrate network. Traditional VNE algorithms are divided into two categories: the exact and the heuristic solutions. Using the exact optimization strategies (Wolsey, [1998](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0038)), the methods aim to find the optimal solution through, e.g. integer linear programming (ILP) and mixed‐integer linear programming (MILP) (Chowdhury et al., [2009](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0012), Shanbhag et al., [2015](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0030)). Due to the high complexity, such exact methods take considerable time on calculating feasible solutions, which means that they can only be used to solve small‐size instances of VNE problems. Instead of being fixed on getting the optimal embedding solution, the heuristic solutions manage to find a feasible solution with lower execution time (Cormen et al., [2009](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0014)). However, one of the problems of the heuristic methods is that they are easily stuck in a local optimum that may be far away from the global optimum (Cao et al., [2019](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0006)). It is obvious that in practice, traditional techniques for solving online problems are impractical because of the unpredictability and the general huge search space of the substrate network.

With the fast development of artificial intelligence (AI), machine learning (ML) and deep learning (DL) have been put into use in the field of VNE (He et al., [2021](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0021), Xiao et al., [2019](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0039)). Due to the high fitting ability and flexible adjustment of network parameters, AI‐based algorithms can obtain the global optimal solution and make embedding strategies within an acceptable time (Yao et al., [2018b](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0043)). In addition, due to the outstanding performance of automatic exploration and quick development, reinforcement learning (RL) (Kaelbling et al., [1996](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0022), Sutton and Barto, [2018](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0033)) has also been taken into account. In RL, an agent starts from scratch and gradually learns the optimal VNE policy, by exploring the external environment, to maximize the accumulated reward. However, there are three major drawbacks of the existing RL‐based methods. First, existing approaches (Yao et al., [2018a](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0042),[b](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0043), Xiao et al., [2019](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0039)) make embedding algorithms focus on the current virtual network request (VNR), which may waste resources on embedding the infeasible VNRs. Second, existing methods treat all VNRs equally and independently, which loses long‐time horizons. Third, in the real scenario, the extrinsic reward is usually sparse, which is returned only when a VNR is embedded completely. Existing methods, however, decompose the extrinsic reward in an implicit way and cannot ensure the globally optimal solution (Yao et al., [2018a](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0042)).

Hierarchical reinforcement learning (HRL) has been proved excellent at speeding up the learning process (Song et al., [2019](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0032)) and solving the task with long‐time horizons in playing games like Go (Silver et al., [2016](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0031)) and Atari (Mnih et al., [2013](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0025)). Typically, to get long‐time horizons, HRL is implemented as a high‐level agent that periodically assigns subgoals to low‐level agents that learns from experience at different temporal abstraction levels simultaneously. Besides, by dividing the reward into extrinsic reward and intrinsic reward, HRL works well in the sparse reward problem (Bacon et al., [2017](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0003)) in comparison with the general reinforcement learning (RL).

In this chapter, based on HRL, a proactive VNE algorithm is proposed, which considers both the long‐term impact of a VNR and the short‐term effect of an embedding action. We call this proactive algorithm as VNE‐HRL. In this algorithm, a high‐level agent first selects the currently feasible VNR that has the greatest long‐term return from a window‐based batch, which ensures long‐term benefits. Then, a low‐level agent embeds the selected VNR on the substrate network according to a low‐level policy, which treats a VNR embedding as a sequence of virtual nodes embedding. Meanwhile, the extrinsic and intrinsic rewards are returned to the high‐level agent and the low‐level agent, respectively, which encourage the agents to improve the embedding policies in order to achieve better performance in the future instead of maximizing the current revenue. Both high‐level and low‐level policies are trained with the double deep Q network (DDQN) algorithm (Van Hasselt et al., [2016](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0036)), which can achieve the stable convergence. With the help of DDQN, agents can gradually optimize the policy from scratch without much manual intervention.

The rest of this chapter is constructed as follows: [Section 4.3](#c04.xhtml#c04-sec-0003) introduces the preliminaries of the work, including the definitions and mathematical description of VNE, RL, and HRL. The framework of the model is presented in [Section 4.4](#c04.xhtml#c04-sec-0011). Performance evaluation results are shown and analyzed in [Section 4.5](#c04.xhtml#c04-sec-0022). [Section 4.6](#c04.xhtml#c04-sec-0029) presents related works. Finally, [Section 4.7](#c04.xhtml#c04-sec-0032) concludes this chapter.

 

## 4.3 Preliminaries and Notations

In this section, we will introduce the following lines: mathematical formulation of the VNE problem ([Section 4.3.1](#c04.xhtml#c04-sec-0004)), reinforcement learning ([Section 4.3.2](#c04.xhtml#c04-sec-0009)), and hierarchical reinforcement learning ([Section 4.3.3](#c04.xhtml#c04-sec-0010)).

 

### 4.3.1 Virtual Network Embedding

 

#### 4.3.1.1 Substrate Network and Virtual Network

VNE is a process of embedding virtual networks to the substrate network according to VNRs. A substrate network (SN) can be defined as a weighted undirected graph ![upper G Superscript upper S Baseline equals left-parenthesis upper N Superscript upper S Baseline comma upper L Superscript upper S Baseline comma upper A Subscript upper N Superscript upper S Baseline comma upper A Subscript upper L Superscript upper S Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0001.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msup><mi>G</mi><mi>S</mi></msup><mo>=</mo><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msup><mi>N</mi><mi>S</mi></msup><mo>,</mo><msup><mi>L</mi><mi>S</mi></msup><mo>,</mo><msubsup><mrow><mi>A</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>N</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>S</mi></mrow></msubsup><mo>,</mo><msubsup><mrow><mi>A</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>L</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>S</mi></mrow></msubsup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math>, where ![upper N Superscript upper S](images/c04-i0002.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msup><mi>N</mi><mi>S</mi></msup></math> and ![upper L Superscript upper S](images/c04-i0003.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msup><mi>L</mi><mi>S</mi></msup></math> denote the sets of substrate nodes and links, respectively, and ![upper A Subscript upper N Superscript upper S](images/c04-i0004.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msubsup><mrow><mi>A</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>N</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>S</mi></mrow></msubsup></math> and ![upper A Subscript upper L Superscript upper S](images/c04-i0005.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msubsup><mrow><mi>A</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>L</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>S</mi></mrow></msubsup></math> represent the attributes of substrate nodes and links, respectively. In this chapter, for the sake of clarity of illustration, CPU processing capability and the bandwidth of each substrate link are considered as the node attribute and the substrate link attribute, respectively.

Similarly, a virtual network (VN) can be described as a weighted undirected graph ![upper G Superscript upper V Baseline equals left-parenthesis upper N Superscript upper V Baseline comma upper L Superscript upper V Baseline comma upper R Subscript upper N Superscript upper V Baseline comma upper R Subscript upper L Superscript upper V Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0006.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msup><mi>G</mi><mi>V</mi></msup><mo>=</mo><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msup><mi>N</mi><mi>V</mi></msup><mo>,</mo><msup><mi>L</mi><mi>V</mi></msup><mo>,</mo><msubsup><mrow><mi>R</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>N</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>V</mi></mrow></msubsup><mo>,</mo><msubsup><mrow><mi>R</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>L</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>V</mi></mrow></msubsup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math>, where ![upper N Superscript upper V](images/c04-i0007.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msup><mi>N</mi><mi>V</mi></msup></math> and ![upper L Superscript upper V](images/c04-i0008.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msup><mi>L</mi><mi>V</mi></msup></math> are virtual nodes and links, and ![upper R Subscript upper N Superscript upper V](images/c04-i0009.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msubsup><mrow><mi>R</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>N</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>V</mi></mrow></msubsup></math> and ![upper R Subscript upper L Superscript upper V](images/c04-i0010.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msubsup><mrow><mi>R</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>L</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>V</mi></mrow></msubsup></math> are the requirements of virtual nodes and links, respectively. Therefore, a VNR can be denoted as ![upper V upper N upper R equals left-parenthesis upper G Superscript upper V Baseline comma t Subscript a Baseline comma t Subscript d Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0011.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>V</mi><mi>N</mi><mi>R</mi><mo>=</mo><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msup><mi>G</mi><mi>V</mi></msup><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>t</mi><mi>a</mi></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>t</mi><mi>d</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math>, where ![t Subscript a](images/c04-i0012.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>t</mi><mi>a</mi></msub></math> is the arrival time of a VNR. It is worth noting that when embedding takes place online, the departure time ![t Subscript d](images/c04-i0013.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>t</mi><mi>d</mi></msub></math> of VNR is uncertain.

 

#### 4.3.1.2 The VNE Problem

The VNE problem can be defined as a mapping process from ![upper G Superscript upper V](images/c04-i0014.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msup><mi>G</mi><mi>V</mi></msup></math> to a subgraph ![upper G Superscript upper S](images/c04-i0015.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msup><mi>G</mi><mi>S</mi></msup></math>:

(4.1)![StartLayout 1st Row 1st Column Blank 2nd Column upper G Superscript upper V Baseline left-parenthesis upper N Superscript upper V Baseline comma upper L Superscript upper V Baseline right-parenthesis right-arrow upper G Superscript upper S Baseline left-parenthesis upper N Superscript upper S Baseline comma upper L Superscript upper S Baseline right-parenthesis EndLayout](images/c04-math-0001.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll"><mtable><mtr><mtd></mtd><mtd><msup><mi>G</mi><mi>V</mi></msup><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msup><mi>N</mi><mi>V</mi></msup><mo>,</mo><msup><mi>L</mi><mi>V</mi></msup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>&#8594;</mo><msup><mi>G</mi><mi>S</mi></msup><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msup><mi>N</mi><mi>S</mi></msup><mo>,</mo><msup><mi>L</mi><mi>S</mi></msup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mtd></mtr></mtable></math> 

The embedding of VNR involves two mapping process: (1) the node mapping procedure, in which the available computing resources of the substrate node should be larger than the requested resources from the mapping virtual node. (2) The link mapping procedure, in which a virtual link can be embedded onto several physical links. The available resources of substrate links should be larger than the requirement of the virtual link. Besides, there are two points to note. First, the virtual nodes from different VNRs may be embedded into the same substrate node. Second, a virtual link may consist of several physical links on the substrate network, which may result in the use of more resources than requested.

To clarify, we describe the embedding procedure in [Figure 4.2](#c04.xhtml#c04-fig-0002). Node ![c](images/c04-i0016.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>c</mi></mrow></math> shares the same substrate node ![upper C](images/c04-i0017.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>C</mi></mrow></math> with the Node ![d](images/c04-i0018.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>d</mi></mrow></math>. In addition, the virtual link between ![d](images/c04-i0019.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>d</mi></mrow></math> and ![f](images/c04-i0020.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>f</mi></mrow></math>, ![upper R Superscript upper V Baseline left-parenthesis d comma f right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0021.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msup><mi>R</mi><mi>V</mi></msup><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>d</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>f</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math>, crosses over two substrate links, including ![upper L Superscript upper S Baseline left-parenthesis upper C comma upper E right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0022.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msup><mi>L</mi><mi>S</mi></msup><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>C</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>E</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math> and ![upper L Superscript upper S Baseline left-parenthesis upper E comma upper G right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0023.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msup><mi>L</mi><mi>S</mi></msup><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>E</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>G</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math>.

![Figure 4.2 A typical scene of a VNE problem. The edge weights and node weights of VNR represent the bandwidth and the CPU requests, respectively. Besides, the edge and node weights of the substrate node stand for the bandwidth and CPU resources, respectively.](images/c04f002.png)

[**Figure 4.2**](#c04.xhtml#R_c04-fig-0002) A typical scene of a VNE problem. The edge weights and node weights of VNR represent the bandwidth and the CPU requests, respectively. Besides, the edge and node weights of the substrate node stand for the bandwidth and CPU resources, respectively.

 

#### 4.3.1.3 Evaluation Metrics

In the VNE field, three key metrics have been widely used to measure the effectiveness of a VNE algorithm (Thakkar et al., [2020](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0035)), including acceptance ratio, long‐time average revenue, and rev2cost.

*   Acceptance Ratio: The VNR acceptance ratio of the substrate network at time ![upper T](images/c04-i0024.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>T</mi></mrow></math> can be defined
