### 4.3.2 Reinforcement Learning

In the standard reinforcement learning (RL) framework, an agent interacts with an environment and learns from the sequential exploration (Wang et al., [2019](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0037)). RL follows a Markov Decision Process (MDP) formulated as ![left-parenthesis script upper S comma script upper A comma script upper P comma r comma gamma right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0040.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi mathvariant="script">S</mi><mo>,</mo><mi mathvariant="script">A</mi><mo>,</mo><mi mathvariant="script">P</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>r</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>&#947;</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math>, where ![script upper S](images/c04-i0041.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi mathvariant="script">S</mi></mrow></math> denotes the environment state space, ![script upper A](images/c04-i0042.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi mathvariant="script">A</mi></mrow></math> denotes the action space, ![script upper P colon script upper S times script upper A times script upper S right-arrow left-bracket 0 comma 1 right-bracket](images/c04-i0043.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi mathvariant="script">P</mi><mo>:</mo><mi mathvariant="script">S</mi><mo>&#215;</mo><mi mathvariant="script">A</mi><mo>&#215;</mo><mi mathvariant="script">S</mi><mo>&#8594;</mo><mo stretchy="false">\[</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">\]</mo></math> denotes the transition function to generate the next state according to the current state and action, ![r colon script upper S times script upper A times script upper S right-arrow double-struck upper R](images/c04-i0044.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>r</mi><mo>:</mo><mi mathvariant="script">S</mi><mo>&#215;</mo><mi mathvariant="script">A</mi><mo>&#215;</mo><mi mathvariant="script">S</mi><mo>&#8594;</mo><mi mathvariant="double-struck">R</mi></math> denotes the reward function, and ![gamma element-of left-bracket 0 comma 1 right-bracket](images/c04-i0045.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>&#947;</mi><mo>&#8712;</mo><mo stretchy="false">\[</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">\]</mo></math> is a discount factor. According to the policy ![pi colon script upper S times script upper A right-arrow left-bracket 0 comma 1 right-bracket](images/c04-i0046.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>&#960;</mi><mo>:</mo><mi mathvariant="script">S</mi><mo>&#215;</mo><mi mathvariant="script">A</mi><mo>&#8594;</mo><mo stretchy="false">\[</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">\]</mo></math>, a trajectory ![tau equals s 0 comma a 0 comma r 0 comma s 1 midline-horizontal-ellipsis](images/c04-i0047.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>&#964;</mi><mo>=</mo><msub><mi>s</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>r</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>s</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mspace width="-0.1em"/><mi>&#8943;</mi></math> is sampled. The objective of RL is to find an optimal policy ![pi Superscript asterisk](images/c04-i0048.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msup><mi>&#960;</mi><mo>&#42;</mo></msup></math> which can maximize the expected cumulative reward ![upper R Subscript t 0 Baseline equals upper E left-bracket sigma-summation Underscript t equals t 0 Overscript infinity Endscripts gamma Superscript t Baseline r Subscript t Baseline right-bracket](images/c04-i0049.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mrow><mi>R</mi></mrow><mrow><msub><mi>t</mi><mn>0</mn></msub></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><mi>E</mi><mfenced open="\[" close="\]" separators=""><mrow><msubsup><mo>&#8721;</mo><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo>=</mo><msub><mi>t</mi><mn>0</mn></msub></mrow><mi>&#8734;</mi></msubsup><msup><mi>&#947;</mi><mi>t</mi></msup><msub><mi>r</mi><mi>t</mi></msub></mrow></mfenced></math>, where ![gamma Superscript t Baseline r Subscript t](images/c04-i0050.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msup><mi>&#947;</mi><mi>t</mi></msup><msub><mi>r</mi><mi>t</mi></msub></math> is the discount reward at time ![t](images/c04-i0051.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>t</mi></mrow></math>.

As we can see, instead of defining an explicit objective function in linear programming or labeling the data in supervised machine learning, the RL agent improves its policy according to the expected cumulative reward. Therefore, the agent may sacrifice the best current reward to obtain a better long‐term performance.

 

### 4.3.3 Hierarchical Reinforcement Learning

HRL has been successfully used in many fields, such as robotics and games (Sutton et al., [1999](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0034)). Typically, to solve tasks with long time horizons, HRL is implemented as a high‐level agent, which periodically assigns subgoals to low‐level agents. It has been proved that different levels of abstraction implemented by this hierarchy consider different long‐term planning and achieve different performance (Sutton et al., [1999](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0034)). The *options framework* (Sutton et al., [1999](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0034)) is one of the most popular HRL architectures, where an MDP endowed with a set of options is extended to a Semi‐Markov Decision Process (SMDP) (Du et al., [2020](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0015)). At the high level of the hierarchy, an option ![left-parenthesis pi comma upper I comma beta right-parenthesis element-of script upper O](images/c04-i0052.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>&#960;</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>I</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>&#946;</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>&#8712;</mo><mi mathvariant="script">O</mi></math> includes a policy ![pi](images/c04-i0053.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>&#960;</mi></mrow></math> over actions, an initial state ![upper I](images/c04-i0054.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>I</mi></mrow></math>, and a terminate function ![beta](images/c04-i0055.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>&#946;</mi></mrow></math>. In the options framework, a high‐level agent chooses an option ![o](images/c04-i0056.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>o</mi></mrow></math> according to the high‐level policy to execute until being terminated by ![beta](images/c04-i0057.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>&#946;</mi></mrow></math>. At the low level of the hierarchy, according to the low‐level policy, a low‐level agent selects a set of actions ![a Subscript i comma j](images/c04-i0058.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>a</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>j</mi></mrow></msub></math> to complete the task indicated by the chosen option.

 

## 4.4 The Framework of VNE‐HRL

 

### 4.4.1 Overview

It is important to handle the VNRs as they arrive rather than handling a large batch of requests at once (Yu et al., [2008](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0045)), because VNRs may arrive dynamically and stay in the substrate network for an arbitrary period. Therefore, we are inspired to apply the *time window* and *request queue* mechanisms to handle VNRs on time (Yu et al., [2008](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0045)). The time window spans a short period, and the VNRs in the current time window will be processed. The processed VNRs may be deferred because of the lack of bandwidth or CPU resources and will be put into the request queue. As shown in [Figure 4.3](#c04.xhtml#c04-fig-0003), a window‐based batch that consists of the VNRs in the time window and the request queue is the input of the algorithm.

![Figure 4.3 The architecture of the VNE‐HRL.](images/c04f003.png)

[**Figure 4.3**](#c04.xhtml#R_c04-fig-0003) The architecture of the VNE‐HRL.

According to the definition of HRL, we model the VNE problem as a Semi‐Markov Decision Process (SMDP). First, we encode the state of VNRs and SNs as the environment state where the agents will be used to make decisions. For each process, a high‐level agent selects a feasible option (i.e. a VNR from a window‐based batch) with the greatest long‐term rewards according to a high‐level policy. Instead of treating all VNRs equally and independently, the high‐level agent (HEA) process described above ensures that the algorithm is proactive. Then, a low‐level agent performs a sequence of actions to embed the VN according to a low‐level policy. After an option or action terminates, the environment will give feedback (i.e. rewards) to encourage the agents to improve the policies. Next, the state of the environment is updated. This process repeats until all VNRs are processed. Based on the above settings, we propose a novel HRL‐based VNE framework as shown in [Figure 4.3](#c04.xhtml#c04-fig-0003), including a high‐level VNE agent (HEA) and a low‐level agent (LEA).

To express the ideas clearly, first we define the required terms.

*   **High‐level State Space** ![upper S Superscript upper H](images/c04-i0059.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msup><mi>S</mi><mi>H</mi></msup></math>: A high‐level state ![s Superscript h Baseline element-of upper S Superscript upper H](images/c04-i0060.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msup><mi>s</mi><mi>h</mi></msup><mo>&#8712;</mo><msup><mi>S</mi><mi>H</mi></msup></math> is a hidden vector representing the high‐level environment state, which is extracted from VNRs in the window and the underlying SN.
*   **Low‐level State Space** ![upper S Superscript upper L](images/c04-i0061.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msup><mi>S</mi><mi>L</mi></msup></math>: A low‐level state ![s Superscript l Baseline element-of upper S Superscript upper L](images/c04-i0062.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msup><mi>s</mi><mi>l</mi></msup><mo>&#8712;</mo><msup><mi>S</mi><mi>L</mi></msup></math> is a hidden vector denoting the low‐level environment state, which is extracted from the selected VNR and the underlying SN.
*   **Option Space** ![script upper O](images/c04-i0063.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi mathvariant="script">O</mi></mrow></math>: An option ![o element-of script upper O](images/c04-i0064.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>o</mi><mo>&#8712;</mo><mi mathvariant="script">O</mi></math> is a VNR selected from a window‐based batch, which can be regarded as an implementation of admission control.
*   **Action Space** ![script upper A](images/c04-i0065.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi mathvariant="script">A</mi></mrow></math>: An action ![a element-of script upper A](images/c04-i0066.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>a</mi><mo>&#8712;</mo><mi mathvariant="script">A</mi></math> is the valid embedding process that allocates a virtual network node onto a substrate network node.
*   **High‐level Reward** ![r Superscript h](images/c04-i0067.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msup><mi>r</mi><mi>h</mi></msup></math>: A high‐level reward ![r Superscript h](images/c04-i0068.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msup><mi>r</mi><mi>h</mi></msup></math> is a scalar returned to the HEA and is also called an extrinsic reward, which evaluates the short‐term benefits of an option.
*   **Low‐level Reward** ![r Superscript l](images/c04-i0069.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msup><mi>r</mi><mi>l</mi></msup></math>: A low‐level reward ![r Superscript l](images/c04-i0070.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msup><mi>r</mi><mi>l</mi></msup></math> is a scalar returned to the LEA and is also called an intrinsic reward, which evaluates the short‐term benefits of an action.
*   **Discount Factor** ![gamma](images/c04-i0071.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>&#947;</mi></mrow></math>: ![gamma element-of left-bracket 0 comma 1 right-bracket](images/c04-i0072.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>&#947;</mi><mo>&#8712;</mo><mo stretchy="false">\[</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">\]</mo></math> defines the discounting factor to measure the expect value of future rewards.

 

### 4.4.2 The High‐level Agent

HEA aims to select a feasible option (i.e. a VNR from a window‐based batch) with the greatest long‐term reward according to a high‐level policy. Instead of treating all VNRs equally and independently, the selection process ensures that the algorithm is proactive, including two steps: (1) The environment states are encoded by extracting the high‐level state features through a state encoder. (2) According to the encoded states, HEA estimates the long‐term cumulative reward of options and executes an ![epsilon](images/c04-i0073.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>&#949;</mi></mrow></math>‐soft strategy (Bacon et al., [2017](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0003)) to select a VNR. Then, HEA assigns LEA to embed the selected VNR. When the selected VNR embedding completes, a high‐level reward is returned and a new VNR will be selected from the window‐based batch, while the VNRs that have been handled in the current batch will be masked. This process continues repeatedly.

In what follows, the details of the state encoder, the estimated long‐term cumulative reward, and the short‐term high‐level reward will be illustrated.

 

#### 4.4.2.1 State Encoder for HEA

The high‐level environment states include the VNRs in the window‐based batch, and the underlying SN. Obviously, the spatial features of network topology are vital for the VNE problem. Instead of using the explicit link features, we choose an alternative method to extract more useful features in an efficient way. Inspired by the work Yan et al. ([2020b](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0041)), we apply graph convolutional network (GCN) to extract the spatial features of VN and SN. The state of SN can be encoded as follows:

The ![i](images/c04-i0074.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></math>‐th VNR can be formulated by,

(4.8)![e Subscript i Superscript upper V Baseline equals upper G upper C upper N left-parenthesis upper V upper N upper R Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c04-math-0008.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll"><msubsup><mrow><mi>e</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>V</mi></mrow></msubsup><mo>=</mo><mi>G</mi><mi>C</mi><mi>N</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mrow><mi>V</mi><mi>N</mi><mi>R</mi></mrow><mi>i</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math> 

In practice, the number of VNRs in a time window is usually unpredictable, and we set a fixed value ![upper M](images/c04-i0075.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>M</mi></mrow></math> to indicate the size of the window‐based batch. If the number of VNRs in the current window and queue exceeds ![upper M](images/c04-i0076.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>M</mi></mrow></math>, the excess VNRs will be added into the current batch gradually to cover the processed VNRs. Otherwise, the batch will be filled up to ![upper M](images/c04-i0077.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>M</mi></mrow></math> with empty VNRs. Thus, the embedding of all the VNRs can be formulated by adding up all the VNRs as follows:

(4.9)![upper E Superscript upper V Baseline equals sigma-summation Underscript i equals 0 Overscript upper M minus 1 Endscripts e Subscript i Superscript upper V](images/c04-math-0009.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll"><msup><mi>E</mi><mi>V</mi></msup><mo>=</mo><munderover><mrow><mo stretchy="false" movablelimits="false">&#8721;</mo></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow><mrow><mi>M</mi><mo>&#8722;</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></munderover><msubsup><mrow><mi>e</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>V</mi></mrow></msubsup></math> 

As last, the high‐level environment state for HEA can be encoded by adding up the embeddings of VNRs and SN:

where ![s Superscript h](images/c04-i0078.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msup><mi>s</mi><mi>h</mi></msup></math> is a vector with length ![upper N Superscript upper S](images/c04-i0079.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msup><mi>N</mi><mi>S</mi></msup></math> and represents the global state.

 

#### 4.4.2.2 Estimated Long‐term Cumulative Reward

Typically, a multilayer neural network with parameter ![theta](images/c04-i0080.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>&#952;</mi></mrow></math> is utilized to estimate the long‐term cumulative reward of an option. Given an ![n](images/c04-i0081.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow></math>‐dimensional state ![s Superscript h](images/c04-i0082.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msup><mi>s</mi><mi>h</mi></msup></math>, it outputs a vector of estimated long‐term rewards over options.

In HEA, we apply a two‐layer fully connected network, and the estimated long‐term rewards ![upper R Subscript h](images/c04-i0083.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>R</mi><mi>h</mi></msub></math> can be calculated by

(4.11)![upper R Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis s Superscript h Baseline semicolon theta right-parenthesis equals upper F u l l upper C o n n e c t upper L a y e r squared left-parenthesis s Superscript h Baseline semicolon theta right-parenthesis](images/c04-math-0011.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>R</mi><mi>h</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msup><mi>s</mi><mi>h</mi></msup><mo>;</mo><mi>&#952;</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>=</mo><msup><mrow><mi>F</mi><mi>u</mi><mi>l</mi><mi>l</mi><mi>C</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>n</mi><mi>n</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>c</mi><mi>t</mi><mi>L</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>y</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>r</mi></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msup><mi>s</mi><mi>h</mi></msup><mo>;</mo><mi>&#952;</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math> 

where ![s Superscript h](images/c04-i0084.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msup><mi>s</mi><mi>h</mi></msup></math> is the encoded global state as defined in Eq. ([4.10](#c04.xhtml#c04-disp-0010)). Thus, we obtain the estimated long‐term cumulative reward of each VNR. Based on the results, we can select the option with maximum estimated long‐term cumulative reward with a probability of ![1 minus epsilon](images/c04-i0085.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mn>1</mn><mo>&#8722;</mo><mi>&#949;</mi></math> or randomly select an option with a probability of ![epsilon](images/c04-i0086.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>&#949;</mi></mrow></math>. Then, we manage to embed the corresponding VNR into the SN by a low‐level agent.

 

#### 4.4.2.3 Short‐term High‐level Reward

A high‐level reward indicates the real effectiveness of an option ![o Subscript t](images/c04-i0087.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>o</mi><mi>t</mi></msub></math>, which is extrinsic and closely related to ISP pricing strategies. To get a high‐yield ratio, we should encourage successful VNR embeddings. A positive reward is returned if the selected VNR is embedded completely and otherwise, a negative one returns. Besides, HEA takes the extrinsic factor into consideration, and the agent should not only consider the profit but also the cost. In this way, the high‐level reward is expressed as follows:

where the meanings of ![upper R e v left-parenthesis upper G Superscript upper V Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0088.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>R</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msup><mi>G</mi><mi>V</mi></msup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math> and ![upper C o s t left-parenthesis upper G Superscript upper V Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0089.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>C</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>t</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msup><mi>G</mi><mi>V</mi></msup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math> have been explained in Eqs. ([4.3](#c04.xhtml#c04-disp-0003)) and ([4.5](#c04.xhtml#c04-disp-0005)), respectively, in [Section 4.3.1.3](#c04.xhtml#c04-sec-0008).

 

### 4.4.3 The Low‐level Agent

After HEA selects an option (i.e. VNR) at a high level, LEA performs a sequence of actions to embed the VN according to the low‐level policy ![pi](images/c04-i0090.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>&#960;</mi></mrow></math>. However, as the size of nodes increases, the number of subgraphs of the network grows exponentially. It is impossible to consider every subgraph as embedding actions, as the action space will be enormous. Thus, it is not fit for the RL agent to search every possible subgraph of the SN to host the VN. In this work, the VNR embedding process is decomposed into a sequence of virtual node embeddings following the work (Yao et al., [2018a](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0042)).

The embedding process of a virtual node includes node mapping and link mapping. With respect to the node mapping, LEA chooses a certain substrate network node to host a virtual network node. The selection process involves two steps: (1) The environment states are encoded by extracting the low‐level state features through a state encoder, and (2) According to the encoded states, LEA estimates the long‐term rewards of all actions through a multilayer neural network and executes an ![epsilon](images/c04-i0091.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>&#949;</mi></mrow></math>‐soft strategy to select a substrate node to host the current VNR. If the chosen substrate node has enough spare resources for the current virtual node, we will map the virtual links between the current virtual node and the other already embedded virtual node. However, it is NP‐hard to find an optimal mapping from a virtual link to a set of substrate paths (Kolliopoulos and Stein, [1997](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0023)). Therefore, for each virtual link, *the* ![k](images/c04-i0092.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow></math>*‐shortest paths* for increasing ![k](images/c04-i0093.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow></math> are searched until we find a substrate path that has sufficient bandwidth to embed the corresponding virtual link. *The* ![k](images/c04-i0094.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow></math>*‐shortest paths* algorithm can be solved in ![upper O left-parenthesis m plus n l o g n plus k right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0095.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>O</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>m</mi><mo>+</mo><mi>n</mi><mi>l</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>g</mi><mi>n</mi><mo>+</mo><mi>k</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math> time in an SN with ![n](images/c04-i0096.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow></math> nodes and ![m](images/c04-i0097.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>m</mi></mrow></math> links (Eppstein, [1998](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0017)). In consideration of computational efficiency, ![k](images/c04-i0098.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow></math> should be kept small.

Next, the details of the state encoder, the estimated long‐term cumulative reward, and the short‐term low‐level reward will be illustrated.

 

#### 4.4.3.1 State Encoder for LEA

The low‐level environment states include the current virtual node and the underlying SN. Thus, the local state can be formulated by adding up the vectors of the current virtual node and the SN as follows:

(4.13)![s Superscript l Baseline equals e Subscript s e l e c t e d Superscript upper V Baseline plus upper E Superscript upper S](images/c04-math-0013.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll"><msup><mi>s</mi><mi>l</mi></msup><mo>=</mo><msubsup><mrow><mi>e</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>s</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>l</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>c</mi><mi>t</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>d</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>V</mi></mrow></msubsup><mo>+</mo><msup><mi>E</mi><mi>S</mi></msup></math> 

where ![e Superscript upper V](images/c04-i0099.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msup><mi>e</mi><mi>V</mi></msup></math> and ![upper E Superscript upper S](images/c04-i0100.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msup><mi>E</mi><mi>S</mi></msup></math> have already been declared in [Section 4.4.2.1](#c04.xhtml#c04-sec-0014).

 

#### 4.4.3.2 Estimated Long‐term Cumulative Reward

Typically, a multilayer neural network with parameter ![xi](images/c04-i0101.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>&#958;</mi></mrow></math> is utilized to estimate the long‐term cumulative reward of an action. Given an ![n](images/c04-i0102.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow></math>‐dimensional state ![s Superscript l](images/c04-i0103.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msup><mi>s</mi><mi>l</mi></msup></math>, it outputs a vector of estimated long‐term rewards over actions.

In LEA, we apply a two‐layer fully connected network and the estimated long‐term cumulative rewards ![upper R Subscript l](images/c04-i0104.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>R</mi><mi>l</mi></msub></math> can be formulated by

(4.14)![upper R Subscript l Baseline left-parenthesis s Superscript l Baseline semicolon xi right-parenthesis equals upper F u l l upper C o n n e c t upper L a y e r squared left-parenthesis s Superscript l Baseline semicolon xi right-parenthesis](images/c04-math-0014.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>R</mi><mi>l</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msup><mi>s</mi><mi>l</mi></msup><mo>;</mo><mi>&#958;</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>=</mo><msup><mrow><mi>F</mi><mi>u</mi><mi>l</mi><mi>l</mi><mi>C</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>n</mi><mi>n</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>c</mi><mi>t</mi><mi>L</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>y</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>r</mi></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msup><mi>s</mi><mi>l</mi></msup><mo>;</mo><mi>&#958;</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math> 

where ![s Superscript l](images/c04-i0105.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msup><mi>s</mi><mi>l</mi></msup></math> is the encoded local state as defined in Eq. ([4.13](#c04.xhtml#c04-disp-0013)). Thus, we obtain the estimated long‐term cumulative reward of each substrate node. Based on the results, we can select the action with maximum estimated long‐term cumulative reward with the probability of ![1 minus epsilon](images/c04-i0106.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mn>1</mn><mo>&#8722;</mo><mi>&#949;</mi></math> or randomly select an action with the probability of ![epsilon](images/c04-i0107.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>&#949;</mi></mrow></math>. Then, we use the abovementioned *The* ![k](images/c04-i0108.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow></math>*‐shortest paths* for link mapping.

 

#### 4.4.3.3 Short‐term Low‐level Reward

A low‐level reward reveals the real short‐term feedback of an action ![a Subscript t](images/c04-i0109.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>a</mi><mi>t</mi></msub></math>. A positive reward is returned if the selected substrate node hosts the current virtual node successfully and otherwise, a negative one returns. To ensure the availability of SN, LEA should embed every virtual node in a cost‐efficient way and maximize the resource utilization. Therefore, the low‐level reward can therefore be expressed as follows:

(4.15)![r Superscript l Baseline equals StartLayout Enlarged left-brace 1st Row 1st Column 10 times StartFraction upper C o s t Subscript t minus 1 Baseline left-parenthesis upper G Superscript upper V Baseline right-parenthesis Over upper C o s t Subscript t Baseline left-parenthesis upper G Superscript upper V Baseline right-parenthesis EndFraction 2nd Column a Subscript t Baseline is successful 2nd Row 1st Column negative 10 times rho Subscript t Baseline 2nd Column otherwise EndLayout](images/c04-math-0015.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll"><msup><mi>r</mi><mi>l</mi></msup><mo>=</mo><mfenced open="{" close="" separators=""><mrow><mtable columnalign="right left" class="lmatrix"><mtr><mtd><mn>10</mn><mo>&#215;</mo><mfrac><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>C</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>t</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo>&#8722;</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msup><mi>G</mi><mi>V</mi></msup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>C</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>t</mi></mrow><mi>t</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msup><mi>G</mi><mi>V</mi></msup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mfrac></mtd><mtd><msub><mi>a</mi><mi>t</mi></msub><mspace width="0.3em"/><mtext>is successful</mtext></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd><mspace width="3.5em"/><mo>&#8722;</mo><mn>10</mn><mo>&#215;</mo><msub><mi>&#961;</mi><mi>t</mi></msub></mtd><mtd><mtext>otherwise</mtext></mtd></mtr></mtable></mrow></mfenced></math> 

where ![rho Subscript t](images/c04-i0110.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>&#961;</mi><mi>t</mi></msub></math> is a discount factor that gradually rises from ![StartFraction 1 Over upper S i z e left-parenthesis upper N Superscript upper V Baseline right-parenthesis EndFraction](images/c04-i0111.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mfrac><mn>1</mn><mrow><mi>S</mi><mi>i</mi><mi>z</mi><mi>e</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msup><mi>N</mi><mi>V</mi></msup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mfrac></math> to 1. It means that virtual nodes that are embedded at the end are more important, and the failure of embedding the last few nodes will result in greater losses.

 

### 4.4.4 The Training Method

In this work, the neural network with a set of trainable parameters is utilized for feature extraction and policy generation. To get the optimal policies, we should optimize the neural network iteratively. Here, to decrease the possibility of divergence or oscillations during the training process, we adopt a dual policy learning method, called DDQN (Van Hasselt et al., [2016](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0036)), which updates parameters by gradient descent in an efficient way.

 

## 4.5 Case Study

 

### 4.5.1 Experiment Setup

To verify the effectiveness of the proposed model, we set up a simulated environment as most previous works (Yao et al., [2018a](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0042),[2020](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0044), Yan et al., [2020b](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0041)) have done. An SN of 100 nodes and about 500 links are generated, which can represent a medium‐sized ISP. The CPU of the substrate node satisfies the uniform distribution from 50 to 100 units, while the bandwidth of links satisfies the uniform distribution from 20 to 50 units. The expected arrival rate is 4 VNRs per 100 time units. Each VNR has an exponentially distributed lifetime with an average of 500 time units. Following many previous works (Yao et al., [2020](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0044), Yan et al., [2020b](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0041)), every VNR has 2–10 nodes and each pair of virtual nodes has a 50% chance to form a link. The initial virtual node and link resources in a VNR are uniformly distributed from 0 to 40 units. A time window includes 500 time units and about 20 VNRs, which is suitable for the agent to process without considerable consumption of computing resources and achieve proactive performance. Besides, the lengths of the time window and the request queue are set to 25 and 10, respectively. In the testing phase, we build a timeline with a length of 50 000 time units, which means that every episode lasts 50,000 time units, and about 2000 VNRs are generated.

 

### 4.5.2 Comparison Methods

In the evaluation, the VNE‐HRL algorithm will be compared with four selected algorithms including NodeRank (Cheng et al., [2011](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0007)), MCViNE (Haeri and Trajković, [2017](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0020)), and seq2seqRL (Yao et al., [2020](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0044)). NodeRank is a traditional algorithm, which was developed based on mixed‐integer programs and PageRank (Page et al., [1999](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0027)). In addition, MCViNE and seq2seqRL are two representative RL‐based algorithms, which are implemented through Monte‐Carlo Tree Search and Recurrent Neural Networks, respectively. We utilize the public VNE simulator VNE‐Sim (Haeri and Trajković, [2016](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0019)) to collect the performance of these algorithms.

 

### 4.5.3 Evaluation Results

In the testing phase, the VNRs with maximum long‐term revenue are selected from a window‐based batch by HEA and embedded by LEA, which makes the method proactive. To assess the performance of the algorithm, the comparison experiments with other algorithms are conducted in terms of the evaluation metrics presented in [Section 4.3.1.3](#c04.xhtml#c04-sec-0008). Besides, to avoid interference caused by random initialization of neural network parameters, the seq2seqRL and VNE‐HRL algorithms need to run for 15 different initialization settings, and the error bar is added to represent the performance under different initialization settings.

 

#### 4.5.3.1 Performance Over Time

For the first group of tests, we evaluate the performance of four algorithms over time. As we can see from [Figure 4.4](#c04.xhtml#c04-fig-0004), the long‐term average revenue and acceptance ratio are relatively high in early epochs, because there are more available resources in the SN at the beginning of the embedding. As the testing process continues, more VNRs are embedded and the available substrate resources are gradually occupied, which leads to the decrease in the long‐term average revenue and acceptance ratio. However, VNE‐HRL algorithm keeps leading, which can accurately identify the long‐term benefits of the current VNR, embed the VNR with a low cost, and leave plenty of room for the coming VNRs. The long‐term revenue to cost ratio has not experienced significant variations throughout the simulation time, which shows that this metric is independent of the available resources. At last, as shown in [Figures 4.4](#c04.xhtml#c04-fig-0004)a and [4.4](#c04.xhtml#c04-fig-0004)c, VNE‐HRL algorithm outperforms NodeRank, MCViNE, and seq2seqRL with a gap of 26.78%, 21.42%, and 14.69% on average revenue and 6.62%, 5.19%, and 3.73% on average acceptance ratio. Significantly, there is a big gap between the traditional algorithms (i.e. NodeRank) and the other three methods (i.e. MCViNE, seq2seqRL, and VNE‐HRL) in [Figure 4.4](#c04.xhtml#c04-fig-0004)b, which indicates that RL agents perform well in the event of automatically exploring the embedding strategy with a low cost. According to the metric figures and error bars, we can draw a conclusion that the VNE‐HRL algorithm has maintained a relatively better performance over the long term due to proactive planning.

![Figure 4.4 The algorithm performance over time on the test dataset. (a) The long‐term average revenue, (b) The long‐term average Rev2Cost, and (c) The long‐term average acceptance ratio.](images/c04f004.png)

[**Figure 4.4**](#c04.xhtml#R_c04-fig-0004) The algorithm performance over time on the test dataset. (a) The long‐term average revenue, (b) The long‐term average Rev2Cost, and (c) The long‐term average acceptance ratio.

![Figure 4.5 The test results of different upper bounds of resource requests of VNR nodes. (a) The long‐term average acceptance ratio and (b) The long‐term average revenue.](images/c04f005.png)

[**Figure 4.5**](#c04.xhtml#R_c04-fig-0005) The test results of different upper bounds of resource requests of VNR nodes. (a) The long‐term average acceptance ratio and (b) The long‐term average revenue.

 

#### 4.5.3.2 Performance of Various VNRs with Diverse Resource Requirements

For the second group of tests, we observe the performance of these four algorithms under various resource request modes. Due to the different resource requirements, the VNRs can be divided into two types: computation‐intensive and communication‐intensive. To simulate these cases, the node resource request distribution varies from the ![left-bracket 0 comma 40 right-bracket](images/c04-i0112.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mo stretchy="false">\[</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>40</mn><mo stretchy="false">\]</mo></math> uniform distribution to a ![left-bracket 0 comma 100 right-bracket](images/c04-i0113.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mo stretchy="false">\[</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>100</mn><mo stretchy="false">\]</mo></math> uniform distribution, and a higher upper bound denotes a more intensive computation mode. In each step, we raise the upper bound by 10. According to the results shown in [Figure 4.5](#c04.xhtml#c04-fig-0005), the VNE‐HRL algorithm outperforms NodeRank, MCViNE, and seq2seqRL with an average of 43.84%, 33.48%, and 29.58% on average revenue, and 14.84%, 15.91%, and 4.47% on acceptance ratio. In addition, as the resource request of VNRs increases, the acceptance ratio of each algorithm drops. This is because the SN components gradually become harder to host virtual networks and a VNR with large resource requests is likely to fail in the steps of embedding. Besides, the average revenue of these algorithms rises first before going down. This is because a VNR with large resource requests can bring a higher revenue. However, as the resource demands of VNRs become bigger, the average revenue descends due to the quick drop in the acceptance ratio of these VNRs. Through comparison, we can conclude that the VNE‐HRL method again outperforms the other algorithms in a wide range of different resource request distributions.

 

## 4.6 Related Work

Research on VNE problems has drawn tremendous attention. In this section, we will introduce the traditional methods ([Section 4.6.1](#c04.xhtml#c04-sec-0030)) and the ML‐based algorithms ([Section 4.6.2](#c04.xhtml#c04-sec-0031)).

 

### 4.6.1 Traditional Methods

Due to the differences in optimization strategies, traditional VNE algorithms are usually classified into two categories, the exact and the heuristic.

As for the exact algorithms, each VNR is embedded by an exact optimization strategy (Wolsey, [1998](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0038)), such as ILP (Schrijver, [1998](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0028)) and MILP (Bénichou et al., [1971](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0004)). Shahriar et al. ([2018](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0029)) transformed VNE into an ILP problem, which jointly optimized spare capacity location and request survivability. Although an auxiliary algorithm was proposed to optimize the execution time, it was still unacceptable to embed a VNR due to the high‐computation complexity of the node‐mapping algorithm. Chowdhury et al. ([2011](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0013)) formulated the VNR problem as an MILP through SN augmentation, which relaxed the integer constraints to obtain a linear program and devised two embedding algorithms. However, this approach suffered from the requirement of considerable computational resources for a linear programming solution. To sum up, the exact algorithms aim at achieving an optimal embedding solution yet with high‐computational complexity, which only can be used to solve small‐sized VNE problems.

The heuristic methods manage to find a feasible VNR embedding solution with less execution time (Cormen et al., [2009](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0014)). Inspired by the Pagerank algorithm (Page et al., [1999](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0027), Cheng et al., [2011](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0007)) ranked the network node based on its resource and topological attributes and mapped virtual nodes to substrate nodes according to their ranks. However, due to the fixed node ranking, the embedding policy was hard to be optimized. The authors in Haeri and Trajković ([2017](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0020)) proposed a multicommodity flow and shortest‐path approach to embedding VNRs into SNs to maximize the profit of the infrastructure provider. However, they did not consider the future resource demand and historical information, leading to the restriction of profit maximization up to a certain extent. To sum up, the heuristic methods usually suffer from the issue of local optimum that may be far away from the global optimum.

 

### 4.6.2 ML‐based Algorithms

Recently, several research works explored the utilization of ML in VNE problems. Xiao et al. ([2019](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0039)) introduced a Markov decision process (MDP) model to capture the dynamic network state and automatically deployed service function chains for requests. However, this work did not take the admission control into account, which wasted many resources on infeasible VNRs and disregarded the long‐term effects of a VNR. Based on Hopfield neural network, He et al. ([2021](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0021)) established an energy‐efficient virtual network embedding model to provide a candidate set for virtual network embedding. In Alsarhan et al. ([2017](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0001)), an RL‐based framework automatically acclimatized itself to the system changes, such as service cost, resource capability, and resource requests. However, it only aims to meet the demand of service‐level agreement (SLA) but treat all VNRs equally. Yao et al. ([2018b](#c04.xhtml#c04-bib-0043)) applied the policy‐based RL with historical request data to the node mapping, of which the overall objective is to maximize the revenue by embedding nodes and links with a convolutional neural network. To sum up, most existing ML‐based approaches focus on the current VNR and treat all VNRs equally, which waste resources when the infeasible VNRs present and disregard the long‐term impacts of the embedding.

 

## 4.7 Conclusion

In this chapter, we proposed a proactive VNE algorithm relying on HRL, which takes the long‐term impact of VNR embedding into consideration. Within the VNE‐HRL framework, the task of embedding a sequence of VNRs has been performed by a two‐level agent. For each process, the high‐level agent aims to select a currently feasible VNR with maximum long‐term reward, and the low‐level agent is designed to embed the selected VNR on the substrate network. Extensive simulation results have shown that the VNE‐HRL algorithm possesses good effectiveness and robustness when the type of VNR changes.

# 5  
Concept Drift Detection for Network Traffic Classification

 

## 5.1 Related Concepts of Machine Learning in Data Stream Processing

 

### 5.1.1 Assumptions and Limitations

With the rapid development of network and information technologies, traffic classification has received significant attention and plays an important role in intrusion detection (Roshan et al., [2017](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0016), Zhang et al., [2021](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0022), Wang et al., [2021](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0020)), flow identification (Zou et al., [2018](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0023), Lotfollahi et al., [2017](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0013)), just to name a few. However, standard machine‐learning frameworks, in which data are processed in an offline way, become limited in network traffic processing due to exponential increase of the available data. In order to deal with the large amounts of data, more studies started to research on the processing of network data as a stream and used machine‐learning methods to classify the traffic online. In terms of machine learning, several types of assumptions and constraints can be identified which are related to the data and to the type of concept to be learned. In the remainder of this chapter, ![x](images/c05-i0001.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>x</mi></mrow></math>, ![y](images/c05-i0002.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>y</mi></mrow></math>, and ![upper P left-parenthesis x comma y right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0003.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>P</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>y</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math> denote the given input feature, the given label, and the concept (i.e. the data distribution), respectively (Lemaire et al., [2015](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0012)).

 

#### 5.1.1.1 Availability of Learning Examples

In the context of this chapter, models are learned from representative examples coming from a classification problem. In practice the availability and the access to these examples can vary, e.g. all examples have been stored in a database, all examples are stored in memory, examples are partially stored in memory, examples are available one by one in a stream. Several types of algorithms for different types of availability of the examples have been reported in the literature (Lemaire et al., [2015](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0012)). However, in some fields such as network traffic classification, data are continuously available from a data stream and, over time, the amount of data will become too large to be stored. According to the “data streams” paradigm, the representative examples can be seen only once and in their order of arrival (without storage) (Lemaire et al., [2015](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0012)). In this case, the learning algorithm must be incremental and needs to have an efficient processing capability due to the potentially high‐data arrival rate from data stream.

 

#### 5.1.1.2 Availability of the Model

Data mining is a two‐step process: (1) train and learn the model, and (2) deploy the model to predict on the new data. In the case of regular batch learning, these two steps are carried out one after the other. But in the case of an incremental algorithm, the training and learning stages are triggered as soon as a new example arrives. In the cases of time‐critical applications, the training and learning steps require a low‐latency algorithm (i.e. with a low‐time complexity).

 

#### 5.1.1.3 Concept to be Learned

The concept to be learned is the joint probability ![upper P left-parenthesis x comma y right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0004.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>P</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>y</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math>, where ![x](images/c05-i0005.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>x</mi></mrow></math> and ![y](images/c05-i0006.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>y</mi></mrow></math> is the given input feature and the given label, respectively. Traditional traffic classification is carried out by learning an underlying data distribution ![upper P left-parenthesis x comma y right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0007.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>P</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>y</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math> from a static data set. However, in real network environment, the concept, i.e. the joint probability, of traffic always changes because of the changing user interests and time‐varying network status, e.g. network congestion. In this chapter, we mainly consider the problem of concept drift in network traffic classification, where the labeling of network traffic is significantly hindered by its nature of streaming applications.

 

### 5.1.2 Concept Drift and Its Solution

The change of concept is called “concept drift.” Although network traffic classification algorithms based on machine learning can alleviate the limitations imposed by traditional techniques, most of them still cannot address concept drift problems (Qu et al., [2019](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0015)), which will bring a devastating blow to the performance of the classifier. For example, in the intrusion detection field, the F1‐score of Artificial Neural Networks (ANN) will drop from 99.24% to 9.59% when the distribution of traffic (i.e. concept) changes (Al‐Riyami et al., [2018](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0002)). Therefore, in order to perform online traffic classification, it is of paramount importance to give in‐depth research on concept drift. According to the changing speed of drift, concept drift can be either abrupt, when the concept is changed suddenly, or gradual, when the concept evolves slowly over time (Wang et al., [2018](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0019)). In addition, based on the definition of “concept,” i.e. ![upper P left-parenthesis x comma y right-parenthesis equals upper P left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis asterisk upper P left-parenthesis y vertical-bar x right-parenthesis equals upper P left-parenthesis y right-parenthesis asterisk upper P left-parenthesis x vertical-bar y right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0008.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>P</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>y</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>=</mo><mi>P</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>&#42;</mo><mi>P</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>y</mi><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mi>x</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>=</mo><mi>P</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>y</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>&#42;</mo><mi>P</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mi>y</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math>, concept drift can manifest four fundamental forms of changes corresponding to the four major variables in Bayes' theorem (Wang et al., [2018](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0019)). In order to solve the problem of classifier's performance degradation caused by concept drift, two types of solutions have been proposed: passive versus active approaches, according to whether an explicit

![Figure 5.1 Main processes of the active approach‐addressing concept drift.](images/c05f001.png)

[**Figure 5.1**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-fig-0001) Main processes of the active approach‐addressing concept drift.

drift detection mechanism is employed (Wang et al., [2018](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0019), Singh et al., [2015](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0017)). The passive approach (also known as the adaptive classifier) refers to the continuous training of the classifier without an explicit trigger reporting the drift. The active approach, whose main processes are shown in [Figure 5.1](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0001), evolves the classifier after determining whether the concept drift occurs. Compared with the passive approach, the active approach can effectively reduce the times of classifier retraining, which is useful under the condition of limited computing resources (Charyyev and Gunes, [2020](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0003)). As the core component of active approaches, concept drift detection has become the main research topic (Wang et al., [2018](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0019), Hu et al., [2020](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0009)). Existing methods of detecting concept drift assume one among three situations according to the availability of data labels (Hu et al., [2020](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0009)), including full access, no access, and limited access to labels of the data streams. They correspond to the supervised, unsupervised, and semisupervised learning approaches, respectively. The semisupervised learning mechanism is a compromise between the label cost and the detection rate. Although supervised detector can detect all types of drift, it is unrealistic to label every network traffic due to its streaming nature. Therefore, in real network environment, a sensible semisupervised concept drift detection scheme is more practical and efficient. In order to obtain the drift indicator, the current semisupervised concept drift detection scheme mainly needs to measure and evaluate the confidence score (Kim and Park, [2017](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0011), Sriwatanasakdi et al., [2017](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0018), Haque et al., [2016a](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0007),[b](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0008)) or the accuracy score (Lughofer et al., [2016](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0014)) of the classifier. However, approaches based on the confidence score (Kim and Park, [2017](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0011), Sriwatanasakdi et al., [2017](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0018), Haque et al., [2016a](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0007),[b](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0008)) only focus on the change of decision boundaries, namely ![upper P left-parenthesis y vertical-bar x right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0009.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>P</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>y</mi><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mi>x</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math>. Therefore, only a small subset of all concept drift types can be detected (Hu et al., [2020](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0009)). Although the indicator based on the accuracy score can no longer detect changes in decision boundaries, the drift analysis method proposed in Lughofer et al. ([2016](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0014)) assumed that the previously obtained indicators follow the Gaussian distribution, which is not always true. In order to efficiently detect both sudden drifts and gradual drifts, Zhang et al. ([2020](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0021)) proposed an ensemble drift analysis method. However, true drifts need to be labeled in this method, which is very difficult to achieve in real‐world environment. In order to efficiently complete concept drift detection under the semisupervised learning, we propose a concept drift detector based on CVAE, which is elaborated in [Section 5.3](#c05.xhtml#c05-sec-0011).

 

## 5.2 Using an Active Approach to Solve Concept Drift in the Intrusion Detection Field

In order to show the process of concept drift detection more intuitively, we use an example in intrusion detection to illustrate.

 

### 5.2.1 Application Background

The explosive growth of computer systems and networks has led to a heightened importance to protect them from attacks or intrusions. As one of the important network security technologies, Intrusion Detection System (IDS) continuously collects different kinds of data about the operating system calls and the network traffic which may show abnormalities caused by malicious attacks or policy violations. In order to overcome disadvantages of traditional IDS, such as time consumption and insensitiveness to emerging types of intrusions, more researchers have made their efforts to utilize machine learning and data mining techniques to automatically detect and classify the abnormalities. However, the real‐world network is a nonstationary environment, in which the characteristics of continuous data stream is time varying and, more importantly, various new types of networks have been emerging endlessly. In this case, it is necessary to retrain the classifier when it does not match the current concept, i.e. the concept drift occurs. In this section, we use two real‐world datasets in the intrusion detection field, i.e. Gure–KDD and NSL–KDD, to simulate the concept drift. NSL–KDD is an improved version of KDD99, which is the data set used for The Third International Knowledge Discovery and Data Mining Tools Competition to build a network intrusion detector. Both NSL–KDD and Gure–KDD contain 5 attacks and 41‐dimensional input features. In this scenario, we use the full training set of NSL–KDD to train the classifier. The testing set is composed of the testing set of NSL–KDD and the full set of Gure–KDD, with the proportion of 1:7. Once the concept drift is detected, we use 5000 labeled data to train the classifier online.

 

### 5.2.2 System Workflow

As shown in [Figure 5.1](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0001), after classifying the unlabeled stream ![x](images/c05-i0010.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>x</mi></mrow></math>, the detector takes ![x](images/c05-i0011.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>x</mi></mrow></math> as input to determine whether concept drift occurs or not. The detailed operation process of the semisupervised detector is shown in [Figure 5.2](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0002). For online traffic classification, it is unrealistic for experts to continuously perform timely and reliable labeling of data. In practice, as shown in [Figure 5.2](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0002), only a small number of samples selected by the **Active Learning** method can be labeled by, e.g. Oracle (Lughofer et al., [2016](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0014)). Oracle can process the selected input feature ![bold upper X](images/c05-i0012.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">X</mi></mrow></math> and obtain their true labels ![bold upper Y](images/c05-i0013.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">Y</mi></mrow></math>, and then it uses the ![left-bracket bold upper X comma bold upper Y right-bracket](images/c05-i0014.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mfenced open="\[" close="\]" separators=""><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">X</mi><mo>,</mo><mi mathvariant="bold">Y</mi></mrow></mfenced></math> as the input of the **Drift Indicator** to calculate the current value of loss function as the indicator. The **Drift Analyzer** then analyzes the loss and generates a signal to determine whether the concept drift occurs. Since the innovations of this research are mainly reflected in the Drift Indicator and Drift Analyzer, in what follows we will mainly introduce these two parts. In the rest of this chapter, random sampling with sampling rate ![r](images/c05-i0015.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>r</mi></mrow></math> is used in the Active Learning method. We use the most intuitive value, that is, the accuracy of the classifier as the indicator of the detector. Then, setting the sampling rate ![r equals 1](images/c05-i0016.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>r</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></math> and using the method in Gama et al. ([2004](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0006)) as the analyzer, when the accuracy of the classifier drops beyond a certain threshold, a drift signal is sent. After receiving the drift signal, the classifier will be retrained. The result is shown in [Figure 5.3](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0003). As we can see from the figure, if we do not deal with the problem of concept drift, the accuracy of classifier will drop sharply. However, if we use drift detector and retrain the classifier whenever the drift occurs, the classifier will have better performance. In this way, the problem of concept drift can be basically solved. Next, we will describe how to achieve a better performance of concept drift detectors with less sampling rate based on CVAE under the semisupervised learning.

![Figure 5.2 Overview of the semisupervised methodology.](images/c05f002.png)

[**Figure 5.2**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-fig-0002) Overview of the semisupervised methodology.

![Figure 5.3 Results of the active approach to solve concept drift.](images/c05f003.png)

[**Figure 5.3**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-fig-0003) Results of the active approach to solve concept drift.

 

## 5.3 Concept Drift Detector Based on CVAE

According to [Section 5.2](#c05.xhtml#c05-sec-0008), there are two main components in concept drift detector, i.e. **Drift Indicator** and **Drift Analyzer**. In the following, we will carefully introduce how to complete these two parts based on CVAE.

![Figure 5.4 The structure of CVAE.](images/c05f004.png)

[**Figure 5.4**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-fig-0004) The structure of CVAE.

 

### 5.3.1 CVAE‐based Drift Indicator

As a generative neural network, variational autoencoder (VAE) is able to model the distribution of observed data, i.e. ![upper P left-parenthesis bold upper X right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0017.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>P</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi mathvariant="bold">X</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math>. CVAE extends VAE by taking labels into consideration, aiming to model the relationship between features and labels, which means that the loss value of CVAE is highly related to the current concept, i.e. ![upper P left-parenthesis bold upper X bold comma bold upper Y right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0018.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>P</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi mathvariant="bold">X</mi><mo mathvariant="bold">,</mo><mi mathvariant="bold">Y</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math>.

The structure of CVAE is shown in [Figure 5.4](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0004). It can be seen that the CVAE is composed of an encoder and a decoder. The encoder takes ![left-bracket bold upper X comma bold upper Y right-bracket](images/c05-i0019.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mfenced open="\[" close="\]" separators=""><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">X</mi><mo>,</mo><mi mathvariant="bold">Y</mi></mrow></mfenced></math> as input to generate the variable ![bold z](images/c05-i0020.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi></mrow></math>. Formally, the goal of encoder is to estimate the parameters of the latent data distribution ![q left-parenthesis bold z vertical-bar bold upper X comma bold upper Y right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0021.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>q</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mi mathvariant="bold">X</mi><mo>,</mo><mi mathvariant="bold">Y</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math> that approximates the prior distribution of the latent vector ![p left-parenthesis bold z vertical-bar bold upper Y right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0022.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>p</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mi mathvariant="bold">Y</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math>. Taking label ![bold upper Y](images/c05-i0023.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">Y</mi></mrow></math> as input, the decoder then samples ![bold z](images/c05-i0024.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi></mrow></math> from the approximate distribution and attempts to reconstruct the original feature ![bold upper X](images/c05-i0025.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">X</mi></mrow></math> back, i.e. using the decoder output ![f left-parenthesis bold z comma bold upper Y right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0026.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>f</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi><mo>,</mo><mi mathvariant="bold">Y</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math> to approximate the input ![bold upper X](images/c05-i0027.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">X</mi></mrow></math>. Both the encoder and decoder parameters can be trained using a deep learning framework (Agrawal and Ganapathy, [2019](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0001)).

If the input feature ![bold upper X](images/c05-i0028.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">X</mi></mrow></math> is reconstructed from distribution ![p left-parenthesis bold upper X vertical-bar bold z comma bold upper Y right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0029.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>p</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi mathvariant="bold">X</mi><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi><mo>,</mo><mi mathvariant="bold">Y</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math>, the target function of CVAE can be written by Eq. ([5.1](#c05.xhtml#c05-disp-0001)), where ![upper D left-parenthesis period right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0030.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>D</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mo>.</mo><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math> is Kullback–Leibler Divergence.

(5.1)![upper L equals upper D left-parenthesis q left-parenthesis bold z vertical-bar bold upper X comma bold upper Y right-parenthesis vertical-bar vertical-bar right-parenthesis of pp left-parenthesis right-parenthesis vertical-bar z vertical-bar upper Y minus upper E Subscript bold z tilde q left-parenthesis bold z vertical-bar bold upper X bold comma bold upper Y right-parenthesis Baseline left-parenthesis log p left-parenthesis bold upper X vertical-bar bold z comma bold upper Y right-parenthesis right-parenthesis](images/c05-math-0001.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll"><mi>L</mi><mo>=</mo><mi>D</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>q</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mi mathvariant="bold">X</mi><mo>,</mo><mi mathvariant="bold">Y</mi><mtext>)</mtext><mfenced open="||" close=")" separators=""><mrow><mi>p</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mi mathvariant="bold">Y</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mfenced><mo>&#8722;</mo><msub><mi>E</mi><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi><mo>&#8764;</mo><mi>q</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mi mathvariant="bold">X</mi><mo mathvariant="bold">,</mo><mi mathvariant="bold">Y</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>log</mi><mi>p</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi mathvariant="bold">X</mi><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi><mo>,</mo><mi mathvariant="bold">Y</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math> 

Generally, the prior distribution of latent vector ![p left-parenthesis bold z vertical-bar bold upper Y right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0031.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>p</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mi mathvariant="bold">Y</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math> is assumed to be a standard normal distribution, and ![q left-parenthesis bold z vertical-bar bold upper X comma bold upper Y right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0032.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>q</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mi mathvariant="bold">X</mi><mo>,</mo><mi mathvariant="bold">Y</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math> is assumed to be a normal distribution with mean ![mu left-parenthesis bold upper X comma bold upper Y right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0033.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>&#956;</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi mathvariant="bold">X</mi><mo>,</mo><mi mathvariant="bold">Y</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math> and variance ![normal upper Sigma left-parenthesis bold upper X comma bold upper Y right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0034.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi mathvariant="normal">&#931;</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi mathvariant="bold">X</mi><mo>,</mo><mi mathvariant="bold">Y</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math> (Doersch, [2016](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0004)). In addition, the second term in Eq. ([5.1](#c05.xhtml#c05-disp-0001)) can be substituted by the negative of mean square error (MSE) (Agrawal and Ganapathy, [2019](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0001)). Finally, the loss function of CVAE for every sample can be rewritten as [Eq. (5.2)](#c05.xhtml#c05-disp-0002).

(5.2)![upper L Subscript upper C Baseline equals upper D left-parenthesis q left-parenthesis bold z vertical-bar bold upper X comma bold upper Y right-parenthesis StartAbsoluteValue EndAbsoluteValue upper N left-parenthesis 0 comma bold upper I right-parenthesis right-parenthesis plus vertical-bar vertical-bar vertical-bar vertical-bar minus minus of ff left-parenthesis right-parenthesis comma z comma YX squared](images/c05-math-0002.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>L</mi><mi>C</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mi>D</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>q</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mi mathvariant="bold">X</mi><mo>,</mo><mi mathvariant="bold">Y</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mi>N</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>,</mo><mi mathvariant="bold">I</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>+</mo><msup><mrow><mfenced open="||" close="||" separators=""><mrow><mi>f</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi><mo>,</mo><mi mathvariant="bold">Y</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>&#8722;</mo><mi mathvariant="bold">X</mi></mrow></mfenced></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup></math> 

The stochastic gradient descent technique can be used to minimize Eq. ([5.2](#c05.xhtml#c05-disp-0002)). After the training process of CVAE, the **CVAE‐based drift indicator** will take the ![k](images/c05-i0035.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow></math>th sample ![left-bracket bold upper X Subscript k Baseline comma bold upper Y Subscript k Baseline right-bracket](images/c05-i0036.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mfenced open="\[" close="\]" separators=""><mrow><msub><mi mathvariant="bold">X</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="bold">Y</mi><mi>k</mi></msub></mrow></mfenced></math>, where ![k equals 1 comma 2 comma 3 comma ellipsis](images/c05-i0037.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>k</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>2</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>3</mn><mo>,</mo><mi>&#8230;</mi></math>, as input and output the value of loss function ![l Subscript k](images/c05-i0038.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>l</mi><mi>k</mi></msub></math> according to [Eq. (5.2)](#c05.xhtml#c05-disp-0002).

 

### 5.3.2 Drift Analyzer

Based on the output value of **CVAE‐based drift indicator**, we can determine whether concept drift happens by using the following theorem.

Suppose **CVAE‐based drift indicator** has output ![upper N](images/c05-i0049.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>N</mi></mrow></math> values since the last concept drift occurs, which is denoted as ![upper L o s s Subscript upper C upper V upper A upper E Baseline equals Start 1 By 4 Matrix 1st Row 1st Column l 1 2nd Column l 2 3rd Column period period period 4th Column l Subscript upper N EndMatrix](images/c05-i0050.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>L</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><msub><mi>s</mi><mrow><mi>C</mi><mi>V</mi><mi>A</mi><mi>E</mi></mrow></msub><mspace width="0.3em"/><mi>&#61;</mi><mfenced open="\[" close="\]" separators=""><mrow><mtable><mtr><mtd class="array"><msub><mi>l</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></mtd><mtd class="array"><msub><mi>l</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></mtd><mtd class="array"><mo>.</mo><mo>.</mo><mo>.</mo></mtd><mtd class="array"><msub><mi>l</mi><mi>N</mi></msub></mtd></mtr></mtable></mrow></mfenced></math>, where ![upper M](images/c05-i0051.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>M</mi></mrow></math> is the size of the window in which we detect whether a concept drift occurs. The mean of loss function value of the concept before the drift happens is ![upper E left-parenthesis upper L o s s Subscript 1 Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0052.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>E</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mrow><mi>L</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>s</mi></mrow><mn>1</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math>, and ![upper E left-parenthesis upper L o s s Subscript 2 Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0053.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>E</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mrow><mi>L</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>s</mi></mrow><mn>2</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math> is the mean value after concept drift occurs. According to [Theorem 5.1](#c05.xhtml#c05-fea-0002), ![upper E left-parenthesis upper L o s s 1 right-parenthesis not-equals upper E left-parenthesis upper L o s s 2 right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0054.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>E</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>L</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><msub><mi>s</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>&#8800;</mo><mi>E</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>L</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>s</mi><msub><mi>s</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math>, it is easy to find that Eq. ([5.6](#c05.xhtml#c05-disp-0006)) holds if a concept drift occurs.

The changed value of current loss function is shown in Eq. ([5.7](#c05.xhtml#c05-disp-0007)).
