If ![delta Subscript upper N Baseline dot delta Subscript upper N minus 1 Baseline less-than 0](images/c05-i0055.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>&#948;</mi><mi>N</mi></msub><mo>&#183;</mo><msub><mi>&#948;</mi><mrow><mi>N</mi><mi>&#8722;</mi><mn>1</mn></mrow></msub><mo>&#60;</mo><mn>0</mn></math>, it means that the data in the ![upper N](images/c05-i0056.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>N</mi></mrow></math>th window and the ![left-parenthesis upper N minus 1 right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0057.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>N</mi><mo>&#8722;</mo><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math>th window no longer belong to the same concept, and the **Drift Analyzer** will generate a drift signal ![s Subscript upper N Baseline equals 1](images/c05-i0058.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>s</mi><mi>N</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></math>; otherwise, ![s Subscript upper N Baseline equals 0](images/c05-i0059.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>s</mi><mi>N</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn></math>. In practice, we can predefine a small value ![m](images/c05-i0060.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>m</mi></mrow></math> to improve robustness of the detection scheme. In other words, when the absolute value of ![delta Subscript upper N](images/c05-i0061.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>&#948;</mi><mi>N</mi></msub></math> is less than ![m](images/c05-i0062.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>m</mi></mrow></math>, the drift signal can be ignored.

 

### 5.3.3 The Performance of CVAE‐based Concept Drift Detector

In this section, artificial datasets are used to verify the performance of the proposed solution. To demonstrate the performance of the proposed CVAE‐based concept drift detector, we compare it with the following three related schemes.

 

#### 5.3.3.1 Comparison Drift Detectors

*   The drift detector proposed in Lughofer et al. ([2016](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0014)) is based on Faded Page–Hinkley test (referred as to PH\_test in the rest of this chapter). Similar with our proposed solution, it has limited access to class labels. The drift indicator of PH\_test is based on the accuracy score of the classifier. However, the drift analysis method, i.e. Faded Page–Hinkley test, assumes that the indicator follows Gaussian distribution, which is not always realistic in many real‐world settings.
*   The drift detection method based on the Hoeffding's Bound (referred as to HDDM) was proposed in Frías‐Blanco et al. ([2015](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0005)). Although the hypothesis of the HDDM has full access to class labels, to make it comparable with our proposed solution, we limit the sample rate and measure the performance of the supervised concept drift detection scheme under limited access of sample labels. We use output of **CVAE‐based drift indicator** as the drift indicator of HDDM.
*   A drift indicator based on Restricted Boltzmann Machine (RBM) was proposed in Jaworski et al. ([2018](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0010)). To compare the indicator based on other generative models such as RBM, we modify the drift analysis method proposed in Jaworski et al. ([2018](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0010)) and incorporate it into our method proposed in [Section 5.3.2](#c05.xhtml#c05-sec-0013).

![Table 5.1  Database description.](images/c05-i0063.png)

[**Table 5.1**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-tbl-0001)  Database description.

 

#### 5.3.3.2 Experiment Settings

In this section, the well‐known artificial data generator, SINE (Wang et al., [2018](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0019)) is adopted. In SINE (Wang et al., [2018](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0019)), each generated point has two features ![left-parenthesis x 1 comma x 2 right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0066.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math> and one label ![y](images/c05-i0067.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>y</mi></mrow></math>. The feature is uniformly distributed in \[0, 1\]. Starting from the first data, a concept drift occurs every 100 data. A total of 99 drifts are generated in the artificial dataset. To measure the performance of the proposed solution in the context of gradual drift in the concept, we generate the SINEg dataset. The details of the generated datasets are provided in [Table 5.1](#c05.xhtml#c05-tbl-0001). In this section, the hidden layer of CVAE consists of 10 neurons and the dimension of ![bold z](images/c05-i0068.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi></mrow></math> is 2. To improve the robustness of the proposed method, we set the predefined value ![m equals 0.25](images/c05-i0069.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>m</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.25</mn></math>. In addition, the windows size ![upper M](images/c05-i0070.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>M</mi></mrow></math> is set to 10. The following widely used performance metrics are considered to evaluate our proposed solution (Wang et al., [2018](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0019)):

*   True Detection Rate (TDR): It is the probability of detecting the true concept drift, as shown in Eq. ([5.8](#c05.xhtml#c05-disp-0008)), where ![upper N Subscript p](images/c05-i0071.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>N</mi><mi>p</mi></msub></math> is the number of true concept drift, and ![upper D Subscript p](images/c05-i0072.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>D</mi><mi>p</mi></msub></math> is the number of detected true drift. In this section, we define that the drift is detected as ![there-exists upper C Subscript e n d Baseline less-than i less-than-or-equal-to upper C prime Subscript e n d Baseline comma s Subscript i Baseline equals 1](images/c05-i0073.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mo>&#8707;</mo><msub><mi>C</mi><mrow><mi>e</mi><mi>n</mi><mi>d</mi></mrow></msub><mo>&#60;</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>&#8804;</mo><msub><mrow><msup><mi>C</mi><mo>&#8242;</mo></msup></mrow><mrow><mi>e</mi><mi>n</mi><mi>d</mi></mrow></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>s</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></math>, where ![upper C Subscript e n d](images/c05-i0074.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>C</mi><mrow><mi>e</mi><mi>n</mi><mi>d</mi></mrow></msub></math> and ![upper C prime Subscript e n d](images/c05-i0075.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mrow><msup><mi>C</mi><mo>&#8242;</mo></msup></mrow><mrow><mi>e</mi><mi>n</mi><mi>d</mi></mrow></msub></math> are the end index of the last concept and the current concept, respectively.
*   False Alarm Rate (FAR): It is the probability of reporting a concept drift that does not exist, as shown in Eq. ([5.9](#c05.xhtml#c05-disp-0009)), where ![upper N Subscript s](images/c05-i0076.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>N</mi><mi>s</mi></msub></math> is the number of samples, and ![upper R Subscript upper F](images/c05-i0077.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>R</mi><mi>F</mi></msub></math> is the number of false alarms. Drift signal ![s Subscript j Baseline equals 1](images/c05-i0078.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>s</mi><mi>j</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></math> is the false alarm if the corresponding drift does not exist or is repeatedly alarmed. (5.8)![upper T upper D upper R equals StartFraction upper D Subscript p Baseline Over upper N Subscript p Baseline EndFraction](images/c05-math-0008.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll"><mi>T</mi><mi>D</mi><mi>R</mi><mi>&#61;</mi><mspace width="0.3em"/><mfrac><mrow><msub><mi>D</mi><mi>p</mi></msub></mrow><mrow><msub><mi>N</mi><mi>p</mi></msub></mrow></mfrac></math> (5.9)![upper F upper A upper R equals StartFraction upper R Subscript upper F Baseline Over upper N Subscript s Baseline EndFraction](images/c05-math-0009.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll"><mi>F</mi><mi>A</mi><mi>R</mi><mi>&#61;</mi><mspace width="0.3em"/><mfrac><mrow><msub><mi>R</mi><mi>F</mi></msub></mrow><mrow><msub><mi>N</mi><mi>s</mi></msub></mrow></mfrac></math> 

We use logistic regression implemented by Scipy to classify the data and sample the data stream ![upper S](images/c05-i0079.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>S</mi></mrow></math> at a certain sampling rate ![r](images/c05-i0080.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>r</mi></mrow></math>, where ![0.2 less-than-or-equal-to r less-than-or-equal-to 1](images/c05-i0081.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mn>0.2</mn><mo>&#8804;</mo><mi>r</mi><mo>&#8804;</mo><mn>1</mn></math>, while detecting the drift in the data stream. For example, the Oracle only labels 20% of the data when the sampling rate is 0.2. [Figure 5.5](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0005) depicts the results of TDR and FAR obtained from our proposed solution (denoted by CVAE) and the three related schemes, using the two artificial datasets ![upper S upper I upper N upper E](images/c05-i0082.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>S</mi><mi>I</mi><mi>N</mi><mi>E</mi></math> and ![upper S upper I upper N upper E g](images/c05-i0083.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>S</mi><mi>I</mi><mi>N</mi><mi>E</mi><mi>g</mi></math> (see [Table 5.1](#c05.xhtml#c05-tbl-0001)). It can be seen from [Figure 5.5](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0005)(a) and (b), when the data sampling rate is low (such as 0.2), the TDR of the RBM and PH\_test are much lower than the drift detector based on CVAE. The main reason for this phenomenon is that the drift indicator based on the loss value of CVAE is more sensitive to the changes of concept. As shown in [Figure 5.5](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0005), detector based on CVAE can find all drifts with only 20% labels.

In addition, as shown in [Figure 5.5](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0005)(c) and (d), the FAR of CVAE is much lower than HDDM and PH\_test, because the proposed drift analyzer uses a predefined value ![m](images/c05-i0084.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>m</mi></mrow></math> to improve the robustness of CVAE‐based detector, i.e. it will ignore the drift signal when the change of indicator is less than ![m](images/c05-i0085.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>m</mi></mrow></math>.

![Figure 5.5 TDR and FAR in two datasets. (a) TDR in SINE, (b) TDR in SINE\_g, (c)FAR in SINE, and (d) FAR in SINE\_g](images/c05f005.png)

[**Figure 5.5**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-fig-0005) TDR and FAR in two datasets. (a) TDR in SINE, (b) TDR in SINE\_g, (c)FAR in SINE, and (d) FAR in SINE\_g

 

## 5.4 Deployment and Experiment in Real Networks

In this section, we systematically describe the deployment methods of data collection, feature extraction, and other steps in the field of network flow recognition and the results of concept drift detection.

 

### 5.4.1 Data Collection and Feature Extraction

In order to collect the network traffic data, we deploy the network topology shown in [Figure 5.6](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0006). As show in the figure, we generate network traffic in our server. In order to generate live stream traffic, we use streamlink to download live stream on Huajiao ![Figure 5.6 The network topology in experiments.](images/c05f006.png)

[**Figure 5.6**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-fig-0006) The network topology in experiments.

[**Table 5.2**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-tbl-0002)  Feature selection.

| Feature || Description |
| --- | --- |
| pkt\_len\_mean || Mean size of packet |
| --- | --- |
| fwd\_pkt\_len\_mean || Mean size of packet in forward |
| bwd\_pkt\_len\_mean || Mean size of packet in backward |
| flow\_rate || The average rate |
| fwd\_flow\_rate || The average rate in forward |
| bwd\_flow\_rate || The average rate in backward |
| flow\_pit || Average of packet interval time |
| fwd\_flow\_pit || Average of packet interval time in forward |
| bwd\_flow\_pit || Average of packet interval time in backward |

 

### 5.4.2 Data Analysis and Parameter Setting

The traffic collected between 9 a.m. and 9 p.m. is the dataset for working hours (“working dataset” for short) and the traffic collected between 9 p.m. and 9 a.m. is the dataset for after work (“relaxing dataset” for short). In addition, we further split the working dataset into two sub‐datasets: the training dataset (“working‐train”) and the testing dataset (“working‐test”) with the proportion of 3:1, respectively. We use the Jensen–Shannon divergence to measure the difference of the three datasets (working‐train, working‐test, and relaxing dataset). The maximum value of the distribution gap between working‐train and working‐test is 0.0368, and the maximum value of the distribution gap between working‐train and relaxing dataset is 0.4659, which means that there is a concept drift between working‐train dataset and relaxing dataset.

In this scenario, we use working‐train as the training dataset. The working‐test, along with the relaxing dataset, is used as the testing dataset. Once the concept drift is detected, we use 2500 labeled data to train the classifier online. The hidden layer of CVAE consists of 20 neurons, and the dimension of ![bold z](images/c05-i0086.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold">z</mi></mrow></math> is 5. In addition, the windows size is ![upper M equals 20](images/c05-i0087.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>M</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>20</mn></math>, and we set the predefined value ![m](images/c05-i0088.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>m</mi></mrow></math> to 1.

 

### 5.4.3 Result Analysis

Since there are currently no real‐world datasets specifically used in the concept drift detection field, to test on the real‐world datasets, the performance of the concept drift detection scheme can be indirectly affected by the accuracy of the classifier. In other words, a good concept drift detection scheme should promptly find the concept drift and let the classifier training on the data to improve the accuracy of the classifier.

We use the adaptive classifier (referred as to Online Learner in the following) as the upper limit because they directly train the data model online without concept drift detection and can obtain the most information. Further, we use the offline classifier (referred as to NoChange in the following) as the lower limit because the classifier does not perform any online training. In addition to the three comparison drift detectors illustrated in [Section 5.3.3.1](#c05.xhtml#c05-sec-0016), to fully utilize the advantages of our solution in concept drift detection, in this section, we also compare it with the fully supervised Drift Detection Method proposed in Gama et al. ([2004](#c05.xhtml#c05-bib-0006)) (referred as to DDM in the following).

In this section, we set the sampling rate of semisupervised schemes (including CVAE‐based detector, PH‐test, HDDM, and RBM) to 0.2, and the sampling rate of DDM and Onliner Learner is set to 1. The performance of the classifier combined with different detectors, including our proposed detector CVAE and the other four related detectors (i.e. PH\_test, DDM, HDDM, and RBM), are shown in [Figure 5.7](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0007). The blue vertical line is the change point of the dataset. All detectors can find one drift in this real‐world dataset. However, according to [Figure 5.7](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0007), it can be seen that the classifier combined with the proposed CVAE‐based detector has a higher accuracy rate than the classifier combined with other detectors, and the drift can be detected earlier by our proposed CVAE‐based detector, with even smaller sampling rate. Therefore, we can find that the concept drift discovered by CVAE‐based detectors has higher training value.

![Figure 5.7 The improvement of various schemes of the classifier on real‐world dataset.](images/c05f007.png)

[**Figure 5.7**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-fig-0007) The improvement of various schemes of the classifier on real‐world dataset.

 

## 5.5 Future Research Challenges and Open Issues

 

### 5.5.1 Adaptive Threshold ![m](images/c05-i0089.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>m</mi></mrow></math>

In this chapter, we propose a drift detection method based on the loss of CVAE. As shown in Section [5.3.2](#c05.xhtml#c05-sec-0013), we introduce predefined threshold ![m](images/c05-i0090.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>m</mi></mrow></math> to improve robustness of the detection scheme. However, in fact, the value of ![m](images/c05-i0091.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>m</mi></mrow></math> will significantly affect the TDR and FAR of the drift detector. Thus, it is important to adaptively correct the value of ![m](images/c05-i0092.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>m</mi></mrow></math> according to the changing characteristics of the program running time and CVAE.

 

### 5.5.2 Computational Cost of Drift Detectors

Although, with fewer labeling cost, the TDR and FAR of the drift detector based on the CVAE are much better than the detector based on the accuracy rate (i.e. HDDM and DDM), it also requires greater computational and resource overhead. The larger number of hidden nodes in CVAE, the greater computational and resource overhead is required for the training and testing processes. Therefore, it is very important to optimize the calculation and resource management process from the aspects of architecture and model.

 

### 5.5.3 Active Learning

The concept drift detection scheme based on semisupervised hypothesis needs to seek a compromise between concept detection performance and labeling cost. Therefore, in order to be able to find the data with most labeling value, active learning, which assumes that there is a large amount of unlabeled data but only a fraction of them can be labeled by human efforts, needs to be more carefully designed so that concept drift can be found with as little labeled data as possible.

 

## 5.6 Conclusion

In this chapter, we briefly introduced the concepts of data stream processing and concept drift. In addition, we implemented a concept drift detector based on CVAE. Through experiments, we can prove that our concept drift detector is more efficient. However, because the traditional CVAE can only be trained offline and does not have the ability to train online, it is difficult to adapt to the current concept in real time. Our solution still has room for improvement.

## Note

1.  [1](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-note-0001) [https://www.huajiao.com/](https://www.huajiao.com)

# 6  
Online Encrypted Traffic Classification Based on Lightweight Neural Networks[^(\*)](#c06.xhtml#c06-note-0001)

 

## 6.1 Introduction

As a crucial tool for network management, traffic classification plays an important role in congestion control and quality‐of‐service (QoS) guarantees (Dai et al., [2019](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0005), Liu et al., [2020](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0018), Yao et al., [2020](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0035), Zou et al., [2018](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0039)). Nowadays, due to growing concerns about privacy and security, many applications have adopted various encryption technologies.

 

## 6.2 Motivation

Existing traffic classification approaches sacrifice efficiency to guarantee the high precision of classification results. Most existing machine learning (ML) methods are based on the extraction of the hand‐crafted statistical features (Niu et al., [2019](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0024), Meng et al., [2015](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0021), Alshammari and Zincir‐Heywood, [2015](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0001)), such as mean packet length, flow duration, and the mean interarrival time of the packet. Observing the entire or large part of a flow is necessary to obtain these statistical features. Therefore, these methods can only apply to offline scenarios. Although traffic classification methods based on deep learning (DL) can eliminate the disadvantage of manual feature construction, some problems still exist. For example, most existing studies either use packet‐level features or flow‐level features, which overlook important information (i.e. these two kinds of features are not used together). Meanwhile, most existing models lack the consideration of model efficiency, where huge overhead of memory and runtime in the complex neural networks leads to high‐energy consumption (Rezaei and Liu, [2019](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0027), Cheng et al., [2020](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0003), [2021](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0004)).

To address the above issues, in this chapter, we propose a lightweight neural network for online‐encrypted traffic classification, which takes three consecutive packets at a random location of a flow as the input. To clarify, we call the proposed model LightNet. Based on the principle “maximizing the reuse of thin modules,” the features at global (flow) and local (packet) levels are extracted by using a thin module, which adopts multihead attention and 1D convolutional neural networks (1D‐CNNs). Generally, global features come from the interactions between packets in a flow, and local features are included in the bytes from a few raw packets. At the global level, with the multihead attention, each packet interacts with all the other packets in one step to calculate the packet weights by multiplying matrices, greatly reducing the number of parameters and the running time. Additionally, a packet is projected into multiple subspaces, and thus different feature interactions in different subspaces can be captured in parallel by the multihead attention. Therefore, the parameters of the multihead attention mainly come from the projection matrix and are usually low. At the local level, 1D‐CNN focuses on the packet bytes and is utilized to extract packet‐level features, which has been proved with high effectiveness and efficiency in traffic classification (Rezaei and Liu, [2018](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0026)).

The rest of this chapter is structured as follows: The preliminaries are presented in [Section 6.3](#c06.xhtml#c06-sec-0003), and the proposed model LightNet is elaborated in [Section 6.4](#c06.xhtml#c06-sec-0007). [Section 6.5](#c06.xhtml#c06-sec-0018) conducts the analysis of experimental results. [Section 6.6](#c06.xhtml#c06-sec-0027) shows the related work of the traffic classification. Finally, [Section 6.7](#c06.xhtml#c06-sec-0033) draws conclusion of this chapter.

 

## 6.3 Preliminaries

 

### 6.3.1 Problem Definition

The problem this chapter aims to address is to classify the encrypted traffic into corresponding applications in an effective and efficient way. In this field, two types of granularity are generally used in both industry and academia, packet and flow (Zou et al., [2018](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0039)). In this chapter, we choose the flow granularity, which can contain both packet and flow information. A flow refers to all packets with the same values of the quintuple,

Formally, a classifier is trained to recognize the category to which a flow belongs (i.e. classifier: ![f Subscript i](images/c06-i0007.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>f</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></math>![right-arrow](images/c06-i0008.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mo>&#8594;</mo></mrow></math> class).

 

### 6.3.2 Packet Interaction

Efficient global information can be extracted with high‐order interaction between packets (He and Chua, [2017](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0010)). Given ![upper N](images/c06-i0009.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>N</mi></mrow></math> packets as the input, the packet interaction can be defined as follows:

(6.4)![g left-parenthesis bold-italic x 1 comma bold-italic x 2 comma ellipsis comma bold-italic x Subscript upper N Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c06-math-0004.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll"><mi>g</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><mi>&#8230;</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mi><mi>N</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math> 

where ![x Subscript i](images/c06-i0010.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></math> is the unified representation of packet ![p Subscript i](images/c06-i0011.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>p</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></math>, and ![g left-parenthesis dot right-parenthesis](images/c06-i0012.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>g</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mo>&#183;</mo><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math> is a nonadditive interactive function, such as outer product (Lian et al., [2018](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0013), Wang et al., [2017a](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0031)) and multiplication (Rendle, [2011](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0025)). For instance, ![bold-italic x 1 times bold-italic x 2](images/c06-i0013.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>&#215;</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></math> is an interactive feature concerning packet ![bold-italic x 1](images/c06-i0014.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></math> and packet ![bold-italic x 2](images/c06-i0015.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></math>. Traditionally, the meaningful high‐order interactive features are extracted by recurrent neural network (RNN). However, it is very time‐consuming to wait for the pairwise interactions between the joint packets. Besides, the limited hidden unit structure in the RNN cannot efficiently show the interpretability of the high‐order features. Hence, we propose to explore an approach that can efficiently discover the meaningful high‐order interactive features, and meanwhile, all packet features can be projected into many subspaces, where each packet can interact with all packets in one step.

 

## 6.4 The Proposed Lightweight Model

As shown in [Figure 6.1](#c06.xhtml#c06-fig-0001), the proposed model will be illustrated in this section. The input of the proposed model comes from either online or offline traffic traces captured on the router. After preprocessing, ![upper N](images/c06-i0016.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>N</mi></mrow></math> consecutive packets in a flow are captured. Then, the initial features of each packet are extracted and embedded into vectors that will be concatenated to represent the flow. The attention encoder is employed to extract the local and global features. At last, a fully connected layer with softmax is employed to output the probability distribution of labels. To enhance the stability of the lightweight model, a series of training optimization methods are adopted, including ResNet (He et al., [2016](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0011)), layer normalization (Ba et al., [2016](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0002)), and the learning rate warmup (He et al., [2016](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0011)).

![Figure 6.1 The preprocessing and architecture of LightNet. The structure of the embedding and attention encoder layers is fixed, and the structure of fully connected layer varies with different tasks.](images/c06f001.png)

[**Figure 6.1**](#c06.xhtml#R_c06-fig-0001) The preprocessing and architecture of LightNet. The structure of the embedding and attention encoder layers is fixed, and the structure of fully connected layer varies with different tasks.

 

### 6.4.1 Preprocessing

As a crucial encryption technology for network management, the hypertext transfer protocol secure (HTTPS) over secure socket layer is based on transmission control protocol (TCP) (Naylor et al., [2014](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0023)). In recent years, more and more applications use HTTPS (Liu et al., [2020](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0018)). In this chapter, we will therefore classify HTTPS traffic. The three‐way handshake in TCP connections is skipped because it contains less information that is useful for traffic classification (Este et al., [2009](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0007)).

For the traffic data, we split it into flows according to the quintuple of a flow. The ![upper N](images/c06-i0017.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>N</mi></mrow></math> consecutive raw packets in a flow are extracted as the only inputs for our model.

 

### 6.4.2 Feature Extraction

 

#### 6.4.2.1 Embedding

The embedding layer is used to extract the shallow features of a packet. In this chapter, both flow‐level and packet‐level features are leveraged. As a result, the feature representation of each packet is unified in the same format, which includes multiple feature fields in our model.

For packet ![bold-italic x Subscript bold-italic i](images/c06-i0018.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mi><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">i</mi></msub></math>, the original feature fields can be represented as follows:

(6.5)![bold-italic x Subscript bold-italic i Baseline equals StartSet x Subscript i 1 Baseline comma x Subscript i 2 Baseline comma ellipsis comma x Subscript i Sub Subscript upper M Subscript Baseline EndSet](images/c06-math-0005.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mi><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">i</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mo stretchy="false">&#123;</mo><msub><mrow><mi>x</mi></mrow><mrow><msub><mi>i</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></mrow></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mrow><mi>x</mi></mrow><mrow><msub><mi>i</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></mrow></msub><mo>,</mo><mi>&#8230;</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mrow><mi>x</mi></mrow><mrow><msub><mi>i</mi><mi>M</mi></msub></mrow></msub><mo stretchy="false">&#125;</mo></math> 

where ![upper M](images/c06-i0019.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>M</mi></mrow></math> is the number of feature fields, and ![x Subscript i Sub Subscript j](images/c06-i0020.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mrow><mi>x</mi></mrow><mrow><msub><mi>i</mi><mi>j</mi></msub></mrow></msub></math> is the feature value of the ![j](images/c06-i0021.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>j</mi></mrow></math>th field. For example, ![x Subscript i 1](images/c06-i0022.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mrow><mi>x</mi></mrow><mrow><msub><mi>i</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></mrow></msub></math> represents the packet position (e.g. ![1 comma 2 comma ellipsis comma upper N](images/c06-i0023.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>2</mn><mo>,</mo><mi>&#8230;</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>N</mi></math>), ![x Subscript i 2](images/c06-i0024.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mrow><mi>x</mi></mrow><mrow><msub><mi>i</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></mrow></msub></math> could denote the packet length, and ![x Subscript i 3](images/c06-i0025.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mrow><mi>x</mi></mrow><mrow><msub><mi>i</mi><mn>3</mn></msub></mrow></msub></math> could be the first 784 bytes after removing the Ethernet header. The effectiveness of the mentioned features has been validated in many works (Wang et al., [2017c](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0033)), (Lotfollahi et al., [2020](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0019)). It is worth noting that even for encrypted traffic, the information in the packet headers can be exploited for traffic classification (Rezaei and Liu, [2019](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0027)). Not only do we leverage the header of the packet, but a portion of the packet payload is also used. For the number of packet bytes, the features are normalized to ![left-bracket 0 comma 1 right-bracket](images/c06-i0026.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mo stretchy="false">\[</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">\]</mo></math> by dividing by 255, where the original bytes range from 0 to 255. Then, we unify these features. For a vectorial feature ![bold-italic x Subscript i Sub Subscript bold-italic j](images/c06-i0027.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mi></mrow><mrow><msub><mi>i</mi><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">j</mi></msub></mrow></msub></math>, the dimension change can be expressed as follows:

where ![bold-italic u Subscript j](images/c06-i0033.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">u</mi><mi>j</mi></msub></math> is an embedding vector for field ![j](images/c06-i0034.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>j</mi></mrow></math>. The above processing applies to all feature fields in each packet. It is worth noting that both parameters ![upper U Subscript j](images/c06-i0035.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>U</mi><mi>j</mi></msub></math> and ![bold-italic u Subscript j](images/c06-i0036.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">u</mi><mi>j</mi></msub></math> are trainable.

Then, the vectoral and scalar features are transformed into a unified representation, and the packet vector ![bold-italic e Subscript i](images/c06-i0037.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">e</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></math> (take packet ![i](images/c06-i0038.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></math> as an example) can be represented as follows:

(6.8)![bold-italic e Subscript i Baseline equals upper W Subscript m a p Baseline left-bracket bold-italic e Subscript i 1 Baseline comma bold-italic e Subscript i 2 Baseline comma ellipsis comma bold-italic e Subscript i Sub Subscript bold-italic upper M Subscript Baseline right-bracket](images/c06-math-0008.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">e</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><msub><mi>W</mi><mrow><mi>m</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>p</mi></mrow></msub><mo stretchy="false">\[</mo><msub><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">e</mi></mrow><mrow><msub><mi>i</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></mrow></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">e</mi></mrow><mrow><msub><mi>i</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></mrow></msub><mo>,</mo><mi>&#8230;</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">e</mi></mrow><mrow><msub><mi>i</mi><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">M</mi></msub></mrow></msub><mo stretchy="false">\]</mo></math> 

where ![left-bracket bold-italic e Subscript i 1 Baseline comma bold e Subscript i Baseline Subscript 2 Baseline comma ellipsis comma bold-italic e Subscript i Sub Subscript bold-italic upper M Subscript Baseline right-bracket element-of double-struck upper R Superscript m](images/c06-i0039.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mo stretchy="false">\[</mo><msub><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">e</mi></mrow><mrow><msub><mi>i</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></mrow></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mrow><msub><mi mathvariant="bold">e</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msub><mo>,</mo><mi>&#8230;</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">e</mi></mrow><mrow><msub><mi>i</mi><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">M</mi></msub></mrow></msub><mo stretchy="false">\]</mo><mo>&#8712;</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="double-struck">R</mi><mi>m</mi></msup></math> is the result of concatenating all feature fields and ![upper W Subscript m a p Baseline element-of double-struck upper R Superscript d times m](images/c06-i0040.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>W</mi><mrow><mi>m</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>p</mi></mrow></msub><mo>&#8712;</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="double-struck">R</mi><mrow><mi>d</mi><mo>&#215;</mo><mi>m</mi></mrow></msup></math> is the mapping matrix. Therefore, the packet can be expressed as a vector of ![d](images/c06-i0041.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>d</mi></mrow></math> dimensions. The flow can be represented as a matrix with a shape of ![upper N times d](images/c06-i0042.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>N</mi><mo>&#215;</mo><mi>d</mi></math> by stacking the packet vectors, where ![upper N](images/c06-i0043.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>N</mi></mrow></math> is the number of packets in a flow.

 

#### 6.4.2.2 Attention Encoder

The attention encoder is designed to extract packet‐level and flow‐level features at a deeper level. Inspired by the literature (Vaswani et al., [2017](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0029)), we apply thin modules with multihead attention mechanism and 1D‐CNN feed‐forward layers. Specifically, in our model, the thin module is reused ![upper T](images/c06-i0044.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>T</mi></mrow></math> times to model the ![upper T](images/c06-i0045.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>T</mi></mrow></math>‐order interactions between the packets, and the number of parameters of the model is significantly reduced.

For the attention encoder, four lines of works are contained (see [Figure 6.2](#c06.xhtml#c06-fig-0002)): (i) multihead attention, (ii) add & norm, (iii) feed‐forward layer and (iv) flatten layer. In what follows, the detailed design of each component will be explained.

![Figure 6.2 The construct of attention encoder.](images/c06f002.png)

[**Figure 6.2**](#c06.xhtml#R_c06-fig-0002) The construct of attention encoder.

 

***Multihead Attention*** The multihead attention block is utilized to extract the flow‐level features, as it can model the interactions between packets. In the field of traffic classification, multihead attention has not been verified for its effectiveness. We find that it can be one of the most effective and efficient blocks to extract flow‐level features, as shown by the experimental results in [Section 6.5.4](#c06.xhtml#c06-sec-0023). With the help of multihead attention, each packet is allowed to interact with all the other packets in one step as shown in ([6.9](#c06.xhtml#c06-disp-0009)), greatly reducing the number of parameters and shortening the running time. Moreover, a packet is projected into multiple subspaces, and therefore the multihead attention can capture different feature interactions in different subspaces. By stacking the module with multihead attention, we can model different orders of feature interactions.

Take packet ![m](images/c06-i0046.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>m</mi></mrow></math> as an example, and we explain the extraction method for high‐order features. According to the work by Vaswani et al. ([2017](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0029)), the attention weight under head ![h](images/c06-i0047.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>h</mi></mrow></math> between packet ![m](images/c06-i0048.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>m</mi></mrow></math> and ![k](images/c06-i0049.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow></math> is calculated as ![alpha Subscript m comma k Superscript h](images/c06-i0050.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msubsup><mrow><mi>&#945;</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>m</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>k</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>h</mi></mrow></msubsup></math> with the value of ![bold-italic e Subscript m](images/c06-i0051.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">e</mi><mi>m</mi></msub></math> and ![bold-italic e Subscript k](images/c06-i0052.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">e</mi><mi>k</mi></msub></math> and the weight of head ![h](images/c06-i0053.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>h</mi></mrow></math>. Then, a temporary representation of packet ![m](images/c06-i0054.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>m</mi></mrow></math> is defined as follows:

(6.9)![bold-italic e overTilde Subscript m Superscript h Baseline equals sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript upper N Endscripts alpha Subscript m comma i Superscript h Baseline bold-italic e Subscript bold-italic i](images/c06-math-0009.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll"><msubsup><mrow><mover accent="true"><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">e</mi><mo mathvariant="bold-italic">&#732;</mo></mover></mrow><mrow><mi>m</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>h</mi></mrow></msubsup><mo>=</mo><munderover><mrow><mo stretchy="false" movablelimits="false">&#8721;</mo></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mrow><mi>N</mi></mrow></munderover><msubsup><mrow><mi>&#945;</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>m</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>i</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>h</mi></mrow></msubsup><msub><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">e</mi><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">i</mi></msub></math> 

by combining all the packets in the same flow with corresponding attention weights. After computing all the ![e overTilde Subscript m Superscript h](images/c06-i0055.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msubsup><mrow><mover accent="true"><mi>e</mi><mo>&#732;</mo></mover></mrow><mrow><mi>m</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>h</mi></mrow></msubsup></math> in all heads, the packet vector can be expressed as follows:

(6.10)![bold-italic e overTilde Subscript bold-italic m Baseline equals upper C o n c a t left-parenthesis bold-italic e overTilde Subscript bold-italic m Superscript bold 1 Baseline comma ellipsis comma bold-italic e overTilde Subscript bold-italic m Superscript bold-italic upper H Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c06-math-0010.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll"><msub><mrow><mover accent="true"><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">e</mi><mo mathvariant="bold">&#732;</mo></mover></mrow><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">m</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mi>C</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>n</mi><mi>c</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>t</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msubsup><mrow><mover accent="true"><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">e</mi><mo mathvariant="bold">&#732;</mo></mover></mrow><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">m</mi></mrow><mrow><mn mathvariant="bold">1</mn></mrow></msubsup><mo>,</mo><mi>&#8230;</mi><mo>,</mo><msubsup><mrow><mover accent="true"><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">e</mi><mo mathvariant="bold-italic">&#732;</mo></mover></mrow><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">m</mi></mrow><mrow><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">H</mi></mrow></msubsup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math> 

where ![upper H](images/c06-i0056.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>H</mi></mrow></math> is the number of heads.

Remarkably, the computation of all the heads is parallelized, providing a benefit to the model running speed.

 

***Add & Norm*** This layer is designed to speed up the training and prevent network degradation of the model. The add & norm module includes residual network (ResNet) and layer normalization, where “add” corresponds to ResNet and “norm” corresponds to layer normalization.

Expressed as ![y equals upper F left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis plus x](images/c06-i0057.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>y</mi><mo>=</mo><mi>F</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>+</mo><mi>x</mi></math>, a ResNet block can prevent network degradation with few parameters. After the multihead attention and feed‐forward layer, we add a standard ResNet to save the raw and previously learned information.

Moreover, layer normalization is a technique to normalize the distributions of intermediate layers, which enables smoother gradients, faster training, and better generalization accuracy (Xu et al., [2019](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0034)).

 

***Feed Forward Layer*** In the feed forward layer, the deployment of 1D‐CNN helps to extract the packet‐level features, as it focuses on a single packet. In our work, a 1D‐CNN layer with kernel size 1 is applied. For each packet, the inner products with each kernel constitute the deeper representation of the packet.

The combination of the three modules above (i.e. multihead attention, add & norm, and feed‐forward) makes up the loop block as shown in [Figure 6.2](#c06.xhtml#c06-fig-0002), with consistent dimensions of input and output.

 

***Flatten Layer*** In the flatten layer, the final vector of the flows is integrated. All the packet vectors ![bold-italic e Subscript bold-italic i](images/c06-i0058.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">e</mi><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">i</mi></msub></math> of the flow ![f](images/c06-i0059.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>f</mi></mrow></math> are concatenated into the flatten vector ![upper E Subscript f](images/c06-i0060.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>E</mi><mi>f</mi></msub></math> to represent the flow ![f](images/c06-i0061.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>f</mi></mrow></math>, shown in ([6.11](#c06.xhtml#c06-disp-0011)).

(6.11)![upper E Subscript f Baseline equals upper C o n c a t left-parenthesis bold-italic e bold 1 comma ellipsis comma bold-italic e Subscript bold-italic upper N Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c06-math-0011.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>E</mi><mi>f</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mi>C</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>n</mi><mi>c</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>t</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">e</mi><mn mathvariant="bold">1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><mi>&#8230;</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">e</mi><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">N</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math>  

#### 6.4.2.3 Fully Connected Layer

Fully connected layer with softmax function converts the flow feature ![upper E Subscript f](images/c06-i0062.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>E</mi><mi>f</mi></msub></math> into the probability distribution of labels. Finally, we choose the label with maximum probability as the output label.

 

## 6.5 Case Study

 

### 6.5.1 Evaluation Metrics

To evaluate the effectiveness of the proposed model, we adopt four well‐known metrics, namely accuracy, precision, recall, and F1‐score. *Accuracy* represents the proportion of correctly classified flows. With respect to the other three metrics, we obtain the true positive (TP), false positive (FP), and false negative (FN) by comparing the output classification results with the labeled ground truth. Take class ![i](images/c06-i0063.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></math> as an example, the information above can be used to calculate the index of ![upper P r e c i s i o n Subscript i Baseline comma](images/c06-i0064.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mrow><mi>P</mi><mi>r</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>c</mi><mi>i</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>i</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>n</mi></mrow><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>,</mo></math> ![upper R e c a l l Subscript i](images/c06-i0065.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mrow><mi>R</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>c</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>l</mi><mi>l</mi></mrow><mi>i</mi></msub></math>, and ![upper F Baseline 1 minus s c o r e Subscript i](images/c06-i0066.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mrow><mi>F</mi><mn>1</mn><mi>&#8208;</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>c</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>r</mi><mi>e</mi></mrow><mi>i</mi></msub></math> as Eqs. 

Additionally, to evaluate the efficiency of LightNet, we measure the training time and testing time per 100 batches, and the batch size is set to 32, where the word “batch” can be interpreted as a set of flows processed each time.

 

### 6.5.2 Baselines

To confirm the effectiveness of our model, we compare the model with the following baselines in terms of accuracy, precision, recall, and F1‐score. For model efficiency, our model is compared with the baseline models in terms of average training time and average testing time.

*   1D‐CNN model This model was proposed in Wang et al. ([2017c](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0033)). This method integrated feature selection, feature extraction, and classifier into a unified end‐to‐end framework with 1D‐CNN, intending to automatically learn the nonlinear relationship between raw input and expected output. To the best of our knowledge, it was the first time to develop an end‐to‐end method for encrypted traffic classification.
*   CNN with LSTM model This model was introduced in Zou et al. ([2018](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0039)) and employed to compare the effectiveness of multihead attention and RNN for flow feature extraction. It should be noted that in Zou et al. ([2018](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0039)), the convolutional network was utilized to extract the packet features for a single packet. Long short‐term memory (LSTM) units were trained to extract the flow features, and the model also took three consecutive packets of a flow as the input.
*   Bi‐GRU model with transfer learning The work was shown in Liu et al. ([2020](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0018)), which also utilized bidirectional gate recurrent unit (Bi‐GRU) to model the packet interactions. With respect to the input, the model selected three packets in a flow, where each packet contained 900 bytes. Besides, the attention mechanism was adopted to assign weights to features according to their contributions to the classification. The Bi‐GRU network in Liu et al. ([2020](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0018)) has been proved to be the state‐of‐art deep learning method for encrypted traffic classification.

 

### 6.5.3 Datasets

Two datasets are employed to validate the performance of LightNet. Dataset A contains flows in all directions (i.e. from client to server and from servers to clients). Dataset B only contains the flow from clients to servers.

*   Dataset A (Shbair, [2016](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0028)) was published by the University of Lorraine, constructed by crawling HTTPS websites over two weeks. The details of Dataset A are shown in [Table 6.1](#c06.xhtml#c06-tbl-0001). The dataset contains PCAP files of crawling top 779 accessed HTTPS websites by Google Chrome and Mozilla Firefox Web browsers. To simplify the training process, we remove the special characters (e.g. dashes and numbers). At last, the applications with more than 1000 flows were chosen to use in the experiment, which contains 47 classes and 84 245 flows.

*   Dataset B (Liu et al., [2020](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0018)) was collected by ourselves from the backbone of China Science and Technology Network (CSTNET), an Internet service provider in China. This dataset only contains unidirectional traffic flows, from clients to servers. In the end, the top 16 services with the most traffic flows were selected as the experimental data, which contain more than 49K flows.

It is important to note that all the original data is raw, and it is necessary to label the data manually. To label the data, we adopt an advanced method proposed in Liu et al. ([2018](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0016)). The process can be summarized as (i) getting the value of server name indication (SNI), which is the domain's substring of applications, (ii) comparing the IP address resolution with the SNI field for the credibility enhancement, and (iii) discarding the flow if the SNI filed is null or the IP cannot be parsed into a domain name.

 

### 6.5.4 Evaluation on Datasets

 

#### 6.5.4.1 Evaluation on Dataset A

*   Effectiveness analysis

As mentioned above, a new embedding approach for packet representation is explored, which can enhance the efficiency of extracting high‐order features. Both the statistical features (e.g. the packet length and packet position) and the packet byte features are embedded into a vector uniformly to represent the packet. In the three comparison baseline works, packet bytes are the only original features as the input into the model. As presented in [Figure 6.3](#c06.xhtml#c06-fig-0003), a comparative experiment of the two embedding methods is conducted on Dataset A. The experimental results are generated from a 10‐fold cross‐validation. As shown in [Figure 6.3](#c06.xhtml#c06-fig-0003)a, when the embedding method we illustrated in [Section 6.4.2.1](#c06.xhtml#c06-sec-0011) is adopted, it is obvious that our model outperforms the baseline models on all metrics. The accuracy, precision, recall, and F1‐score rate reach 99.76%, 98.84%, 98.74%, and 98.79%, respectively. Improvements of 15.01%, 2.89%, and 1.38% on F1‐score compared to the 1D‐CNN, CNN with LSTM, and Bi‐GRU models demonstrate the great performance of our model. As shown in [Figure 6.3](#c06.xhtml#c06-fig-0003)b, when only the packet bytes are embedded as the original features such as the three baseline works, the performance of all models degrades due to less information available. The experimental results show that our embedding method improves the precision of LightNet by 1.7%. To summarize, the model performs better when our proposed embedding method is applied.

![Figure 6.3 Dataset A: comparisons between LightNet and the three baseline models on accuracy, precision, recall, and F1‐score. (a) LightNet with the embedding method we illustrated in Section 6.4.2.1. (b) LightNet that only embeds the packet bytes as the original features like the three baseline works.](images/c06f003.png)

[**Figure 6.3**](#c06.xhtml#R_c06-fig-0003) Dataset A: comparisons between LightNet and the three baseline models on accuracy, precision, recall, and F1‐score. (a) LightNet with the embedding method we illustrated in [Section 6.4.2.1](#c06.xhtml#c06-sec-0011). (b) LightNet that only embeds the packet bytes as the original features like the three baseline works.

The model which employs 1D‐CNN to classify flows performs worst due to the oversimplified model structure, which focuses on extracting packet‐level features. However, this work inspires us to explore an end‐to‐end approach for traffic classification. The second benchmark model, CNN with LSTM, utilizes both the packet features and flow features as considered in our model, where CNN extracts the packet‐level features and LSTM is used to obtain the flow‐level features. Experimental results show that LSTM is not as effective as multihead attention in the extraction of flow features. This is because the multihead attention is attributed to the one‐step interaction of all packets and the parallel computing of all subspaces. Owing to the use of multihead attention, a key advantage of our model is that the number of parameters and running time are significantly reduced. Therefore, the multihead attention mechanism is proved to be effective to extract features in high dimensions. Besides, our model also can benefit from the application of ResNet, layer normalization, and learning rate warm‐up. The Bi‐GRU model performs better than the other two baselines, which utilizes an attention mechanism to assign greater attention to more important features. Similar with the LSTM, the Bi‐GRU model can only handle the interaction of two packets at a clip. As a result, it is time‐consuming and inefficient to wait for the output from the previous interaction in the RNN model due to the extra time overheads required.

*   Efficiency analysis

To improve running efficiency, we adopt some principles, which can be summarized as (i) reuse of thin modules, (ii) parallel computation in multiple heads (i.e. subspaces), and (iii) multiple training optimization techniques, such as ResNet, layer normalization, and learning rate warm‐up.

LightNet benefits greatly from the principles mentioned above. The number of parameters of LightNet is 1.8% of 1D‐CNN, 2.7% of CNN with LSTM, and 19.8% of the Bi‐GRU model. With respect to the time efficiency, training and testing time of every 100 batches are considered as metrics. The training time of LightNet is 44.0% of 1D‐CNN, 5.7% of CNN with LSTM, and 33.7% of the Bi‐GRU model. It should be noted that LSTM and Bi‐GRU can also be conducted to extract flow‐level features, but only two adjacent packets can interact at a time in LSTM, which leads to high time consumption.

 

#### 6.5.4.2 Evaluation on Dataset B

To make the results more credible, we also conduct an experiment on Dataset B as we do on Dataset A, in terms of accuracy, precision, recall, and F1‐score. As shown in [Figure 6.4](#c06.xhtml#c06-fig-0004), the accuracy, precision, recall, and F1‐score of LightNet reach 96.84%, 95.98%, 95.53% and 95.75%, respectively. Improvements of 10.22%, 3.85%, and 1.49% on F1‐score compared to the 1D‐CNN, CNN with LSTM, and the Bi‐GRU models give more evidence that our model performs well for encrypted traffic classification.

[Figure 6.5](#c06.xhtml#c06-fig-0005) contains three subgraphs, which display the precision, recall, and F1‐score values of 16 classes in Dataset B. In [Figure 6.5](#c06.xhtml#c06-fig-0005)(a), the precision values for 10 classes are greater than 95%, and the average precision of LightNet is greater than that of the other three models by 9.12%, 2.33%, and 1.56%, respectively. As shown in [Figure 6.5](#c06.xhtml#c06-fig-0005)b, for the class “s.qhupdate.com,” LightNet achieves a recall of 96.89%, with improvements of 17.81%, 3.10%, and 1.50%, respectively, compared to the other three baselines. In [Figure 6.5](#c06.xhtml#c06-fig-0005)c, the F1‐scores of 5 classes are greater than 99%. However, all models perform poorly on the “i.hdslb.com,” because there are only 1806 flows in this class. Through the experiment in this section, it can be concluded that for services from different providers, LightNet can achieve excellent performance.

![Figure 6.4 Dataset B: comparisons between LightNet and the three baseline models on accuracy, precision, recall, and F1‐score.](images/c06f004.png)

[**Figure 6.4**](#c06.xhtml#R_c06-fig-0004) Dataset B: comparisons between LightNet and the three baseline models on accuracy, precision, recall, and F1‐score.

![Figure 6.5 Dataset B: comparisons with the three baseline models. ## 6.6 Related Work

Our model involves three lines of works: (i) encrypted traffic classification, (ii) flow‐based methods, and (iii) packet‐based methods, which are introduced in this section. Meanwhile, the differences and improvements that are compared with the previous work will be summarized.

 

### 6.6.1 Encrypted Traffic Classification

Traffic classification task is to categorize the encrypted network traffic into appropriate classes. First, the target of traffic classification should be defined clearly. The general goals include QoS provisioning, intrusion detection, malware detection, resource usage planning, and so on. To meet its target, the traffic classification is usually carried out based on (i) protocols (e.g. HTTP, SSL, SMTP, DNS, or QUIC), (ii) traffic types (e.g. video, chat, or browsing), (iii) applications (e.g. Amazon, Apple, Microsoft, or Google), and (iv) websites. For all the above four cases, labeling each flow with the corresponding traffic classes is a necessity. In our work, we identify the application of the flow. Second, a large and representative dataset is required. Although a few public datasets are available for research, there is no agreed common dataset in the field of traffic classification (Rezaei and Liu, [2019](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0027)).

![Table 6.2  A summary of existing traffic classification methods.](images/c06-i0069.png)

[**Table 6.2**](#c06.xhtml#R_c06-tbl-0002)  A summary of existing traffic classification methods.

 

### 6.6.2 Packet‐Based Methods

The packet‐based methods for traffic classification take the bytes of a packet as the input directly. The packet bytes include the information of the packet header and payload data. [Table 6.2](#c06.xhtml#c06-tbl-0002) shows a summary of the packet‐based methods. With respect to the packet header, the information in layers 3 and 4 are useful for nonencrypted traffic (Rezaei and Liu, [2019](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0027)). Before the deep learning era, fields including port number, protocol, and packet length were carefully chosen by domain experts as representative features. In some recent approaches, entire packets are taken as the input. For payload data, the information above the layer 4 header can be exploited for traffic classification. In work Lotfollahi et al. ([2020](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0019)), after an initial preprocessing phase, packets were fed into the model with stacked autoencoder and CNN to classify network traffic. Liang et al. ([2019](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0014)) proposed a deep reinforcement learning approach to solve the packet classification problem. The method in Liang et al. ([2019](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0014)) used succinct representations to encode state and action space, and efficiently explored candidate decision trees to optimize a global objective. Finamore et al. ([2010](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0008)) proposed a classification approach that leveraged the statistical characterizations of the payload by extracting payload signatures. However, the packet‐based methods focus on the detailed information of a few packets and lack attention to global characteristics. In our model, both packet‐level and flow‐level features are considered, making the model efficient and effective.

 

### 6.6.3 Flow‐Based Methods

Flow‐based methods require the selection of flow features, and these features are then fed into the classifier. [Table 6.2](#c06.xhtml#c06-tbl-0002) also shows a summary of the flow‐based methods. In general, there are two main classes: traditional machine learning‐based and deep learning‐based.

 

#### 6.6.3.1 Traditional Machine Learning‐Based Methods

Based on complex manual features, existing traditional machine learning‐based methods for traffic classification at the flow level have their limitations. Zhu and Zheng ([2019](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0038)) studied the application of support vector machine (SVM) to classify the network traffic by using the packet length and TCP flags, which converted the actual problem into the high‐dimensional feature space through the kernel function. Fu et al. ([2016](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0009)) built hidden Markov models (HMMs) to classify the services of mobile messaging Apps by jointly modeling user behavioral patterns, network characteristics, and temporal dependencies. Along this line, the authors in Fu et al. ([2016](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0009)) first segmented Internet traffic from traffic‐flows into sessions with several dialogues in a hierarchical way, and two features including packet length and time delay are employed. According to the distribution of time and packet size, the authors in Yao et al. ([2020](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0035)) classified the obfuscated traffic with Gaussian mixture models and HMM, which established the connection between inner‐packet time and packet size to improve the accuracy of the flow feature description. However, these classifiers have to observe the whole flow or most of the packets in a flow to obtain these features, which are more appropriate for offline classification. In this chapter, with only a few consecutive packets as the input, the proposed model can meet the real‐time requirements in the field of online traffic classification.

 

#### 6.6.3.2 Deep Learning‐Based Methods

Deep learning methods have been widely used in traffic classification. In Liu et al. ([2020](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0018)), the Bi‐GRU was used to extract the forward and backward features of the byte sequences in a flow. Authors in Liu et al. ([2019](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0017)) applied Bi‐GRU to extract the hidden information in the packet length sequence. The study (Wang et al., [2017c](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0033)) took a few consecutive packets as the input of 1D‐CNN, which was the first work to apply the end‐to‐end method on the traffic classification and achieved an accuracy of 85.8%. To get spatial and temporal features, both CNN and RNN were used in Wang et al. ([2017b](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0032)). The study (Wang et al., [2017b](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0032)) first learned the spatial features of network traffic by using deep CNN and then learned temporal features by using the recurrent network. The authors in Zou et al. ([2018](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0039)) proposed a model with 2D‐CNN and LSTM networks, which accepts a few consecutive packets as the input. In Zou et al. ([2018](#c06.xhtml#c06-bib-0039)), CNN was used to extract the packet features for a single packet, and the LSTM network was trained to pick out the flow features based on the inputs of the packet features in a flow. As we can see above, pure CNN lacks the ability to extract interactive information, which leads to the fact that CNN often combines with RNN to extract the flow features. However, the dependencies between units in RNN make it time‐consuming to wait for the output of the previous unit. In our proposed method, interactions of all packets are implemented directly by the application of multihead attention.

 

## 6.7 Conclusion

Burning requirements on the efficiency of traffic classification are put forward due to the widespread adoption of encrypted traffic. In this chapter, a lightweight model called LightNet was proposed to reduce resource and time consumption. “Maximizing the reuse of thin modules” is our design principle. A thin module has been adopted for the multihead attention and the 1D convolutional network. Attributed to the one‐step interaction of all packets and the parallel computation of the multihead attention mechanism, a key advantage of LightNet is that the number of parameters and the training and testing time are significantly reduced. To improve the stability of the model, the designed network is trained with the aid of ResNet, layer normalization, and learning rate warm‐up. The proposed model outperformed the state‐of‐the‐art works based on deep learning on two datasets. The results have shown that our model has higher accuracy and running efficiency.
