## Note

1.  [^(\*)](#c07.xhtml#R_c07-note-0001)This chapter is written based on our previous publication (Sun et al., [2020](#c07.xhtml#c07-bib-0033)).

# 8  
Anomaly Classification with Unknown, Imbalanced and Few Labeled Log Data

 

## 8.1 Introduction

With the development of the Internet of Things, 5G, and cloud computing, the scale of the system size is rapidly growing. In this context, anomalies get easier to turn up and will affect the quality of services and the stability of the system. To maintain the system performance, network operators apply various anomaly classification techniques to monitor the system status, identify and locate anomalies, and then enhance the system reliability (Huang et al., [2018](#c08.xhtml#c08-bib-0011), Cao et al., [2021](#c08.xhtml#c08-bib-0001)).

Effective anomaly classification methods need to rely on abundant system data. Compared with other numerical data such as system Key Performance Indicator (KPI), e.g. CPU utilization and memory utilization) (Meng et al., [2018](#c08.xhtml#c08-bib-0017)), logs can provide more useful descriptive information for anomaly classification. For example, when the CPU utilization rises sharply, the system may be anomalous. However, the KPI value is not sufficient to determine the anomaly. Logs can therefore help find out the specific events that affect the CPU utilization. Besides, logs are natural language sequences, which can not only represent a single point anomaly but also show collective anomalies.

With the development of AI technologies and the promotion of big data platform, many log‐based anomaly classification methods were developed based on machine‐learning techniques (Guo et al., [2021](#c08.xhtml#c08-bib-0007), Sun et al., [2020](#c08.xhtml#c08-bib-0027), Zuo et al., [2020](#c08.xhtml#c08-bib-0038)). Lin et al. ([2016](#c08.xhtml#c08-bib-0014)) proposed an unsupervised log‐based identification method named LogCluster based on the Agglomerative Hierarchical Clustering (AHC) algorithm and applied it to the real‐world online service system. Pande and Ahuja ([2017](#c08.xhtml#c08-bib-0020)) proposed word embeddings for anomaly classification (WEAC) based on the Skip‐Gram (SG) and the Continuous Bag of Words (CBOW) model of the word embedding method, which is called word2Vec for anomaly classification. Meng et al. ([2018](#c08.xhtml#c08-bib-0017)) proposed LogClass, a data‐driven system to detect and classify anomalies based on Positive‐Unlabeled (PU) Learning and Support Vector Machines (SVM), to tackle the challenges of device‐agnostic vocabulary and partial labels. Zhang et al. ([2019](#c08.xhtml#c08-bib-0037)) proposed LogRobust, a log analysis approach, which is robust to the unstable log data based on word embedding and Long Short‐Term Memory (LSTM).

However, there are still limitations in the existing log‐based anomaly classification methods. In what follows, we elaborate the limitations.

First, the limitation of the feature extraction. Some existing methods need to build a classification model using the known log events (i.e. the templates of log messages) and log sequences (i.e. a series of log events that record specific execution flows) extracted from the training dataset (Zhang et al., [2019](#c08.xhtml#c08-bib-0037)). Some other methods need to build a word dictionary in advance, and then construct feature vectors by counting word frequency (Meng et al., [2018](#c08.xhtml#c08-bib-0017)). They fail to work with unseen log events and log sequences. However, real‐world logs are unstable, and new but similar log events and log sequences often present due to the system upgrade (Zhang et al., [2019](#c08.xhtml#c08-bib-0037)).

Second, the limitation of the few‐shot problem. Most machine learning‐based anomaly classification methods rely on the large‐scale, balanced, and labeled training datasets, which is hard to construct in a real‐world system. On the one hand, obtaining such data in real‐world systems require tremendous efforts for annotation. On the other hand, compared to the logs under normal system operations, the occurrence of anomalous logs is low. Some anomalous logs only occur a few times and are difficult to reproduce. We may face a few‐shot problem (Wang et al., [2020](#c08.xhtml#c08-bib-0032)), where each class contains only a small number of samples, which makes it difficult to start a training process for machine learning models.

Third, the limitation of the closed world assumption (Fei and Liu, [2016](#c08.xhtml#c08-bib-0004)). Most machine‐learning‐based anomaly classification methods make the closed‐world assumption, which focuses on designing accurate classifiers under the assumption in experimental environment. The closed‐world assumption in log‐based anomaly classification methods include the “closed” of data categories, data scale, and data balance. The “closed” data categories mean that all test classes are assumed to be known at training time. The “closed” data scale refers to the target system that contains sufficient training data. The “closed” data balance means that each data category in the training dataset contains a similar number of samples. However, the log‐based anomaly classification is an open‐world problem (Xu et al., [2019](#c08.xhtml#c08-bib-0035)). An ideal model not only needs to face a small amount of unbalanced data but also needs to incrementally learn the unknown categories without retraining. However, supervised machine‐learning models have a fixed structure and parameters. They are sensitive to data and will classify an unknown anomalous data into a wrong known category.

To address the above problems, the method proposed in this chapter called OpenLog has some exploratory work. For the limitation of the feature extraction, we use a multilevel semantic model, mining the semantic, structural, and sequential information of logs. For the limitation of the few‐shot problem, we use a multitask training strategy on the auxiliary datasets obtained from other systems to enhance the performance of the classification model so that it has the ability to work on small samples. For the limitation of the closed‐world assumption, we try to transform the multiclassification task into the binary‐classification task of learning the depth of relationship between samples so that the model can detect and classify new categories.

We call the unbalanced data with increasing categories and few samples as “open” data and verify OpenLog using such data. We compare OpenLog with two advanced anomaly classification methods called LogRobust and LogClass based on three kinds of experiments to prove the superiority of OpenLog.

The remainder of this chapter is organized as follows: in [Section 8.2](#c08.xhtml#c08-sec-0002), we will introduce different feature extraction methods in log analysis, few‐shot problem, and strategies of few‐shot learning by examples to help readers better understand these concepts. In [Section 8.3](#c08.xhtml#c08-sec-0008), we will describe the proposed framework of OpenLog, including problem definition, data preprocessing, and the architecture of our method. In [Section 8.4](#c08.xhtml#c08-sec-0020), we will describe our experiments in detail and analyze the results. In [Section 8.5](#c08.xhtml#c08-sec-0030), we will discuss the shortcomings and further outlook of our proposed method. In [Section 8.6](#c08.xhtml#c08-sec-0031), we will summarize this chapter.

 

## 8.2 Examples

 

### 8.2.1 The Feature Extraction of Log Analysis

As shown in [Figures 8.1](#c08.xhtml#c08-fig-0001) and [8.2](#c08.xhtml#c08-fig-0002), logs are semistructured natural language sequences generated by logging statements and are used to describe the state and behavior of a system. A log consists of a log header and a log content. The log header is fixed and has a particular format, including timestamp, level, component, etc. The log content is a flexible text written by the developers, including the description text and variables. Logs are usually collected by the big data platforms, such as Kafka ![Figure 8.1 The components of a Log.](images/c08f001.png)

[**Figure 8.1**](#c08.xhtml#R_c08-fig-0001) The components of a Log.

Different log analysis tasks have different requirements for the components of a log. For example, traditional search‐based log analysis methods can focus on the specific system events they are interested in and ignore other unimportant or redundant events by searching the log level in a large‐scale data (Gulcu, [2003](#c08.xhtml#c08-bib-0006)). Most machine‐learning‐based log analysis methods need the description text of log content to analyze the system behavior, detect, and predict anomalies (Zhang et al., [2019](#c08.xhtml#c08-bib-0037)). Some log analysis methods also can make full use of log information by combining description text and parameters to have a deeper investigation of the system problems (Du et al., [2017](#c08.xhtml#c08-bib-0003)). There are also some log analysis methods that can rely on timestamp of logs to detect the sequential or quantitative anomalies of logs (Meng et al., [2019](#c08.xhtml#c08-bib-0018)).

![Figure 8.2 Logging and log collection.](images/c08f002.png)

[**Figure 8.2**](#c08.xhtml#R_c08-fig-0002) Logging and log collection.

To adapt to different log analysis tasks, the feature extraction of logs is very important. In recent years, the common log feature extraction in log analysis can be divided into the statistical feature extraction and the semantic feature extraction.

 

#### 8.2.1.1 Statistical Feature Extraction

Logs are generated by logging statements written by developers to record important system behaviors and events. The description text in the log is a fixed part, which we call log event or log template. One of the statistical feature extraction methods in log analysis is to create event count matrices (He et al., [2016](#c08.xhtml#c08-bib-0008)). As shown in [Figure 8.3](#c08.xhtml#c08-fig-0003), first, we need to slice the raw logs into a set of log sequences. There are three slicing strategies, including fixed window, sliding window, and the session window. Then, for each log sequence in a window, an event count vector is generated, which represents the number of occurrences of each event. All feature vectors together can form a feature matrix, that is, an event count matrix.

![Figure 8.3 The process of creating event count matrices.](images/c08f003.png)

[**Figure 8.3**](#c08.xhtml#R_c08-fig-0003) The process of creating event count matrices.

Another statistical feature extraction method is based on the bag‐of‐words expression (Wu et al., [2008](#c08.xhtml#c08-bib-0034)). Each vector component is assigned a value related to the estimated importance (also known as weight) of words in the logs (Soucy and Mineau, [2005](#c08.xhtml#c08-bib-0026)). Among them, the most common weighting expression is Term Frequency–Inverse Document Frequency (TF‐IDF). TF indicates how often a word appears in a document. IDF shows how infrequent a word is in all documents. The mathematical expression of TF‐IDF is as follows:

(8.1)![upper T upper F minus upper I upper D upper F equals t f Subscript i comma j Baseline times l o g left-parenthesis StartFraction upper N Over d f Subscript i Baseline EndFraction right-parenthesis](images/c08-math-0001.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll"><mi>T</mi><mi>F</mi><mo>&#8722;</mo><mi>I</mi><mi>D</mi><mi>F</mi><mo>=</mo><mi>t</mi><msub><mrow><mi>f</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>j</mi></mrow></msub><mo>&#215;</mo><mi>l</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>g</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mfrac><mrow><mi>N</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>d</mi><msub><mrow><mi>f</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></mfrac><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math> 

where ![t f Subscript i comma j](images/c08-i0001.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>t</mi><msub><mrow><mi>f</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>j</mi></mrow></msub></math> is the number of occurrences of the word ![i](images/c08-i0002.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></math> in document ![j](images/c08-i0003.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>j</mi></mrow></math>, ![d f Subscript i](images/c08-i0004.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>d</mi><msub><mrow><mi>f</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub></math> is the number of documents containing the word ![i](images/c08-i0005.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></math>, and ![upper N](images/c08-i0006.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>N</mi></mrow></math> is the total number of documents.

 

#### 8.2.1.2 Semantic Feature Extraction

The statistical feature extraction has some limitations. For example, to create an event count matrix, we need to slice log sequence in advance and fix the number of log events; to create a weighted feature matrix, we need to build a dictionary in advance and count the word frequency. This feature extraction method will fail to work with previously unseen log events and log sequences. With the development of Natural Language Processing (NLP), the semantic feature extraction based on word embedding has been widely used. Word embedding is a feature extraction method based on extensive pretraining. The common word embedding methods include word2Vec (Mikolov et al., [2013](#c08.xhtml#c08-bib-0019)), GloVe (Pennington et al., [2014](#c08.xhtml#c08-bib-0021)), Bert (Vaswani et al., [2017a](#c08.xhtml#c08-bib-0030)), just to name a few. Word embedding can provide a vector representation for each word by learning on large‐scale corpus. The words with similar semantics are close to each other, while dissimilar words can be more easily differentiated. On this basis, a log is represented by the average/weighted average semantic vector of all words, or the concatenation of all word vectors of the fixed dimension, which will not change with log events and log sequences.

 

### 8.2.2 Few‐Shot Problem

The target of machine learning models is to minimize the prediction error, which is the gap of the predicted value according the hypothetical function ![h](images/c08-i0009.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>h</mi></mrow></math> and the true value. In general, the error can be reduced by training on a large number of examples ![upper I](images/c08-i0010.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>I</mi></mrow></math> and learning a proper ![h](images/c08-i0011.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>h</mi></mrow></math> which is shown in [Figure 8.4](#c08.xhtml#c08-fig-0004)(a), where ![modifying above h with caret](images/c08-i0012.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>&#293;</mi></mrow></math> is a hypothetical function that minimizes the gap of the predicted value and the real value in all data, including the training data, validation data, and the testing data. ![h Superscript asterisk](images/c08-i0013.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msup><mrow><mi>h</mi></mrow><mrow><mo>&#42;</mo></mrow></msup></math> is a function in hypothetical space that minimizes the gap of the predicted value and the real value for all data. ![h Subscript upper I](images/c08-i0014.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mrow><mi>h</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>I</mi></mrow></msub></math> is a function in hypothetical space that minimizes the gap of the predicted value and the real value for the training data. Thus, the goal of machine‐learning models is to learn ![h Subscript upper I](images/c08-i0015.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mrow><mi>h</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>I</mi></mrow></msub></math> on the training data as close to ![h Superscript asterisk](images/c08-i0016.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msup><mrow><mi>h</mi></mrow><mrow><mo>&#42;</mo></mrow></msup></math> as possible. However, when the machine learning model meets the few‐shot problem, the number of available examples ![upper I](images/c08-i0017.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>I</mi></mrow></math> is small. It is then hard to learn an appropriate hypothetical function and get an accepted error as shown in [Figure 8.4](#c08.xhtml#c08-fig-0004)(b).

![Figure 8.4 Comparison of learning with sufficient and few training samples. (a) Large upper I and (b) Small upper I.](images/c08f004.png)

[**Figure 8.4**](#c08.xhtml#R_c08-fig-0004) Comparison of learning with sufficient and few training samples. (a) Large ![upper I](images/c08-i0007.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>I</mi></mrow></math> and (b) Small ![upper I](images/c08-i0008.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>I</mi></mrow></math>.

To address the few‐shot problem caused by a small training samples, the few‐shot learning based on prior knowledge is proposed. Few‐shot learning usually relieves the few‐shot problem from the three aspects shown in [Figure 8.5](#c08.xhtml#c08-fig-0005), including data, model, and the algorithm (Wang et al., [2020](#c08.xhtml#c08-bib-0032)).

![Figure 8.5 Different strategies on how few‐shot learning methods can solve the few‐shot problem. (a) Data, (b) Model, and (c) Algorithm.](images/c08f005.png)

[**Figure 8.5**](#c08.xhtml#R_c08-fig-0005) Different strategies on how few‐shot learning methods can solve the few‐shot problem. (a) Data, (b) Model, and (c) Algorithm.

*    Data. The data‐based strategy uses prior knowledge to augment the training data as shown in [Figure 8.5](#c08.xhtml#c08-fig-0005)(a). The common methods include data enhancement (Kozerawski and Turk, [2018](#c08.xhtml#c08-bib-0013), Santoro et al., [2016](#c08.xhtml#c08-bib-0024)) and data transforming (Pfister et al., [2014](#c08.xhtml#c08-bib-0022), Liu et al., [2018](#c08.xhtml#c08-bib-0015)). After enhancing the training data, standard machine learning models and algorithms can then be used with the augmented data.
*   Model. The model‐based strategy uses prior knowledge to constrain the complexity of hypothetical space as shown in [Figure 8.5](#c08.xhtml#c08-fig-0005)(b). For example, the gray area is not considered for optimization as they are known to be unlikely to contain the optimal hypothetical function according to prior knowledge. The common methods include multitask learning (Hu et al., [2018](#c08.xhtml#c08-bib-0010), Luo et al., [2017](#c08.xhtml#c08-bib-0016)) and embedding learning (Triantafillou et al., [2017](#c08.xhtml#c08-bib-0029)). This strategy is used in the proposed OpenLog projected in [Section 8.3](#c08.xhtml#c08-sec-0008).
*   Algorithm. The algorithm‐based strategy uses prior knowledge to search for the parameters set which parameterizes the best hypothetical function as shown in [Figure 8.5](#c08.xhtml#c08-fig-0005)(c). Prior knowledge alters the search strategy by providing a good initialization or guiding the search steps.

 

## 8.3 Methodology

 

### 8.3.1 Data Preprocessing

Logs are unstructured data and have different types of components, including *texts* (constant strings), *numerical values* (variable values like IP address and port number), and other *special structures* such as the CamelCase variables (shaped like “`isCommittable`”). Therefore, before a log analysis, we need to perform data preprocessing to obtain the structured log information.

![Figure 8.6 Log parsing tree‐using Drain with depth of 3.](images/c08f006.png)

[**Figure 8.6**](#c08.xhtml#R_c08-fig-0006) Log parsing tree‐using Drain with depth of 3.

 

#### 8.3.1.1 Log Parsing

Log parsing can convert each log message into a specific log template associated with key parameters. In a parsed log template, natural language text denotes the constant token as a log event. OpenLog adopts log parser, Drain (a fixed **d**epth t**r**ee b**a**sed onl**i**ne log parsi**n**g method) (He et al., [2017](#c08.xhtml#c08-bib-0009)), to conduct log parsing. Drain is an online log parser with a fixed depth of tree, which encodes specially designed rules for parsing as shown in [Figure 8.6](#c08.xhtml#c08-fig-0006). The process of log parsing based on the parse tree is as follows:

1.  Log segmentation. Through the predefined regular expression, a raw log information is extracted and divided into different components, including timestamp, level, component, etc.
2.  Search by length. The first‐layer nodes in the parse tree represent log groups whose log messages are of different log message lengths. For example, the length of the raw log “PacketResponder for block blk\_123” is 4, and it should be traversed to the internal node “Length: 4.”
3.  Search by preceding tokens. This step is based on the assumption that tokens in the beginning positions of a log message are more likely to be constants. For example, for log message “Receive from node 4,” Drain traverses from the first‐layer node “Length: 4” to the second‐layer node “Receive,” because the token in the first position of the log message is “Receive.”.
4.  Search by token similarity. Before this step, Drain has traversed to a leaf node, which contains a list of log groups. In this step, Drain selects the most suitable log group from the log group list by token similarity.
5.  Update the parse tree. If a new log finds a suitable log group, the corresponding log template will be updated. Drain scans the tokens in the same position of the log message and the log template. If the two tokens are the same, we do not modify the token in that token position. Otherwise, we update the token in that token position by “\*” in the log template. If the log does not find a suitable log group, a new log group is created according to the log. The log template of the log group is the log itself.

The online version of Drain is used to parse the raw log messages. That is, it processes log entries instead of log files, so the same log event may have a different parsed result at a different time, which can simulate the evolution and changes of the log to a certain extent due to the system upgrade or processing noise. We treat a parsed log event as a sentence ![upper S equals left-bracket w 1 comma w 2 comma period period period comma w Subscript upper N Baseline right-bracket](images/c08-i0018.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>S</mi><mo>=</mo><mfenced open="\[" close="\]" separators=""><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>w</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mrow><mi>w</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msub><mo>,</mo><mo>.</mo><mo>.</mo><mo>.</mo><mo>,</mo><msub><mrow><mi>w</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>N</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></mfenced></math> and a log template event as a document ![upper D equals left-bracket s 1 comma s 2 comma period period period comma s Subscript upper M Baseline right-bracket](images/c08-i0019.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>D</mi><mo>=</mo><mfenced open="\[" close="\]" separators=""><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>s</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mrow><mi>s</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msub><mo>,</mo><mo>.</mo><mo>.</mo><mo>.</mo><mo>,</mo><msub><mrow><mi>s</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>M</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></mfenced></math> in natural language, where ![w Subscript i Baseline comma i element-of left-bracket 1 comma upper N right-bracket](images/c08-i0020.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mrow><mi>w</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub><mo>,</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>&#8712;</mo><mfenced open="\[" close="\]" separators=""><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mi>N</mi></mrow></mfenced></math> is the ![i](images/c08-i0021.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></math>‐th token (word) in the sentence (log event), and ![s Subscript j Baseline comma j element-of left-bracket 1 comma upper M right-bracket](images/c08-i0022.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mrow><mi>s</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>j</mi></mrow></msub><mo>,</mo><mi>j</mi><mo>&#8712;</mo><mfenced open="\[" close="\]" separators=""><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mi>M</mi></mrow></mfenced></math> is the ![j](images/c08-i0023.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>j</mi></mrow></math>‐th sentence (log event) in the document (log event sequence). To facilitate the subsequent analysis, we filter out some tokens in log messages as follows: (1) remove noncharacter tokens such as numerical and nonnatural language tokens, (2) remove stop words such as “`a`” and “`the`”, (3) split the CamelCase variable (e.g. split “`isCommittable`” into “`is`” and “`Committable`”), and (4) change all tokens to lowercase.

 

#### 8.3.1.2 Log Enhancement

We set up three types of experiments to evaluate our method, using unknown class data, imbalanced data, and few‐shot data, respectively. In order to ensure the uniqueness of variable factors, we need balanced datasets in the experiments with the unknown class data and few‐shot data. Therefore, we enhance our datasets by Easy Data Augmentation (EDA) (Wei and Zou, [2019](#c08.xhtml#c08-bib-0033)). For a given log in the training dataset, EDA randomly chooses and performs one of the following operations:

*   Synonym replacement: Randomly choose ![n](images/c08-i0024.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow></math> nonstop words from the sentence and replace each of the words with one of its synonyms chosen at random.
*   Random insertion: Randomly select a nonstop word from the sentence, and randomly select one of its synonyms and insert it into a random position in the sentence. Do this ![n](images/c08-i0025.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow></math> times.
*   Random swap: Randomly choose two words in the log and swap their positions. Do this ![n](images/c08-i0026.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow></math> times.
*   Random deletion: Randomly remove each word in the sentence with a probability.

Through the above four operations, a log entry can be enhanced to multiple similar but different log entries. The category with few samples can be enhanced to balance with the category with more samples.

 

#### 8.3.1.3 Log Vectorization

In order to extract the semantic information of logs and achieve the purpose that log events with a similar meaning have a similar vector representation, we adopt the widely used method, *word embedding* to vectorize the log event to log vector representations that are pretrained via *GloVe*. Each token in the log event is converted into the corresponding ![d](images/c08-i0027.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>d</mi></mrow></math>‐dimensional semantic vector, where ![d equals 200](images/c08-i0028.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>d</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>200</mn></math> in our settings. For unknown words, we replace them with a unique token “UNK” and fix its embedding as a zero vector. After vectorization, a log event ![upper S](images/c08-i0029.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>S</mi></mrow></math> is transformed into an embedding matrix ![bold-italic s equals left-bracket bold-italic e 1 comma bold-italic e 2 comma period period period comma bold-italic e Subscript upper N Baseline right-bracket](images/c08-i0030.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mstyle mathvariant="bold-italic"><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">s</mi></mstyle><mo>=</mo><mfenced open="\[" close="\]" separators=""><mrow><msub><mrow><mstyle mathvariant="bold-italic"><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">e</mi></mstyle></mrow><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mrow><mstyle mathvariant="bold-italic"><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">e</mi></mstyle></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msub><mo>,</mo><mo>.</mo><mo>.</mo><mo>.</mo><mo>,</mo><msub><mrow><mstyle mathvariant="bold-italic"><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">e</mi></mstyle></mrow><mrow><mi>N</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></mfenced></math>, where ![bold-italic e Subscript i Baseline comma i element-of left-bracket 1 comma upper N right-bracket](images/c08-i0031.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mrow><mstyle mathvariant="bold-italic"><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">e</mi></mstyle></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub><mo>,</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>&#8712;</mo><mfenced open="\[" close="\]" separators=""><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mi>N</mi></mrow></mfenced></math> is the ![i](images/c08-i0032.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></math>‐th word vector in the list, and ![upper N](images/c08-i0033.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>N</mi></mrow></math> is the number of tokens in a log event sentence.

 

### 8.3.2 The Architecture of OpenLog

The overall architecture of OpenLog, depicted in [Figure 8.7](#c08.xhtml#c08-fig-0007), consists of three modules: *Encoder Module*, *Prototypical Module* and *Relation Module*. There is also an important knowledge base that can dynamically add and delete log data to achieve the purpose of class increments without retraining. We elaborate these components in the following sections.

![Figure 8.7 The architecture of OpenLog.](images/c08f007.png)

[**Figure 8.7**](#c08.xhtml#R_c08-fig-0007) The architecture of OpenLog.

 

#### 8.3.2.1 Encoder Module

After log preprocessing, we need to encode the log event sequence sample ![d Subscript i](images/c08-i0034.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mrow><mi>d</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub></math> into a lower‐dimensional numerical feature vector, whereby classifiers can be applied. In this way, similar samples are close with each other, while dissimilar samples can be more easily differentiated. The encoder module in OpenLog consists of a word sequence encoder based on Convolutional Neural Network (CNN) and a sentence encoder base on Bidirectional Long Short‐Term Memory (Bi‐LSTM) with self‐attention, as shown in [Figure 8.8](#c08.xhtml#c08-fig-0008)).

![Figure 8.8 The encoder module. (a) Word‐level encounter and (b) Sentence‐level encounter.](images/c08f008.png)

[**Figure 8.8**](#c08.xhtml#R_c08-fig-0008) The encoder module. (a) Word‐level encounter and (b) Sentence‐level encounter.

![Figure 8.9 The prototypical module.](images/c08f009.png)

[**Figure 8.9**](#c08.xhtml#R_c08-fig-0009) The prototypical module.

 

#### 8.3.2.2 Prototypical Module

We apply the *prototypical network* (Snell et al., [2017](#c08.xhtml#c08-bib-0025)) in this module as shown in [Figure 8.9](#c08.xhtml#c08-fig-0009), which is an embedding learning method of the few‐shot classification. It learns a metric space in which classification can be performed by computing the distances to the prototype representations of each class (Snell et al., [2017](#c08.xhtml#c08-bib-0025)). The classification part of prototypical networks is a nonparameter, so we can train a more general encoder model in a multiepisode training manner. Given an encoded feature representation ![bold-italic x](images/c08-i0073.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mstyle mathvariant="bold-italic"><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mi></mstyle></math> of a log template sequence, the size of ![bold-italic x](images/c08-i0074.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mstyle mathvariant="bold-italic"><mi mathvariant="bold-italic">x</mi></mstyle></math> is ![d Subscript s](images/c08-i0075.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mrow><mi>d</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>s</mi></mrow></msub></math>. This module will compute a prototype ![p Subscript k Baseline element-of upper R Superscript d Super Subscript s](images/c08-i0076.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mrow><mi>p</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow></msub><mo>&#8712;</mo><msup><mrow><mi>R</mi></mrow><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>d</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>s</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></msup></math> of each class of samples, where ![k](images/c08-i0077.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow></math> is the ![k](images/c08-i0078.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow></math>‐th class in the support set. Each prototype is the mean vector of the embedded representation belonging to its class. ![p Subscript k](images/c08-i0079.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mrow><mi>p</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow></msub></math> can be expressed as follows:

Prototypical networks then produce a probability distributions over distance based on a softmax function,

where ![upper P Subscript phi Baseline left-parenthesis y equals k vertical-bar x right-parenthesis](images/c08-i0086.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mrow><mi>P</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>&#981;</mi></mrow></msub><mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mrow><mi>y</mi><mo>=</mo><mi>k</mi><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><mi>x</mi></mrow></mfenced></math> is the probability that point ![x](images/c08-i0087.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>x</mi></mrow></math> belongs to the class ![k](images/c08-i0088.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow></math>.

 

#### 8.3.2.3 Relation Module

This module is learning to compare the encoded testing sample and the prototype pairs from labeled support set and unlabeled testing set by a deep learning network and outputs the relation score of them. [Figure 8.10](#c08.xhtml#c08-fig-0010) is the architecture of the relation module. Given a testing point ![x Subscript i](images/c08-i0089.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mrow><mi>x</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub></math> and a prototype ![p Subscript k](images/c08-i0090.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mrow><mi>p</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow></msub></math>, we apply a combination operation ![upper C left-parenthesis x Subscript i Baseline comma p Subscript k Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c08-i0091.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>C</mi><mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>x</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mrow><mi>p</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></mfenced></math> on them. In this work, we set ![upper C left-parenthesis dot comma dot right-parenthesis](images/c08-i0092.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>C</mi><mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mrow><mo>&#183;</mo><mo>,</mo><mo>&#183;</mo></mrow></mfenced></math> to an absolute subtraction operation. The result of ![upper C left-parenthesis x Subscript i Baseline comma p Subscript k Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c08-i0093.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>C</mi><mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>x</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mrow><mi>p</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></mfenced></math> is fed into the relation module, which produces a scalar in the range of 0‐1 representing the similarity between testing point ![x Subscript i](images/c08-i0094.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mrow><mi>x</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub></math> and the prototype ![p Subscript k](images/c08-i0095.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mrow><mi>p</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow></msub></math>, called the relation score. If there are ![upper C](images/c08-i0096.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>C</mi></mrow></math> classes of anomaly data in the knowledge base, we will get ![upper C](images/c08-i0097.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>C</mi></mrow></math> scores in this module, used to classify ![x Subscript i](images/c08-i0098.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mrow><mi>x</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub></math> as a known class or detect it as an unknown class. The relation network consists of a Bi‐LSTM layer and two fully connected networks as follows:

(8.13)![s Subscript x Sub Subscript i Subscript comma p Sub Subscript k Subscript Baseline equals sigma left-parenthesis upper W 2 dot upper R e l u left-parenthesis upper W 1 dot upper B i upper L upper S upper T upper M left-parenthesis upper C left-parenthesis x Subscript i Baseline comma p Subscript k Baseline right-parenthesis right-parenthesis plus b 1 right-parenthesis plus b 2 right-parenthesis](images/c08-math-0013.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll"><msub><mrow><mi>s</mi></mrow><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>x</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mrow><mi>p</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><mi>&#963;</mi><mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>W</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msub><mo>&#183;</mo><mi>R</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>l</mi><mi>u</mi><mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>W</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow></msub><mo>&#183;</mo><mi>B</mi><mi>i</mi><mi>L</mi><mi>S</mi><mi>T</mi><mi>M</mi><mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mrow><mi>C</mi><mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>x</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mrow><mi>p</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></mfenced></mrow></mfenced><mo>+</mo><msub><mrow><mi>b</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow></msub></mrow></mfenced><mo>+</mo><msub><mrow><mi>b</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msub></mrow></mfenced></math> ![Figure 8.10 The relation module.](images/c08f010.png)

[**Figure 8.10**](#c08.xhtml#R_c08-fig-0010) The relation module.

where ![s Subscript x Sub Subscript i Subscript comma p Sub Subscript k Subscript](images/c08-i0099.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mrow><mi>s</mi></mrow><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>x</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mrow><mi>p</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></msub></math> is the relation score between the testing point ![x Subscript i](images/c08-i0100.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mrow><mi>x</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub></math> and the prototype ![p Subscript k](images/c08-i0101.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mrow><mi>p</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow></msub></math>. For the sake of the purpose of illustration and simplicity, we denote the operations in the relation module as the following equation:

(8.14)![s Subscript x Sub Subscript i Subscript comma p Sub Subscript k Subscript Baseline equals f Subscript phi Superscript r Baseline left-parenthesis x Subscript i Baseline comma p Subscript k Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c08-math-0014.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll"><msub><mrow><mi>s</mi></mrow><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>x</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mrow><mi>p</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><msubsup><mrow><mi>f</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>&#966;</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>r</mi></mrow></msubsup><mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>x</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mrow><mi>p</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></mfenced></math> 

where ![phi](images/c08-i0102.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>&#966;</mi></mrow></math> is the learnable parameter of the relation module. We set the relation score threshold to 0.5 for the sigmoid function, which is a common threshold for a relatedness score (Xu et al., [2019](#c08.xhtml#c08-bib-0035), Pushp and Srivastava, [2017](#c08.xhtml#c08-bib-0023)), to indicate the probability of the testing point ![x Subscript i](images/c08-i0103.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mrow><mi>x</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub></math> and the prototype ![p Subscript k](images/c08-i0104.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mrow><mi>p</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow></msub></math> belonging to the same class ![k](images/c08-i0105.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow></math>. If the score of the sample pairs is less than or equal to 0.5, they are not in the same class. Otherwise, they belong to the same class.

(8.15)![y equals Start 2 By 2 Matrix 1st Row 1st Column 0 comma i f f Subscript phi Superscript r Baseline left-parenthesis x Subscript i Baseline comma p Subscript k Baseline right-parenthesis less-than-or-equal-to 0.5 comma 2nd Column Blank 2nd Row 1st Column 1 comma i f f Subscript phi Superscript r Baseline left-parenthesis x Subscript i Baseline comma p Subscript k Baseline right-parenthesis greater-than 0.5 comma EndMatrix](images/c08-math-0015.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll"><mi>y</mi><mo>=</mo><mfenced open="{" close="." separators=""><mrow><mtable><mtr><mtd class="array"><mn>0</mn><mo>,</mo><mi>i</mi><mi>f</mi><msubsup><mrow><mi>f</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>&#966;</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>r</mi></mrow></msubsup><mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>x</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mrow><mi>p</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></mfenced><mo>&#8804;</mo><mn>0.5</mn><mo>,</mo></mtd><mtd class="array"/></mtr><mtr><mtd class="array"><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mi>i</mi><mi>f</mi><msubsup><mrow><mi>f</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>&#966;</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>r</mi></mrow></msubsup><mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>x</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mrow><mi>p</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></mfenced><mo>&#62;</mo><mn>0.5</mn><mo>,</mo></mtd></mtr></mtable></mrow></mfenced></math>  

### 8.3.3 Training Procedure

Different from the traditional deep learning methods, OpenLog has a special two‐stage training procedure. The first stage is the representation learning phase like a pretraining. The model structure in this phase consists of the encoder module and the prototypical module. We apply the episode‐based meta‐training (Geng et al., [2019](#c08.xhtml#c08-bib-0005)). The representation learning phase is an episode‐based meta‐training, OpenLog aims to train the encoder module through multiple meta‐tasks. We select the part of the preprocessed auxiliary datasets as the training dataset ![upper T](images/c08-i0106.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>T</mi></mrow></math> and the validation dataset. In every episode, OpenLog randomly chooses classes and samples in the training dataset ![upper T](images/c08-i0107.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>T</mi></mrow></math> to train the model. For example, it randomly selects ![upper C](images/c08-i0108.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>C</mi></mrow></math> classes from the training dataset, chooses ![upper K](images/c08-i0109.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>K</mi></mrow></math> samples in each class as the sample dataset ![upper S](images/c08-i0110.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>S</mi></mrow></math>, and chooses ![upper Q](images/c08-i0111.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>Q</mi></mrow></math> samples in each class as the query dataset ![upper Q](images/c08-i0112.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>Q</mi></mrow></math>. It is called a ![upper C](images/c08-i0113.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>C</mi></mrow></math>‐Way‐![upper K](images/c08-i0114.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>K</mi></mrow></math>‐Shot‐![upper Q](images/c08-i0115.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>Q</mi></mrow></math>‐Queries meta‐training setting (Sung et al., [2018](#c08.xhtml#c08-bib-0028)), where the value of ![upper C](images/c08-i0116.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>C</mi></mrow></math>, ![upper K](images/c08-i0117.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>K</mi></mrow></math>, and ![upper Q](images/c08-i0118.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>Q</mi></mrow></math> are set manually as parameters. The classification results of the sample pairs in the prototypical module are used to tune the parameters of the encoder module continuously via the backpropagation and parameter optimization, which is the same as the normal deep‐learning training procedure. The validation set is used to adjust the hyperparameters and make a preliminary assessment of the model's performance. The second stage is the relation‐learning phase. The model structure in this phase consists of the encoder module, prototypical module, and the relation module. We apply a similar but different training strategy in this phase. We aggregate the rest of the auxiliary datasets and the small part of the target dataset as set ![upper M](images/c08-i0119.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>M</mi></mrow></math>. OpenLog randomly chooses a subset of classes from ![upper M](images/c08-i0120.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>M</mi></mrow></math> like the former phase. The difference with the former phase is that, in every episode, we choose some pairs of samples with positive labels and negative labels instead of multiple labels, to indicate that they belong to the same class or different classes. Besides, if there are more samples in each class, we can also use the method mentioned in Xu et al. ([2019](#c08.xhtml#c08-bib-0035)), to select the top‐![k](images/c08-i0121.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow></math> different‐class nearest samples from ![upper M](images/c08-i0122.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>M</mi></mrow></math> to improve the performance and reduce the complexity of calculation. One of the sample pairs is a query sample, and the other is a prototype calculated from multiple support samples (randomly selected or top‐![k](images/c08-i0123.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow></math> nearest neighbors). We set the ratio of the sample pairs belonging to the same class and the different classes as 2:1 to avoid overfitting. This phase also needs the help of the validation set to evaluate the generalization of the model.

 

### 8.3.4 Objective Function

The representation‐learning phase is a multiclassification task. We therefore use the *cross‐entropy loss function* to train the model as follows:

(8.16)![upper J 1 left-parenthesis phi right-parenthesis equals minus l o g upper P Subscript phi Baseline left-parenthesis y equals k vertical-bar x right-parenthesis](images/c08-math-0016.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll"><msub><mrow><mi>J</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow></msub><mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mrow><mi>&#966;</mi></mrow></mfenced><mo>=</mo><mo>&#8722;</mo><mi>l</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>g</mi><msub><mrow><mi>P</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>&#981;</mi></mrow></msub><mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mrow><mi>y</mi><mo>=</mo><mi>k</mi><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><mi>x</mi></mrow></mfenced></math> 

The relation‐learning phase is a binary classification task. We thus use *the* *mean square error* (MSE) loss function to train the model as follows:

(8.17)![upper J 2 left-parenthesis phi right-parenthesis equals normal upper Sigma Subscript i equals 1 Superscript m Baseline normal upper Sigma Subscript j equals 1 Superscript n Baseline left-parenthesis s Subscript i comma j Baseline minus 1 left-parenthesis y Subscript i Baseline equals equals y Subscript j Baseline right-parenthesis right-parenthesis squared](images/c08-math-0017.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll"><msub><mrow><mi>J</mi></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msub><mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mrow><mi>&#966;</mi></mrow></mfenced><mo>=</mo><msubsup><mrow><mstyle mathvariant="normal"><mi mathvariant="normal">&#931;</mi></mstyle></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mrow><mi>m</mi></mrow></msubsup><msubsup><mrow><mstyle mathvariant="normal"><mi mathvariant="normal">&#931;</mi></mstyle></mrow><mrow><mi>j</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow></msubsup><msup><mrow><mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>s</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>j</mi></mrow></msub><mo>&#8722;</mo><mn>1</mn><mfenced open="(" close=")" separators=""><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>y</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><mo>=</mo><msub><mrow><mi>y</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>j</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></mfenced></mrow></mfenced></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn></mrow></msup></math>  

## 8.4 Experimental Results and Analysis

We evaluate OpenLog by a variety of experiments, including the anomaly classification experiment using the unknown class data, the anomaly classification experiment using the imbalanced data, and the anomaly classification experiment using the few‐shot data. We use two existing important methods as baselines: a deep‐learning method LogRobust based on Bi‐LSTM and self‐attention (Zhang et al., [2019](#c08.xhtml#c08-bib-0037)), and a traditional machine learning method based on SVM, called LogClass (Meng et al., [2018](#c08.xhtml#c08-bib-0017)). In what follows, we introduce the datasets we used in the experiments and the details of the experiment settings and analyze the results of the experiments.

 

### 8.4.1 Experimental Design

 

#### 8.4.1.1 Baseline

![Figure 8.11 The baselines. (a) LogRobust and (b) LogClass.](images/c08f011.png)

[**Figure 8.11**](#c08.xhtml#R_c08-fig-0011) The baselines. (a) LogRobust and (b) LogClass.

 

#### 8.4.1.2 Evaluation Metrics

We use accuracy, recall, precision, and macro‐F1 score as the evaluation metrics to measure the performance of OpenLog in terms of classifying the known anomalies as multiclass classification. In the binary classification, the evaluation metrics depend on the true positives (TP) indicating that the number of positive samples that are predicted correctly, true negatives (TN) denoting the number of negative samples that are predicted correctly, false positives (FP) showing the number of positive samples of incorrect predictions, and false negatives (FN) representing the number of negative samples of incorrect predictions:

(8.18)![upper A c c u r a c y equals StartFraction upper T upper P plus upper T upper N Over upper T upper P plus upper T upper N plus upper F upper P plus upper F upper N EndFraction](images/c08-math-0018.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll"><mi>A</mi><mi>c</mi><mi>c</mi><mi>u</mi><mi>r</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>c</mi><mi>y</mi><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><mi>T</mi><mi>P</mi><mo>+</mo><mi>T</mi><mi>N</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>T</mi><mi>P</mi><mo>+</mo><mi>T</mi><mi>N</mi><mo>+</mo><mi>F</mi><mi>P</mi><mo>+</mo><mi>F</mi><mi>N</mi></mrow></mfrac></math> (8.19)![upper P r e c i s i o n equals StartFraction upper T upper P Over upper T upper P plus upper F upper P EndFraction](images/c08-math-0019.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll"><mi>P</mi><mi>r</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>c</mi><mi>i</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>i</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>n</mi><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><mi>T</mi><mi>P</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>T</mi><mi>P</mi><mo>+</mo><mi>F</mi><mi>P</mi></mrow></mfrac></math> (8.20)![upper R e c a l l equals StartFraction upper T upper P Over upper T upper P plus upper F upper N EndFraction](images/c08-math-0020.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll"><mi>R</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>c</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>l</mi><mi>l</mi><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><mi>T</mi><mi>P</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>T</mi><mi>P</mi><mo>+</mo><mi>F</mi><mi>N</mi></mrow></mfrac></math> (8.21)![upper F Baseline 1 equals StartFraction 2 times upper P r e c i s i o n times upper R e c a l l Over upper P r e c i s i o n plus upper R e c a l l EndFraction](images/c08-math-0021.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll"><mi>F</mi><mn>1</mn><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>&#215;</mo><mi>P</mi><mi>r</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>c</mi><mi>i</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>i</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>n</mi><mo>&#215;</mo><mi>R</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>c</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>l</mi><mi>l</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>P</mi><mi>r</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>c</mi><mi>i</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>i</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>n</mi><mo>+</mo><mi>R</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>c</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>l</mi><mi>l</mi></mrow></mfrac></math> 

In multiclassification, we split the task into multiple binary classifications and perform the statistics of the corresponding TP, FP, TN, and FN in every class. Then, we calculate the accuracy and recall F1 score according to the above formulas. The average of the F1 scores from all classes is called macro‐F1 shown as follows:

(8.22)![m a c r o minus upper F Baseline 1 equals StartFraction 1 Over n EndFraction sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript upper N Endscripts upper F 1 Subscript i](images/c08-math-0022.png)<math display="block" overflow="scroll"><mi>m</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>c</mi><mi>r</mi><mi>o</mi><mo>&#8722;</mo><mi>F</mi><mn>1</mn><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow></mfrac><munderover><mrow><mo stretchy="false" movablelimits="false">&#8721;</mo></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mrow><mi>N</mi></mrow></munderover><mi>F</mi><msub><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub></math> 

where ![upper F 1 Subscript i](images/c08-i0124.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>F</mi><msub><mrow><mn>1</mn></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub></math> is the F1 score of the ![i](images/c08-i0125.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></math>‐th class.

 

### 8.4.2 Datasets

We use four public real‐world *High‐Performance Computer* (HPC) log datasets (Chen et al., [2013](#c08.xhtml#c08-bib-0002)) in our experiments, namely *Blue Gene/L*, *Thunderbird*, *Liberty* and *Spirit*. The log examples of four datasets are shown in [Figure 8.12](#c08.xhtml#c08-fig-0012).

![Figure 8.12 The examples of BGL, Thunderbird, Liberty, and Spirit logs.](images/c08f012.png)

[**Figure 8.12**](#c08.xhtml#R_c08-fig-0012) The examples of BGL, Thunderbird, Liberty, and Spirit logs.

 

#### 8.4.2.1 Data Processing

Since the four datasets are all HPC log data, they share some common characteristics, so they can be used as the auxiliary datasets to each other. We use the following strategy to organize the experimental data. The BGL dataset contains rich anomaly classes (41 categories), where it is suitable for organizing them into different meta‐tasks in meta‐training; therefore, we use BGL as the basic auxiliary datasets. Then, we use the Thunderbird, Liberty, and Spirit datasets as target datasets for comparative experiments. For example, when the Thunderbird dataset is used as the target dataset, we merge the BGL, Liberty, and Spirit dataset into a large training dataset with 61 anomaly classes, and randomly choose ![60 percent-sign](images/c08-i0126.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mn>60</mn><mi>%</mi></math> of the training dataset as the encoder training set, and the rest of the training data and the different proportions of the target datasets (Thunderbird) as the relation training set. For the different experiments, we have different data processing and construct different training/testing datasets. We also filter the duplicate logs because the datasets contain many redundant log templates. [Table 8.1](#c08.xhtml#c08-tbl-0001) summarizes the information of the filtered datasets.

[**Table 8.1**](#c08.xhtml#R_c08-tbl-0001)  The details of datasets.

| Datasets || Total || Anomaly class size || Anomalies || Filtered data |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| BGL || 4 713 493 || 41 || 348 460 || 93 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Thunderbird || 211 212 192 || 10 || 3 248 239 || 360 |
| Liberty || 265 569 231 || 18 || 2452 || 746 |
| Spirit || 272 298 969 || 8 || 172 816 564 || 250 |

In the anomaly classification experiment using the unknown class data, we use the EDA method described in [Section 8.3.1.2](#c08.xhtml#c08-sec-0012) to enhance the dataset, so that each class in the dataset contains 2000 samples. Then, we use the ![25 percent-sign](images/c08-i0127.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mn>25</mn><mi>%</mi></math>, ![50 percent-sign](images/c08-i0128.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mn>50</mn><mi>%</mi></math>, ![75 percent-sign](images/c08-i0129.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mn>75</mn><mi>%</mi></math>, and ![100 percent-sign](images/c08-i0130.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mn>100</mn><mi>%</mi></math> of class size in the target dataset as the training classes, respectively, and ![80 percent-sign](images/c08-i0131.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mn>80</mn><mi>%</mi></math> samples of the training class dataset as the training dataset, and ![20 percent-sign](images/c08-i0132.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mn>20</mn><mi>%</mi></math> of the samples in the target dataset as the testing data, which contains model‐unseen anomaly samples. In this experimental setup, we evaluate the performance of different methods in the presence of the unknown class data. In the anomaly classification experiment using the imbalanced data, we use the imbalanced data as shown in [Figure 8.13](#c08.xhtml#c08-fig-0013) and use the ![100 percent-sign](images/c08-i0133.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mn>100</mn><mi>%</mi></math> of class size in the target dataset. We can find that some classes of data account for more than 30% of all data, since some classes of data account for less than 0.1% of all data. In some classes, for example, for the “R\_EXT\_CCISS” of Spirit dataset in [Figure 8.13](#c08.xhtml#c08-fig-0013)(c), the number of samples is even less than 10. In this experimental setup, we evaluate the performance of different methods in the presence of the imbalanced data. In the anomaly classification experiment using the few‐shot data, we conduct an experiment dataset based on the setting of few‐shot learning. There is only a very small amount of training data (5, 10, 15, 20 samples) in each class. In this experimental setup, we evaluate the performance of different methods in the presence of the few‐shot data.

![Figure 8.13 The samples proportion of raw imbalanced log in Thunderbird, Liberty, and Spirit datasets. (a) BGL, (b) Liberty, and (c) Spirit.](images/c08f013.png)

[**Figure 8.13**](#c08.xhtml#R_c08-fig-0013) The samples proportion of raw imbalanced log in Thunderbird, Liberty, and Spirit datasets. (a) BGL, (b) Liberty, and (c) Spirit.

![Figure 8.14 A comparison of the evaluation scores against the baselines on Thunderbird, Liberty, and Spirit in anomaly classification on the unknown class data.](images/c08f014.png)

[**Figure 8.14**](#c08.xhtml#R_c08-fig-0014) A comparison of the evaluation scores against the baselines on Thunderbird, Liberty, and Spirit in anomaly classification on the unknown class data.

 

### 8.4.3 Experiments on the Unknown Class Data

In order to break the closed‐world assumption of data categories and evaluate the performance of anomaly classification methods in “open” environment, new anomaly classes will continue to be present. We split the training data and the testing data and set the ![5 percent-sign](images/c08-i0134.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mn>5</mn><mi>%</mi></math>, ![25 percent-sign](images/c08-i0135.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mn>25</mn><mi>%</mi></math>, ![75 percent-sign](images/c08-i0136.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mn>75</mn><mi>%</mi></math>, and ![100 percent-sign](images/c08-i0137.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mn>100</mn><mi>%</mi></math> of all classes in the target dataset as unseen classes. The experimental results are shown in [Figure 8.14](#c08.xhtml#c08-fig-0014). The feature extraction of LogClass is based on the bag‐of‐words and TF‐IDF weighting. LogClass needs to build a global index‐based vocabulary dictionary and statistics word frequency of every vocabulary in the dictionary. However, the new class is likely to bring new log events containing new vocabulary. Many key words are likely to be placed as the same unknown index. As the experimental results show, many important features that are useful for anomaly classification are lost through the feature extraction method in LogClass. When the ratio of class size in the training data is under ![100 percent-sign](images/c08-i0138.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mn>100</mn><mi>%</mi></math>, LogClass has a lower‐performance score on the three datasets. The feature extraction of LogRobust is based on word embedding, and therefore, it is not easy to be affected by the changes of logs. But neither logRobust nor logClass has the ability to detect unknown class anomalous samples, and they will classify the samples of unknown class as the wrong seen class. OpenLog focuses on the comparison between log representation learned from the auxiliary datasets, instead of the parameter model mapping based on a large amount of data. Therefore, OpenLog achieves better scores and is almost unaffected by unseen classes.

 

### 8.4.4 Experiments on the Imbalanced Data

In order to break the closed‐world assumption of data balance and evaluate the performance of anomaly classification methods in open environment where samples of each class is extremely imbalanced. We use the raw data ratio of the three datasets. The experimental results are shown in [Figure 8.15](#c08.xhtml#c08-fig-0015). The deep‐learning based LogRobust is very sensitive to the imbalanced data. The experimental results show that LogRobust gets the recall, precision, and macro‐F1 scores that are much lower than accuracy. This is because LogRobust has good classification ability for some classes containing sufficient training data but is incompetent for other classes containing few samples. The SVM‐based logClass is not easily affected by the imbalance of training data because SVM's decision plane is only related to a few decisive support vectors. Due to its special data sampling strategy, OpenLog is completely unaffected by data imbalance and can maintain stable performance.

![Figure 8.15 A comparison of the evaluation scores against the baselines on Thunderbird, Liberty, and Spirit in anomaly classification on the imbalanced data.](images/c08f015.png)

[**Figure 8.15**](#c08.xhtml#R_c08-fig-0015) A comparison of the evaluation scores against the baselines on Thunderbird, Liberty, and Spirit in anomaly classification on the imbalanced data.

 

### 8.4.5 Experiments on the Few‐shot Data

In order to break the closed‐world assumption of data scale and evaluate the performance of anomaly classification methods in “open” environment, where the target system contains a few training samples. There are only 5, 10, 15, 20 samples in each class in our experimental setup. The experimental results are shown in [Figure 8.16](#c08.xhtml#c08-fig-0016). The SVM‐based LogClass is a machine‐learning method, and its decision plane and prediction results are only related to a small number of support vectors. That is suitable for training on a small amount of data. But the few training data in each class cannot bring enough information for SVM to achieve higher performance. It can be improved with the enrichment of training data from 5 samples to 20 samples in each class. However, a deep learning‐based method, LogRobust, with a large number of parameters need to be optimized depending on the training on a large amount of labeled data. LogRobust can hardly work on the few‐shot data, where it can only get very low‐performance scores. Based on the sufficient auxiliary datasets, OpenLog can keep stable performance on few‐shot data. OpenLog is fully trained on a large number of different but similar meta‐tasks sampled from the auxiliary datasets and is not limited by the lack of training data in the target dataset. Even when each class contains only five samples, OpenLog can also achieve the average accuracy and macro‐F1 score higher than 90%.

![Figure 8.16 A comparison of the evaluation scores against the baselines on Thunderbird, Liberty, and Spirit in anomaly classification on the few‐shot data.](images/c08f016.png)

[**Figure 8.16**](#c08.xhtml#R_c08-fig-0016) A comparison of the evaluation scores against the baselines on Thunderbird, Liberty, and Spirit in anomaly classification on the few‐shot data.

 

## 8.5 Discussion

In this chapter, we proposed an anomaly classification method based on meta‐learning and few‐shot learning and proved the advantages and effectiveness of the proposed method. However, there are some aspects that need to be improved in the future.

1.  The idea of meta‐learning and few‐shot learning can provide exploration and development direction for some of the bottlenecks in the existing log analysis methods. However, due to the characteristic of meta‐learning and few‐shot learning, most of methods belong to the supervised learning, which can solve the problem of anomaly classification, fault diagnosis, etc., but it is more difficult for other unsupervised log analysis tasks, for example, correlation analysis and failure prediction.
2.  The prototypical module of the proposed method is based on metric learning, that is, by comparing the relationship between the samples, to achieve the purpose of classification and detection of unknown class samples. This strategy is convenient, effective, and flexible, but the cost of time and calculation will increase with the increase in data size.
3.  The experiments in this chapter are based on limited public datasets, limited by the format and complexity of the datasets. If more experiments are carried out on real industrial datasets, the algorithm can be further extended and improved.

The components of the proposed OpenLog architecture can be replaced with any neural network structures. For example, the encoder can be selected according to the organization of the log. We can use a separate CNN or recurrent neural network (RNN), or other complex mature models in NLP, such as TextCNN (Kim, [2014](#c08.xhtml#c08-bib-0012)) and Transformer (Vaswani et al., [2017b](#c08.xhtml#c08-bib-0031)).

 

## 8.6 Conclusion

Large‐scale systems are generating a large amount of log data every day. Intelligent log analysis methods based on machine learning are constantly being proposed to ensure the stability and reliability of large‐scale systems. In this chapter, we addressed three problems and restrictions encountered by most of the existing methods: (1) The problem of the feature extraction; (2) The problem of the few‐shot problem; (3) The problem of the closed‐world assumption. In this chapter, we briefly introduced the feature extraction in log analysis and the few‐shot problem by examples. Then, we proposed a general anomaly classification method based on meta‐learning and few‐shot learning to address these issues. For the problem of the feature extraction, this proposed method used two layers of deep learning semantic coding in the encoder module to simplify the complex feature engineering and adapt to the changing log events. For the few‐shot problem, the proposed method used meta‐learning strategy to train the models on sufficient auxiliary datasets to enhance the model performance and reduce its dependence on the labeled samples of the target system. For the problem of the closed‐world assumption, the method transformed the multiclassification task of the fixed model into the binary‐classification task of learning the depth relationship between samples and can classify new anomalies incrementally without retraining. Extensive experimental results showed that OpenLog achieves better results than the existing important methods in terms of the classification with the unknown anomaly class data, imbalanced data, and few‐shot data.
